# PREFACE.

The interest of the following astronomical—or rather, cosmographical <note>original: "cosmographical"; a branch of science that deals with the general features of the universe, including both the earth and the heavens</note>—tract for students of Irish language and literature lies mainly in the fact that it belongs to a category of our literature which has, up to the present, been little investigated by those whose work is directed toward the publication of our manuscript literature. The investigation and publication of such documents is of special importance for determining the state of native education and culture and its relationship, at different periods, to foreign civilizations.

<margin>
jo
30
</margin>
The translations of medieval Latin texts into Irish, which were probably used as textbooks in the schools of the 14th and 15th centuries, would supply material for specialized study. A comparison of this current text with some of the numerous medical tracts—almost all of which still remain unedited—would undoubtedly provide much interesting material regarding the capacity of our language to treat purely scientific and technical subjects.

This work has not been entirely neglected until now. The late Reverend Maxwell Close arranged for the text to be translated into English for him and dealt exhaustively with the subject matter of the tract from a scientific perspective in a paper read before a meeting of the Royal Irish Academy in June 1901. This paper was published in the *Proceedings* of that organization, Volume 6, pages 457–464. Mr. Close's manuscript notes for the paper, along with a typewritten copy of the translation mentioned above, are now kept in the Academy Library.

Another article on the subject, containing a chapter-by-chapter summary of the contents, was published under—

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic history of the Irish Astronomical Tract, mentioning previous publications and identifying the main source as a Latin translation of an Arabic work by the 8th-century astronomer Messahalah.</meta>

the title of "An Irish Astronomical Tract," by J. E. Gore, Member of the Royal Irish Academy <note>original: "M.R.I.A."</note>, in *Knowledge*, February 1909. This again deals only with the scientific aspect of the tract, apparently gathered <note>original: "gleaned"</note> from the above-mentioned translation, and contains scarcely anything not already commented on by Mr. Close.

Chapters 8, 39, and a portion of chapter 9, with another small fragment of the text, were published with the same English translation in *Celtia*, Volume 11, pages 54–56, 90–92, and 101–103. The text that follows here has been arranged without consulting this small published fragment, and my translation has been made quite independently of the translation from which Mr. Close worked.

### SOURCES OF THE WORK.

We are indebted to the research of Mr. Close for our knowledge of one of the Latin texts that is the source of the greater part of this work.

According to Mr. Close, two-thirds of the tract are part paraphrase and part translation of a Latin version of an Arabic treatise by Messahalah (or Masha'allah), a Jewish astronomer of Alexandria who was active <note>original: "flourished"</note> shortly before 800 AD. This work was translated into Latin by Gerard of Sabionetta (near Cremona) in the 13th century. Edited by J. Stabius, it was printed at Nuremberg in 1504 under the title *On the Science of the Motion of the World* <note>original: "De Scientia Motus Orbis"</note>. A transcript of this, obtained by Mr. Close from a copy in the British Museum, is in the Academy Library. My translation of the Irish text had already been made when this was found, but it was not too late to make use of the help it provided in the rendering of certain passages evidently misunderstood by the Irish translator. This transcript is now numbered 3. B. 32. Gerard’s work was again edited by Joachim Heller, under the title *On the Elements and Celestial Orbs* <note>original: "De Elementis et Orbibus Coelestibus"</note>...

<summary>This page details the scholarly history of the tract, identifying its primary source as a 13th-century Latin translation of an Arabic astronomical work by Messahalah.</summary>
<keywords>Messahalah, Masha'allah, Gerard of Sabionetta, De Scientia Motus Orbis, J. E. Gore, Maxwell Close, Royal Irish Academy, history of astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Latin sources for the Irish Astronomical Tract, specifically comparing different printed editions and manuscripts to determine which version the Irish translator likely used.</meta>

-ntibus <note>This likely completes the Latin word "evidentibus," meaning "evident" or "clear," potentially from a title or passage mentioned on the previous page.</note>, and reprinted at Nuremberg in 1549. A comparison of the opening words of the chapters in these two editions with the Latin headings of the Irish text led Mr. Close to the conclusion that the Irish translator worked from a text different from either of the above. In Mr. Close’s manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> notes, we read: — “There is a manuscript of the Latin version of this work by Messahalah <note>original: "Messahalah," also known as Masha'allah, a prominent 8th-century Jewish-Arab astronomer.</note> in the Bodleian Library (MS Ashmole 393). It matches closely with the version edited by Stabius and Heller.” However, as Mr. Close has not attempted to define the relationship between this Bodleian manuscript and the Irish text, and as I have not had an opportunity to examine this Latin manuscript, the question of the origin of the different versions cannot be pursued further at this time.

The Irish text cannot be described as a literal translation of the edition by Stabius <note>Johannes Stabius (d. 1522), an Austrian cartographer and scholar.</note>. It is instead an adaptation. In some parts, the rendering is indeed literal, but there is hardly a chapter where there is not either more or less material than in the corresponding Latin version. These additions, if they can be called that, are not merely random insertions <note>original: "interpolations"</note>; they do not harm the sense of the text, nor do they differ in language or style from the passages for which we have a matching Latin original. Similarly, the omissions do not leave gaps in the arguments. It is quite probable that Stabius’s edition is not the actual original source for the Irish rendering, and the Irish translator may have had some other edition of Gerard of Sabionetta’s <note>A 13th-century translator known for his work on Arabic scientific texts.</note> work before him. The portion of the text not found in the Latin edition by Stabius consists of the introductory remarks (leading down to the table of contents) and chapters 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 14, 15, 17, 35, 36, and 37 (numbered 34, 35, and 36 according to the numbering of the English translation from which Mr. Close worked). Nothing is known of the original source of these chapters. They are evidently drawn from another Latin text or texts, and some of them appear to be of a much later date than Messahalah’s original work.

<summary>This page explores the textual origins of the Irish Astronomical Tract, noting that while it is based on Messahalah’s work, the Irish version is an adaptation that includes unique chapters not found in the standard Latin editions of the time.</summary>
<keywords>Messahalah, Masha'allah, Gerard of Sabionetta, Johannes Stabius, Bodleian Library, Ashmole 393, Nuremberg, Irish Astronomical Tract, Latin translation, manuscript analysis</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Irish Astronomical Tract, discussing the likely sources of the text and identifying specific chapters that appear to be later additions or taken from non-astronomical works.</meta>

work; for instance, in Chapter 7, the mention of spectacles—which did not come into use until the early 14th century—suggests a later addition. However, this may simply be an insertion <note>original: "interpolation"</note> by the Irish writer. Mr. Close <note>Maxwell Henry Close (1822–1903), an Irish clergyman and geologist who studied this manuscript.</note> remarks that in Chapter 36 (or 35), the habitable regions of the earth are described as extending further north than they would have been in Messahalah's <note>Masha'allah ibn Athari (c. 740–815 AD), a prominent Persian Jewish astronomer and astrologer.</note> time. He also points out several statements in these chapters that contradict Messahalah's own teachings. It is noteworthy that the interesting chapters on geology (8), mineral springs (9), volcanoes (10), tides (11), the Nile (12), and the seven habitable regions (36) are neither written by Messahalah nor strictly astronomical in nature. Thus, it seems the source from which these chapters were taken was cosmographical <note>A term referring to the study of the general features of the universe, including geography, physics, and astronomy.</note> rather than purely astronomical.

Messahalah's astronomy is largely based on that of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Roman mathematician and astronomer whose geocentric model dominated science for centuries.</note>. However, I do not intend to discuss that subject here, nor how the Irish translator handled it, as Mr. Close deals with this in great detail in his article, to which I again refer my readers.

There is, however, just one error in that same article caused by the complete omission of two words in the text. These words are initially unintelligible due to a mistake made by the scribe. Mr. Close was greatly surprised that in Chapter 35 (or 34), the ratio of a circle's circumference to its diameter is given as 3 to 1. This is surprising because Archimedes <note>The ancient Greek mathematician (c. 287–212 BC) who calculated an early, accurate value for Pi.</note> had shown long before Messahalah's time that the ratio is between 3 $^1/_7$ and 3 $^{10}/_{71}$. Furthermore, Al-Farghani <note>original: "Alfergani," a 9th-century Muslim astronomer.</note>, a contemporary of Messahalah, established it as equal to 3 $^1/_7$. The text here contains a curious error that can be easily corrected. It reads, "three and a [corrupted word]" <note>original Irish: "tri mile ⁊ ḟeth."</note>. It is evident that the scribe intended to write the letter *r* instead of *f*. If we read it as "three and a seventh" <note>original Irish: "tri mile ⁊ seacht[m]adh," where "seachtmadh" is the Irish word for "seventh."</note>, we arrive at the correct fraction.

<summary>The author discusses evidence that certain chapters of the Irish Astronomical Tract were later additions not original to Messahalah, and corrects a scribal error regarding the mathematical value of Pi.</summary>
<keywords>Messahalah, Masha'allah, Maxwell Henry Close, Ptolemy, Archimedes, Al-Farghani, Irish Astronomical Tract, Pi, geology, cosmography, medieval science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Irish Astronomical Tract's sources, moving from a discussion of its content and contradictions with Messahalah's original work to a detailed physical description of the specific manuscripts used for this edition.</meta>

### *The Manuscripts*

The text is contained in three manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note>:
1. Stowe, B. II. 1. Royal Irish Academy <note>original: "R.I.A."</note>
2. Z. 2. 2. 1. (until recently numbered V. 3. 1. 38), in Marsh’s Library, Dublin.
3. 23. F. 13. Royal Irish Academy.

1. A vellum <note>A high-quality writing surface made from calfskin.</note> manuscript, in very good condition, containing 12 leaves written in a small, tidy hand in double columns. Bound with it are 5 leaves of a medical treatise <note>original: "tract"</note> which is apparently a translation from Latin. The last page is completely unreadable <note>original: "illegible"</note>. The text is illustrated by skillfully drawn diagrams; however, a few of these are incorrect and do not match the text.

The first page is occupied by an astronomical rotula <note>original: "rotula"; a rotating paper or parchment instrument with a movable pointer, used for astronomical calculations.</note> with a movable index, containing the names of the Signs of the Zodiac and the planets in Latin; it also includes the names of the months and numerical figures. At the top of the page is a Lunar Calendar and a list of weights and measures which are very difficult to decipher due to stains on the manuscript. At the bottom of the rotula is a note in Irish explaining the connection between the signs of the Zodiac and human anatomy <note>This refers to the "Zodiac Man," a medieval concept linking astrological signs to specific parts of the human body.</note>, a portion of which is unreadable. The contents of this page are entirely absent from the other manuscripts. It is reproduced as the frontispiece <note>The illustration facing the title page of a book.</note> to this volume.

Inside the cover are inserted some sheets of paper written by Col. Vallancey, Dr. Parsons, and Mr. Astle on the subject of the manuscript, which shed light on its later history. It was bought by Thomas Astle, Keeper of the Records in the Tower of London, in 1763. He evidently asked Dr. Parsons, a Fellow of the Royal Society <note>original: "F.R.S."</note> and member of the Society of Antiquaries, to examine the manuscript and report on it to him; he did so in the letter preserved here. With the confidence that often accompanies ignorance, Dr. Parsons states that he found it to be “very valuable on several accounts; first for its antiquity, as it was

<keywords>manuscripts, Royal Irish Academy, vellum, astronomical rotula, Zodiac, medical tract, Thomas Astle, Dr. Parsons, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical and historical description of the first manuscript (Stowe, B. II. 1), specifically recounting the eighteenth-century letters and notes regarding its origins and subsequent ownership.</meta>

"...certainly written within a century of the conversion of the people to Christianity by St. Patrick; for this is the purest and most ancient character of the Magogian tongue <note>A term used by some eighteenth-century scholars who incorrectly believed the Irish language descended from the biblical figure Magog.</note> from which the Greek and every other alphabet of Europe originated." The letter continues in this same tone. It is dated June 6, 1765.

Upon Astle’s death in 1804, the manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> came into the possession of the Marquis of Buckingham at Stowe. Around 1849, it was purchased by the Earl of Ashburnham, and in 1883, the Ashburnham collection was housed in the Royal Irish Academy Library.

At the bottom of Parson’s letter, Colonel <note>original: "Col."</note> Vallancey adds some remarks, dated London, March 10, 1782, and says, "the astronomical essay appears to be written in the language of the 13th century." He wisely does not attempt to defend his opinion or provide <note>original: "adduce"</note> any evidence to prove it. Higher up on the page, level with Parson’s signature, Vallancey notes the previous writer's wild statements with a brief <gloss>unbelievable here. Charles Vallancey. <note>original Irish: "mi-creadamhuil annso. Cathal Uabhallansi"</note></gloss>

Again, at the bottom of a letter on the same subject addressed by Vallancey to Astle—evidently written the same day as the previous remarks, since it is also dated March 10, though the year is not mentioned—there is a memorandum in Vallancey’s handwriting as follows:

> "Dublin, June 20, 1785. A copy of this work was found in the manuscript closet of St. Patrick’s Library with significant additions. Another damaged <note>original: "mutilated"</note> copy is in the possession of Colonel Vallancey."

This memorandum is important for tracing the history of the other manuscripts.

Before moving on to consider them, it should be mentioned that the name "Donncha O’Connaill" is written in ornate Roman capitals at the foot of the second-to-last <note>original: "last leaf but one"</note> leaf. It may be the name of the scribe or a former owner of the manuscript, but it is likely not authentic.

<summary>This page traces the provenance of the primary manuscript from the late 18th century through its acquisition by the Royal Irish Academy, noting conflicting expert opinions on its age.</summary>
<keywords>Stowe manuscript, Colonel Vallancey, Magogian tongue, manuscript provenance, Royal Irish Academy, Donncha O’Connaill</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the physical description of the manuscripts, transitioning from the first manuscript (B. II. 1) to a second vellum manuscript designated as "Z," and discusses its historical records within Marsh's Library in Dublin.</meta>

2. A vellum manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> of 11 leaves, very well preserved, written in double columns in a handwriting different from that of B. II. 1. The diagrams are not as neatly executed as those in manuscript B, but they all agree with the descriptions in the text and are therefore more reliable. The last page is entirely illegible; only the Latin heading and the first line of the Irish translation of the final chapter are preserved, as the remainder of the text continued on this final page. The text of Z is remarkably good. There are few corrupted passages, and any textual difficulties found in manuscript B have almost always been solved with its help.

How the manuscript came to Marsh's Library <note>Founded in 1701, this was the first public library in Ireland.</note> is not clear. The first record of it I can find is in a manuscript catalog <note>original: "MS. Catalogue"</note> of the library compiled by Robert Dougatt, who was the librarian from 1719 to 1730.

The entry—"Anonymous Elements of Astronomy <note>original Latin: "Anonymi Elementa Astronomiae"</note> M. 3. 6. 16."—is, however, on the left-hand side of the page and not in Dougatt's handwriting; it was evidently inserted after 1730. The present librarian, Dr. Newport White, tells me that the fact it is marked with the letter "M" would point to it having been in Archbishop Marsh's collection, and that it was probably lost or mislaid when the catalog was originally compiled.

Colonel <note>original: "Col."</note> Vallancey, in his *Collectanea* <note>original: "Collectanea de Rebus Hibernicis," a collection of essays on Irish history and antiquities</note>, Volume VI, part II, page 316, published in 1804, says: "It is evident that treatises on that science (astronomy) did exist about fifty years ago, when Smith and Harris published the histories of Cork (1750) and Down (1757). Both authors mention that they had seen one in manuscript, written in the Irish script <note>original: "Irish character"</note>, in the Library of St. Patrick's Cathedral. A strict search was often made at my request, but no such book could be found."

This statement, published in 1804, is certainly strange when taken in conjunction with the memorandum of 1785 quoted above. We are not certain, then, when or how...

<summary>This page describes "Manuscript Z," a high-quality vellum manuscript used to correct errors in other versions, and traces its mysterious administrative history within Marsh's Library.</summary>
<keywords>Manuscript Z, Marsh's Library, Robert Dougatt, Archbishop Marsh, Newport White, Charles Vallancey, Irish astronomy, vellum, St. Patrick's Cathedral</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the second manuscript's history in Marsh's Library and introduces a third fragment of the text.</meta>

the manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> reached Marsh's Library, but it probably was contained in Bishop Marsh's collection, was lost or mislaid between 1719–1730, was certainly there between 1750–1757, and apparently lost sight of again until 1785, when it was found with other manuscripts in a small closet in the wall. The "great additions" mentioned above by Vallancey are imaginary.

3. A vellum manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> of eight leaves <note>A leaf is a single sheet of vellum, forming two pages.</note>, in very bad condition, comprising twenty-seven chapters of the text in handwriting different from that of either of the other manuscripts. The text, as far as it goes, is also illustrated by diagrams; some of these are very stained and faint, but they appear to be correct because they agree with the descriptions in the text.

About one-half of this fragment is clear, but somewhat more of it can be read in a very good light and with the assistance of the other manuscripts.

I can find nothing of its history. In the Catalog of the Royal Irish Academy <note>original: "R.I.A."</note>, entry 23. F. 13. is followed by two blank pages. In *O'Curry's Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History* (Dublin, 1861), page 13 of the facsimiles, chapter 19 of the "F" text—"If, however, the sun were smaller, etc." <note>original Latin: "Si Autem Sol Minoris esset, etc."</note>—is reproduced with the accompanying diagram.

O'Curry dates the treatise to "around 1400 AD" <note>original Latin: "Circa A.D. 1400"</note> without providing any reason for his opinion, and describes the fragment as "a beautiful vellum manuscript of eight leaves, in the finest style of handwriting." In my opinion, the handwriting of either of the other manuscripts is much finer, particularly that of manuscript B, but of course O'Curry had not seen either of these. The same portion of the text and the diagram were reproduced in *Specimens of Irish National Manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note>, Part III, section ii, No. 23.*

I have not been able to discover how the manuscript came into the possession of the Royal Irish Academy, but it may be the "mutilated copy" mentioned as being in Colonel <note>original: "Col."</note> Vallancey's possession in the memorandum of 1785.

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial introduction, explaining the methodology used to compile the text from different manuscript sources, specifically Manuscripts B, Z, and F.</meta>

The following edition of the text is primarily based on Manuscript B. Passages that were uncertain have been corrected using Manuscripts Z and F; in the case of F, corrections were only made where the texts could be compared. Scribal errors <note>Errors made by the original person who hand-copied the manuscript.</note> have been found to be less common in Manuscript Z than in B, but on the whole, the two texts differ only slightly. 

In almost all cases where differences occur, Manuscript F has been found to agree with Z rather than with B, though occasionally the readings in F and B differ from those in Z. Regarding the diagrams, Z and F are also in agreement. At the bottom of each page, the variant readings <note>original Latin: "variae lectiones"; these are the different versions of the same passage found in various manuscripts</note> of any importance are provided. Where the text of Manuscript B has been corrected using F or Z, the original B reading is included in the comparison list. 

I have thought it unnecessary to use italics for the more common expansions of abbreviations <note>original: "compendia"; scribes often used shorthand symbols to save space on expensive vellum</note> except where there was room for doubt. With words like *speir* <note>sphere</note>, *cercall* <note>circle</note>, *naduir* <note>nature</note>, and others that frequently occur in the text, italics have been used only the first time they appear. The word *talamh* <note>earth</note> has been expanded throughout according to the consonant declension <note>a specific grammatical pattern in Irish</note>, which it follows in the two instances where it is written out in full in the manuscript. In no case has the spelling of the original text been altered; however, punctuation marks and capital letters have been added where necessary. I have designated the Stabius transcript (3. B. 32.) as **S** in the footnotes and glossary.

### *POSSIBLE DATE*

I fear it will be nearly impossible to establish even an approximate date for this text. Evidence from within the work provides clues that are, at best, inconclusive. The lack of other dated texts and the unique style of this work compared to others make the task even more difficult. 

It is enough to say that no evidence has been found of linguistic features that were not part of the spoken language of, say, the last five hundred years. The linguistic features associated with Middle Irish—the survival...

<summary>The editor explains the technical process of comparing manuscripts B, Z, and F to create this edition and discusses the difficulty of dating the text due to its linguistic style.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript collation, scribal errors, Middle Irish, diagrams, dating methods, variant readings</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial introduction, focusing on the linguistic analysis and dating of the Irish manuscript compared to the Latin and Arabic originals.</meta>

...and the confusion of infixed pronouns <note>pronouns inserted into the middle of a verb, common in Old Irish but lost in the modern language</note>, the persistence of the neuter gender, the shifting verb forms, and noun declensions—all these have disappeared. The infixed pronoun (a reliable test) does not occur; therefore, in short, the language of the tract may be regarded as a good example of how technical subjects were handled in the later schools of learning. The forms have stabilized and are practically the same as those used by proficient speakers of Irish at the present day.

Determining the exact state of a language at any given period is always difficult, but it is doubly so in the case of Irish. Much of the Irish prose of the modern period—say from 1500 onward—is obviously not the Irish of the period in which it was written. There is a conscious striving after words and forms that were long since obsolete or at least archaic, with a deliberate avoidance of the language of everyday life. This generally results in a pedantic <note>narrowly or ostentatiously learned</note> mass of bombast <note>high-sounding language with little meaning</note> that is wholly unnatural and quite valueless from an artistic or linguistic <note>original: "philologically"</note> perspective.

The subject matter of our text prevents all that rhetoric and expansion so popular in the translation of narrative stories. Since clarity is the chief object, the style is simple and straightforward, sometimes even plain in its descriptions. It bears no trace of affectation, becoming almost conversational in places, so that one is tempted to categorize it as a sample of the spoken Irish of the fourteenth or fifteenth centuries. However, any such attempts at dating must necessarily be speculative. 

External evidence provides us with some information in this regard. The Arabic work of Messahalah <note>Masha'allah ibn Athari (c. 740–815 AD), an influential Jewish astronomer and astrologer</note> was translated into Latin in the thirteenth century—I cannot at present find a more accurate date. If the reference to spectacles in chapter VII were in the original of the second Latin text from which the Irish translator worked, and not a later addition <note>original: "interpolation"</note>, it could scarcely have been written before 1325; certainly, the Irish text could not have been produced before then.

<summary>The editor discusses the linguistic features of the Irish text, noting its modern style and lack of archaic affectation, and uses internal clues like the mention of spectacles to estimate a post-1325 date for the translation.</summary>
<keywords>Irish language, linguistic dating, infixed pronouns, Messahalah, medieval translation, technical Irish, spectacles, philology</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page concludes the editor's introduction, discussing the dating of the manuscript and acknowledging scholarly contributors.</meta>

...then. If we take 1325 as the earliest possible date <note>original: "superior limit"</note> for the Irish translation, it would suggest that it was translated almost immediately. If we assume the mention of spectacles was not in the original Latin, that date might still remain the earliest limit, although their use in Ireland probably began somewhat later than in mainland Europe <note>original: "the Continent"</note> and England.

It is, however, unlikely that the Irish translation followed the Latin version so quickly. The text would have to reach Ireland, be translated, and then be copied. This process might take place within a few years, or on the other hand, it might take a century to occur. The former is perhaps more likely. Mr. Close, in his already much-quoted article, cites the case of *The Rose of English Medicine* <note>original: *Rosa Medicinae Anglicana*</note>, by John Gaddesden, who died in 1361. Extracts from that work were translated into Irish in the *Yellow Book of Lecan* <note>a famous medieval Irish manuscript compiled around 1390</note> in 1390.

That none of the three manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note> contains the original translation is proved, I think, by the distortion <note>original: "mutilation"</note> of the Latin spelling in the chapter headings; this could not have happened if the writer had the actual Latin text in front of him.

I admit that the previous points prove nothing definite, but it is probable that such translations were made and used for educational purposes in Ireland during the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries—the period which Mrs. J. R. Green associates with the "Second Irish Revival."

In conclusion, I must thank Professor Bergin, whom I had the honor of being a pupil of, and at whose suggestion this edition was undertaken, for much valuable advice and assistance, which was gladly and readily given. To him fell the thankless task of proofreading, and without his aid, the progress of this work through the printing press would have been difficult.

->MAURA POWER.<-

March, 1914.

# AN IRISH
## ASTRONOMICAL TREATISE <note>original: "TRACT"</note>

GLORY TO GOD THE BEGINNING <note>original Latin: "GLORIA DEO PRINCIPIO"</note> — that is, glory to God, a beginning without beginning and an end without end; to Him who existed forever before all things, who will exist forever after all things, and whom no human sense or reason can reach to know or recognize what He is.

And since He did not wish to remain forever without revealing Himself to humanity, He taught His works and His art to the learned, so that through the works the Maker <note>original: "toibriteoir"</note> might be known, and through the deeds the Creator <term>Creator</term> <note>original Irish: "tuismigteoir," literally 'the one who begets or generates'</note> might be recognized. Therefore, it is fitting for the learned to whom He revealed His secrets to give glory to Him above all other things.

Therefore, here begin the difficult and complex questions of the ancients <term>ancients</term> <note>original: "na narsatan," likely referring to ancient authorities like Aristotle or Ptolemy</note> regarding the study of [His] works. Specifically, it concerns the qualities of the firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>The heavens or the sphere of the stars</note> and the qualities of the four elements <term>four elements</term> <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, the building blocks of the universe in medieval science</note>, as well as their arrangement and creation, with the help of the Creator whom we mentioned, and through very true reasons, necessary arguments, and unbreakable, undeniable conclusions.

There are forty chapters in this book in total, and this is the first chapter of them, namely:

->Concerning the creation and revelation of the firmament.<-
->Concerning the four elements and their places as the Creator ordained them.<-
->Concerning movements and natures.<-

And since He did not wish to remain forever without revealing Himself to humanity, He instructed the learned in His works and arts, so that the Maker <note>original: "the worker"</note> might be known from His works and the Creator <term>Creator</term> <note>original: "tuismigteoir," which can mean one who generates or begets</note> from His deeds. ### 1. THE FIRMAMENT IS, ETC.
<note>original Latin: "Firmamentum est etcetera"</note>

That is, the firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>the heavens or the sky conceived as a solid dome or a series of spheres</note> is round, having been created with a beginning and an end, and it is perpetually governed by its own Creator. And the stars <term>stars</term> <note>original: "airdreannaid," which can refer to planets or constellations</note> are in the seven spheres of the firmament like fixed nails in a board; they have no motion of their own, except for the motion of the sphere <term>sphere</term> <note>original: "cercaille," literally "circle," referring to the transparent orbiting shells of the Ptolemaic system</note> in which they are located.

And it is for this reason that they are not seen moving past one another, nor one behind the other, but they remain in a single, everlasting, and eternal order. They maintain a constant distance from each other, remaining as near or as far as they have always been. To prove that this governance is maintained by the Creator of the world, and that it will remain forever upon His own works, the course He ordained for them at the beginning of the world is maintained by them perfectly and without fail.

To prove this, the learned have knowledge of every natural thing before it occurs; for they know perfectly the movement of the stars and the planets every year, every month, every week, every day, and every moment. And besides this, they have knowledge of the seasons themselves before they arrive—namely, knowledge of summer, autumn, winter, and spring—and knowledge of everything that naturally occurs within them. This is a certain argument to prove that He who created the world still governs it; for if He did not, the things we have mentioned would change...

<summary>This page concludes the table of contents and introduces the first chapter, arguing that the unchanging, predictable movement of the stars and seasons proves the world is governed by a divine Creator.</summary>
<keywords>firmament, celestial spheres, stars, planets, seasons, divine governance, Ptolemaic astronomy, Creator</keywords>

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

<margin>Chapter 1</margin>
<margin>The eight inhabited regions of the earth.</margin>
<margin>The two places where the entire year consists of one day and one night <note>a reference to the North and South Poles</note>.</margin>
<margin>The winds and their nature.</margin>

## 1. THE FIRMAMENT IS, ETC. <note>original Latin: "Firmamentum est, etcetera"</note> 

The firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>the heavens or the sky, traditionally imagined as a series of solid, transparent spheres</note> is round according to its creation; it will eventually come to an end, and it is perpetually ruled by its own Creator.

There are stars in the seven spheres <term>spheres</term> <note>the nested, rotating shells that carried the planets and stars in the ancient geocentric model of the universe</note> of the firmament, set like fixed nails in a plank. They have no motion of their own, except for the movement of the circle in which they are placed. For that reason, they are never seen moving past each other or following after each other; instead, they always preserve a single, constant, and everlasting order, maintaining equal distances to and from one another.

As a proof that this governance is maintained by the Creator of the world, and that it will depend upon His works forever, these stars follow—without deception and without fail—the course He ordained for them at the beginning of the world.

As further proof of that, the learned have knowledge of every natural phenomenon before it occurs, for they fully understand the motion of the stars and of the planets for every year, month, week, day, and every moment. Furthermore, they even have knowledge of the seasons before they have even begun—possessing knowledge of summer, autumn, winter, and spring, and everything that occurs naturally within them. This is a certain argument to prove that He who created the world is still governing it; otherwise, the things I have mentioned would have altered...

<summary>This page describes the fixed, orderly motion of the stars within the celestial spheres and argues that the predictability of astronomy and the seasons proves the universe is still directed by its Creator.</summary>
<keywords>firmament, celestial spheres, fixed stars, divine governance, astronomy, seasons, Irish Astronomical Tract</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for divine order within the universe, asserting that the regularity of celestial movements and the seasons proves that the Creator still actively governs the world. It then transitions to describing the physical arrangement of the four elements—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—within the cosmos.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

...contrary to the operation we have described. And thus the high stars <note>original: hairdrinnaigh; likely referring to the fixed stars or the higher planets</note> and the planets would be—sometimes faster and sometimes slower than one another, and at other times stationary without moving at all. And in the same way, the seasons would come out of their proper order, and there would be natural days longer than others. And consequently, the fruits of the earth would sometimes appear, and at other times nothing at all would grow. And so everything between heaven and earth would be troubled and confused <note>original: measgaithi; mixed or chaotic</note>, without the philosopher or the expert knowing what to say of them. And from that, it would follow again that the noble sciences <term>noble sciences</term> <note>the liberal arts, particularly astronomy, mathematics, and logic</note> that were established would be destroyed, along with the movement, rest, number, placement, and ordering of the works of God.

Therefore, since we see that the noble sciences exist and that every other thing is un-troubled and un-confused, occurring according to a single order and coming certainly in their own proper times, from this it is understood that He who created the world is still ordering and governing <note>original: follamnugad; ruling or administering</note> it.

### Concerning the four elements and their places, and how the Creator ordered them. <note>original: tuismiteoir; literally "the begetter" or "one who brings forth"</note>

## 2. THE EARTH IS IN THE MIDDLE OF THE WORLD, ETC.
<note>original Latin: Terra est in medio mundi etc.</note>

That is, the earth is a round point in the exact center of the world like the shape of a round ball <term>round ball</term> <note>original: liatroide cruinne; a spherical globe</note>, without any substance beneath it to hold it up. And water, according to its nature, is on every side around it; yet the Creator brought forth the upper part of the earth as a dwelling place for humans and for the animals that could not live under water. And the air is around them both. And the fire is around those three. And the firmament is on every side around those four. And this is the description of those four elements <term>four elements</term> <note>the classical elements of Earth, Water, Air, and Fire, which were believed to make up all matter</note>:

<summary>This page argues that the stability of nature and the "noble sciences" proves God's ongoing governance of the universe, and it describes the Earth as a sphere suspended at the center of the four elements.</summary>
<keywords>divine providence, noble sciences, four elements, geocentrism, spherical earth, Creator, cosmology</keywords>

It then transitions to describing the physical arrangement of the four elements—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—within the cosmos.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>

<margin>Chap. ii.]</margin>
...by the functions I have described, the stars and the planets would sometimes be faster and sometimes slower than one another, and at other times they would be stationary, not moving at all. In the same manner, the seasons would come out of their proper order, and natural days would vary in length from one another. Consequently, the fruits of the earth would grow at one time and be barren at another. Therefore, everything in heaven and earth would be chaotic and confused, so that neither the philosopher nor the seer would know what to say about them. Furthermore, it would follow that the exact sciences <term>exact sciences</term> <note>original: na heolaidheachta deimhneacha; likely referring to the quadrivium: arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy</note>—which were established to study the motion, rest, number, position, and order of the works of God—would be rendered useless.

However, since we see that the exact sciences do exist, and that everything else occurs predictably in its own season, regularly and without confusion according to a single order, we know from this that He who created the world still orders and governs it.

---

### The four elements and their positions as the Creator ordained them.

## 2. THE EARTH IS IN THE MIDDLE OF THE WORLD, ETC.
<note>original: TERRA EST IN MEDIO MUNDI, ETCETERA</note>

The Earth is a round point in the very center of the universe, fashioned as a perfect sphere with no physical substance beneath to support it. The water, as is natural, surrounds it on every side; moreover, the Creator created the upper part of the Earth as a dwelling place for humans and for the animals that cannot live underwater. Air surrounds both the Earth and the water. Fire surrounds those three elements, and the firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>In medieval cosmology, the firmament was the outermost sphere of the physical universe that enclosed the four elements</note> is on all sides around those four.

The following is a description of those four elements:—

Description of fire: a hot, dry, burning, light, fluid, and mobile body, beneath which is the air. Description of the water: a cold, moist, fluid, and mobile body; heavy compared to the air and light compared to the earth, beneath which is the earth. Description of the earth: a cold, dry, heavy, and difficult-to-move body that lies at the center of the entire world. ### 3. THE PHILOSOPHERS SAY, ETCETERA. <note>original: *DICUNT PHILOSOPHI ETCETERA*</note>

The philosophers say that there are three types of motion: motion from the center, motion toward the center, and motion around the center.

Motion from the center is the motion that travels outward from the earth equally toward every part of the surrounding circle. Motion toward the center is the motion that travels downward from the surrounding circle to the earth. Motion around the center is the motion that moves according to the path of the surrounding circle. It is from these primary motions that every other motion in the world is generated. Among these is the motion of the four elements <term>elements</term> <note>original: *dúla*</note>; for some of them move from the center and some toward the center. Nothing moves naturally around the center except the firmament or any of its constituent parts.

Of the two elements that move toward the center—namely, earth and water—the earth’s motion toward the center is faster than that of water. Of the two elements that move from the center—namely, air and fire—the fire moves faster from the center than the air. Thus, the elements that move toward the center are considered "heavy," and the elements that move away from the center are "light." And it is understood how they move...

3. THE PHILOSOPHERS SAY, AND SO ON <note>original: "DICUNT PHILOSOPHI, ETCETERA"</note>. The philosophers declare that there are three types of motion: motion from the center, motion toward the center, and motion around the center.

Motion to the center is the motion that proceeds downward from the surrounding circle to the earth. Motion around the center is the motion that revolves with the circle <note>original Latin footnote 2: "quod revolvitur circa terram," meaning "which revolves around the earth"</note>. From these primary motions, every motion in the world is produced. One of these is the motion of the four elements, for some of them move from the center and some toward the center; nothing moves around the center naturally except for the firmament or some portion of it.

Of the two elements that move toward the center—earth and water—the motion toward the center is swifter in earth than in water. Of the two elements that move from the center—air and fire—fire moves more swiftly from the center than air. Thus, the elements that move toward the center are considered heavy, and the elements that move from the center are considered light. From these facts...

Freezing frost <note>original: "cuisne reoigh"</note> shows us immediately, and in that same moment, the power of its coldness. And the reason we perceive those extreme <note>original: "ainmesarda," meaning immoderate or unbalanced</note> things is because we have a temperate nature ourselves.

This is the reason why it is said that some of these qualities are active <note>original: "gnimhach"</note> and some are passive. 

Every body from the sphere of fire downwards is composed <note>original: "cumaisce"</note> of these, and they themselves are unmixed <note>original: "neamhcumusca," meaning simple elements</note>, except in the sense that they are said to be composed of their own qualities. For example, fire is hot and dry, composed of those two qualities, for it is those two qualities that predominate in fire. And from dryness and wetness, air is composed, for those are the qualities that predominate there. <note>In traditional Aristotelian physics, air was usually described as hot and moist; the author here is explaining the mixture of qualities that define each element.</note> 

And it is the same way with the mastery of the other two elements regarding their own qualities. Although these four elements are composed of the primary qualities, they are called "unmixed" in comparison to the composite bodies <note>such as plants, animals, or minerals</note> that are composed from them. Thus, these four are both composed and unmixed at the same time: they are unmixed when looking at everything composed from them, but they are composed when looking at the primary qualities of which they actually consist.

And so, it is proper for an unmixed body to have an unmixed <note>simple/linear</note> motion, and for a composite body to have a composite motion.

Although these four elements are composed of the primary properties, they are termed "simple" in comparison with the physical bodies that are composed of the elements themselves. Thus, these four are both compound and simple: simple compared to every object that is made from them, and compound compared to the primary qualities which are essential to their nature. Therefore, it is fitting for a simple body to have a simple motion, and for a compound body to have a compound motion.

**Properties:** qualities or characteristics
**Passive qualities:** qualities that are acted upon rather than acting (wetness/dryness)
**Active qualities:** qualities that produce a physical sensation immediately (heat/cold)
**Fire:** the uppermost and lightest element
**Hoar frost:** frozen dew
**Sphere of fire:** the region above the air and below the moon in ancient cosmology
**Elements:** the four building blocks of the world (fire, air, water, earth)
**Air:** the element between fire and water
**Prime qualities:** the four basic states (hot, cold, wet, dry)
**Simple motion:** movement in a single straight line (either up or down)
**Compound motion:** complex movement resulting from a mixture of elements

5. **THE EARTH IS IN THE MIDDLE, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "TERRA EST IN MEDIO ETCETERA"</note> That is to say, the earth is located in the exact center of the firmament, serving as the destination for the downward descent of heavy bodies, acting as the center <term>seantrom</term> <note>from the Latin "centrum," the mathematical center-point</note>—that is, the middle point of a round object. And as we have said, the natural place of water is to be around the earth. If water found a path without hindrance from the earth—since it is itself a heavy, flowing, and mobile body—it would not stop until it reached the center of the earth. It would remain there, for that is, as we said, the final place of travel for heavy bodies. And the parts of the water press against one another, flowing over each other to seek the center of the world as their own natural place, if only the firmness of the earth would allow them. It is necessary then, since the earth is spherical and solid, resisting the water and preventing it from reaching the center, that the water is also spherical around the earth. In the same way, the other two elements <note>air and fire</note> that move upward from the center toward the surrounding circle also have a round shape.

For fire, because of its lightness, pulls upward until the firm and incorruptible circle of the Moon catches it. Since fire finds no way past it, it is held and preserves itself under the round vault <term>ascaill</term> <note>literally "armpit," used here to describe the concave interior curve or hollow of a celestial sphere</note> of that circle. Therefore, it is necessary for fire itself to be round, just as the circle...

5. **THE EARTH IS IN THE MIDDLE, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "TERRA EST IN MEDIO, ETCETERA"</note> The earth is in the very center of the firmament, acting as a center point for the descent of heavy bodies—that is, a middle point within a round object.

As I mentioned, the natural position of the water is surrounding the earth. If it were given space without obstruction from the earth—since water is a heavy, liquid, and movable body—it would not stop until it reached the center of the earth. It would remain there because that center, as we mentioned, is the ultimate point of motion for heavy bodies. The parts of the water press against each other, seeking the center of the universe as their natural position, if only the solidity of the earth permitted it. Since the earth is spherical and firm, it resists the water and prevents it from reaching the center. Therefore, the water must form a sphere around the earth. In the same way, the other two elements <note>The elements of air and fire</note> that move upward from the center toward the surrounding outer circle also have a round shape.

For fire, because of its lightness, continues to draw upward until it meets the firm, indestructible sphere of the moon. Since it cannot pass through it, the fire stays and covers itself <note>The original phrasing suggests it "has kept and covered itself"; the editor notes this and the remainder of the sentence are an interpolation, or a later addition to the text</note> beneath the round axis of that circle. Therefore, fire itself must be round, just like the circle of the moon that contains it.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elemental spheres, specifically explaining how the four elements (earth, water, air, and fire) are nested within one another to form a perfect, continuous sphere without any vacuum, and how their qualities—such as heat and dryness—relate to one another.</meta>

the sphere <note>original: ré; here meaning the celestial sphere or circle</note> that encloses them within itself. It is that circle which is the final destination for the motion of light bodies. What makes the air round is that the round surface of the water and its lower curves are positioned beneath it, while the upper part of the air itself is positioned within the lower curve of the fire. Since the fire and the water are both perfectly spherical <note>original: comcruinn</note>, as we have proven, the air that is enclosed between them must also be round, following their shape. This arrangement of the elements is so tightly packed, each fitting into the curve of the other, that nothing else can exist between them; thus, no empty space <note>a vacuum</note> can exist in the entire world.

6. **IT IS KNOWN THAT EACH OF THE FOUR ELEMENTS, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "NOTUM EST UNUM QUODQUE QUATUOR ELEMENTORUM ETCETERA"</note> It is clear that each of the four elements <term>duile</term> is contrary to the others in their natures, their locations, and their movements. Among all things that move away from the center <note>upward</note>, fire is the fastest; likewise, among all things that move toward the center <note>downward</note>, earth is the fastest. Thus, earth and fire are contrary to one another because the quality of heat in the fire causes it to be light, while the quality of coldness in the earth causes it to be heavy. 

Understand that when it is said that earth and fire (or any other two elements) are contrary to each other, it is the qualities of the elements that are understood to be contrary at that time, and not their actual substances. For the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> says in the *Book of Categories* <note>original Latin: "in libro predicamentorum"</note>, "nothing is contrary to substance" <note>original Latin: "substantiae nihil est contrarium"</note>, because a substance itself has no opposite. For this reason, when it is said that fire is hot and dry and earth is cold and dry, the heat and cold of those two elements are contrary to each other, yet they are in agreement with each other because it is dryness <term>tirmigacht</term> that produces speed in both of them.

<margin>Chapter 6</margin> encloses the fire within itself. That circle is the final destination for the movement of light bodies. What makes the air spherical is that the surface of the spherical water is pressed up into its lower curve <note>original: "embrace," likely referring to the concave/convex interface between elemental spheres</note>, while the upper part of the air itself is held within the lower curve of the fire. Since both the fire and the water are spherical, as I have proven, the air that is enclosed by them must also be spherical to match their shape.

The position of these closely-packed elements is such that they fit into one another's curves, so that nothing else can exist between them; therefore, there can be no vacuum <note>an empty space containing no matter</note> in the whole of creation.

***

6. **IT IS KNOWN THAT EACH OF THE ELEMENTS, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "NOTUM EST UNUM QUODQUE ELEMENTORUM, ETCETERA"</note> It is clear that each of the four elements is opposed to the others in its nature, position, and movement. For of all things that move away from the center <note>upward, away from the Earth</note>, fire is the fastest. Likewise, of all the things that move toward the center <note>downward</note>, earth is the fastest. Thus, earth and fire are opposites because the heating properties of fire make it light, while the cold properties of earth make it heavy. 

Note that when we say that earth and fire, or any other two elements, are opposed to each other, it is the properties <term>qualities</term> of the elements that are understood to be opposed, and not their physical substances. As the philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> says in the *Book of Categories* <note>original Latin: "Liber Praedicamentorum"</note>, "There is no contrary to substance" <note>original Latin: "Substantiae nihil est contrarium"</note>—meaning the basic essence of a thing cannot have an opposite, only its qualities can.

Thus, when we say that fire is hot and dry, and earth is cold and dry, the heat and cold of those two elements are opposites. However, they are in agreement with each other regarding their dryness, as that dryness causes them both to move quickly.

And so it is that the air and the water are both in agreement and in disagreement with each other. They are in agreement in their passive <term>receptive</term> qualities—namely, moisture, which works to produce slowness in both of them alike. However, they are in disagreement in their active <term>productive</term> qualities—namely, the heat that produces lightness in the air and the coldness that produces heaviness in the water. 

In the same way, fire and water are in total disagreement with each other in both their active and passive qualities; for fire is hot, dry, fast, and light, whereas water is cold, wet, slow, and heavy. Finally, it is evident that those things which possess direct <term>linear</term> motion remain in their own natural places unless they are moved out of them against their nature. When one element is transformed into another by the power of that element, or when one is forced out of its own natural place, it will return as soon as it finds a cause or a small amount of help. Without hesitation, it makes a swift and sudden turn back toward its own nature and its natural, ancestral home.

It is clear again that everything which moves away from the center <note>upward, away from the Earth</note> is hot, and everything that moves toward the center <note>downward</note> is cold; and that everything which speeds up its movement is incomparably dry, while everything that slows its movement is incomparably wet.

And it is in this way that the Blessed Creator conceived and ordered the world from the four elements.

Another argument to prove the same point: if you were to travel from the center of the world toward the northern sea, stars would be revealed to you there that you had never seen from the center of the world. Conversely, some of the southern stars that you used to see from the center of the world would be hidden from you. Likewise, if you were to make that same journey toward the south, stars would be revealed to you that you had not seen in the north or at the center of the world, and those stars you had seen in those other places would be hidden from you.

Therefore, it is certain that it is the curvature of the Earth rising up behind you during your journey that reveals the stars ahead of you and conceals the stars that are behind you.

Furthermore, regarding the same subject: from every place you stand upon the Earth, you see a specific part of the firmament <term>the heavens or sky</term> that you cannot see from any other place. From this, it is proven that the vault <note>original: boga — literally "bow" or "arch"</note> surrounding the Earth is perfectly spherical, and that the Earth sits at its center. 

We also say, regarding the same subject, that as the sun travels around the Earth, it illuminates the half of the Earth that is directly facing it. The light existing between the sun and the Earth constitutes "day" perpetually; meanwhile, the other half of the Earth is always dark because of the Earth's own shadow. This dark shadow is what we call "night." Thus, whatever path the sun takes around the Earth, the day follows it, and the night flees before it to the opposite side of the Earth.

It is obvious to every learned person in the world that this is false, since we can see that the sun remains the same size whether it is in the east, the west, or the north <note>The "north" here likely refers to the sun's position at the highest point of its arc in the sky</note> of the world.

...and let B represent the west and C represent the other side. I will draw a small circle to represent the sun's body at each of these points, and then I will draw three lines from the center of the Earth to the orbital circle of the sun. One of those lines will go to A, the second line to B, and the third line to C. It is obvious to anyone who considers this that these three lines are all of equal length.

Therefore, the Earth is at an equal distance from the sun regardless of where the sun may be—whether it is rising, setting, or at the peak of its orbit. Thus, it is clear that the Earth and the sun's orbit around the Earth are perfectly symmetrical on all sides. 

If someone were to argue against this by pointing out that the sun clearly looks larger when rising or setting than it does at the peak of midday, and that this suggests the sun is further away from us at midday than at those other positions—concluding that this proves the Earth is a flat plane without any curvature <term>original: atmeracht — meaning the outward curve of a sphere</term>—we would respond with a proper explanation <note>original: "solaidi" — an example or illustration used to prove a point</note> for that argument. This phenomenon happens frequently, but it does not happen always; and when it does occur, this is the reason why. 

When the sun is rising or setting, it draws up moisture and rain. Dark, damp vapors <note>original: "dethaighi" — literally "smokes" or "vapors"</note> rise high into the air between us and the sun. When we look at the sun through these vapors, the mist widens and thickens our field of vision. Due to the physical density and "earthy" nature of that mist, the sun appears larger through it than it would if the mist were not there. However, as the day progresses and the sun reaches the peak of the firmament <term>the heavens or the sky</term> with no mist between us and it, the sun is then seen in its true size.

If anyone should argue against this by claiming that the sun appears distinctly larger when rising or setting than it does at its highest point at midday, and suggests that this means it is further away at midday than when it is in those other positions—concluding that this proves the earth is a flat plane without <term>convexity</term> <note>original: "convexity"; the outward curvature of a spherical surface</note>—I reply to them with a proper solution to that argument. 

That phenomenon often happens, but not always. When it does occur, the reason is that when the sun is rising or setting, it draws up moisture and rain, and dark, damp vapors rise to a great height between us and the sun. Then, when we look at the sun, the mist we see broadens and amplifies our <term>sphere of vision</term> <note>original: "sphere of vision"; the perceived area or range of sight</note>. Therefore, according to the density and thickness of that mist, the sun appears larger through it than it would appear if that mist were not present. As the day advances and the sun reaches the highest point of the <term>firmament</term> <note>original: "firmament"; the sky or heavens, conceived as a vast dome</note> with no mist between us and it, we see it in its own proper size.

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

This example is clear in the case of a naked person under water, for to their <term>vision</term> <note>original: "radarc"; sight or visual perception</note>, an object seems larger under water than it does outside of the water. However, this proves nothing except that the wet, dense water is broadening and magnifying the vision there, preventing it from reaching the person in its own natural, refined state. It is for this same reason that things seen through glass appear larger and <term>thicker</term> <note>original: "roime"; broader or more magnified</note> than they do from the outside. That is why elderly people, whose sight is failing so that they cannot read small letters, use glass <term>spectacles</term> <note>original: "spechlai"; an early reference to eyeglasses</note> to magnify the letters. This is the same reason that the sun appears larger in the early morning and in the evening than it does at midday, as we have already stated.

And if any ignorant person should say the same—that is, that the earth is a flat plane and that the sun travels in a perfect circle around the earth—and that all the people of the world would see it at the same hour when it rises in one place, we would say that such a claim is a falsehood. To illustrate this, imagine two cities in your mind: one city in the east of the world and another city in the west. Imagine if the earth were a flat plane without <term>convexity</term> <note>original: "nematmar"; literally 'un-swollen,' meaning lacking the outward curvature of a sphere</note>, as this current opinion suggests. Then the people of the eastern city would see the sun close to them as it rises, appearing very large; and after it had traveled its circuit, they would see it setting in the west at a much smaller size than before. It would be the reverse for the people of the western city: they would see the sun rising at a small size because of its distance from them, and setting near them at a much larger size because of its proximity. In that case, it would appear to the people of the eastern city that the first half of the day was shorter than the second half. Similarly, it would appear to the people of the western city that the latter half [of the day was shorter]...

To everyone in the world who wishes<gloss>103</gloss> to examine it closely, it is clear that [the theory of a flat earth] is not the truth. Anyone who wishes to examine the matter closely will see that the [flat earth theory] is untrue. Therefore, the premises from which it arises are erroneous—namely, the idea that the earth is a flat plane without any <term>convexity</term> <note>original: "convexity"; the outward curvature of a sphere</note>. For the sake of the truth, it is declared that the earth is instead convex and spherical.

As proof that this is true: the sun does not set in the same place relative to any two cities in the world. If you change your position, the point of the sunset changes relative to you as well. For example, if you were in the city of Jerusalem, the sun would set for you in the direction of Rome. If you were in Rome, it would set in the west of France. If you were there, it would set in the west of Spain. Beyond that, some location in the Atlantic Ocean would hide the sun from you. If the sea could support you and you were able to follow the sun, its setting point would change relative to you every day that you followed it, until at last it would set in the very same place where you saw it rising when you were in the city of Jerusalem.

It is certain that there is no actual difference in the sunset itself; rather, it is the curvature of the earth constantly coming between us and the sun that causes this change. If the earth were a flat plane, as the theory mentioned above suggests, there would be only one place where the sun would rise for all the inhabitants of the world, and only one place where it would set. Consequently, since there are many different places where it rises and sets, the earth must be round and not a flat plane.

Therefore, let the Eternal First Cause <note>A philosophical and theological term for God as the creator and prime mover of the universe.</note> who ordered it thus be blessed forever.

## 8. SEAS AND RIVERS CHANGE VARIOUS PLACES, ETC. <note>original Latin: "Maria et flumina diuersa loca matant etcetera"</note>

The sea<gloss>1</gloss>—that is, the sea and the streams change in many places, though this is not evident until after many hundreds of years have passed. This change happens in this way: as the waters break down the hills, the earth of the hills falls into the depths of the waters and fills the place where the water was. Since the waters are forced to leave their own place against their will, they must go to another place<gloss>2</gloss> where they can find a path.<gloss>3</gloss> And for this reason, the cities, towns, and territories where<gloss>4</gloss> people dwell in valleys and low-lying places near the sea—the sea comes over them<gloss>5</gloss> and submerges them.<gloss>6</gloss>

It is the "sea" that comes from the clouds <note>The author refers to rain as a "sea" falling from the clouds.</note> which is the cause of this; for when it falls, it is in a constant flow<gloss>7</gloss> across the earth, excavating the ground. Whatever it finds in the earth that is most easily opened<gloss>8</gloss> and weakest, it carries<gloss>9</gloss> from place to place in the streams. The power of the streams sweeps these same things into<gloss>11</gloss> the sea, and the bottom of the sea is filled with them. It is for this reason<gloss>12</gloss> that the sea is forced to leave that place and move to another. It breaks down the hills in the place toward which it goes<gloss>13</gloss> and fills the valleys. In this way, the places of human habitation and possession<gloss>14</gloss> are changed—namely, the cities, territories, hills, and valleys.

For it is a natural property of water, since it is fluid, not to remain constantly in one place but to move from place to place. After that, the streams carry the weakest earth with them into the sea;<gloss>15, 16</gloss> and because it remains there for a very long time, it calcifies and hardens until it becomes stone.<gloss>17</gloss> By the constant striking of the waves beneath and over it continually, the stones are worn down<gloss>18</gloss> and smoothed,<gloss>19</gloss> and different shapes are put upon them; for they are made into fragments<gloss>20</gloss>—some small and round, some broad, some long, and some sharp.

Finally, the remaining earth is left standing as hills and large mountains between them. This is how the blessed Creator of the world ordered the natural environment.

### 9. Heat and Cold Working Violently in the Earth <note>original Latin: "Calor et frigidus opus uetimens in terrum"</note>

The actions that cold and heat perform within the earth are violent and swift. In the summer, when the sun's heat warms the surface of the earth, the cold flees from the heat into the depths of the earth, because two opposing forces cannot occupy the same space. This cools the water found underground, which is why well water is cold in the summer. 

For the same reason, because the sun is far from us in the winter, the cold gains strength on the entire surface of the earth. This drives the heat to flee into the earth's interior <note>original: "inne," meaning bowels or internal parts</note>. Consequently, well waters are warm in the winter.

When that cold remains powerfully within the earth during the summer, it hardens and compresses itself because the earth’s density and firmness do not allow it to escape. The longer it stays there, the more its strength and power increase. 

In the winter, when the cold on the earth's surface drives the heat into the interior, the heat encounters its "prisoner"—the cold already trapped within the earth. These forces act against each other, as each tries to destroy the other. This causes the earth to shake, an event known as an <term>earthquake</term> <note>original Latin: "terra motus"; original Irish: "crit na talman"</note>. This shaking causes the earth to crack and break open. A powerful wind, accompanied by thunder and a great noise, erupts from the rupture, carrying soil and stones with it. If one of these stones strikes a person, an animal, a castle, or any other solid object, it will inevitably pass right through it.

It often happens during an <term>earthquake</term> that the sun becomes darkened. This darkness is caused by the powerful wind blowing out from the cracks in the earth; it blows so...

## 9. Heat and Cold Work Violently in the Earth, etc. <note>original Latin: *Calor et frigus opus vehemens in terram, etcetera*</note>

The actions that cold and heat perform within the earth are intense and swift. In summer, the heat of the sun warms the surface of the earth. Since two opposing forces <note>original: "contrary things"</note> cannot remain in the same place, the cold flees from the heat into the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth. This cools the water found underground, which is why well water is cold in the summer.

For the same reason, because of the sun’s distance from us in winter, the cold gains strength across the entire surface of the earth. This drives the heat to flee into the earth's interior—therefore, well water is warm in winter. When that cold remains in the center of the earth with all its strength during the summer, it concentrates and compresses itself there, because the density and firmness of the earth do not allow it to escape. The deeper it is, the greater its power and strength.

In the winter, when the cold on the earth's surface drives the heat into the center of the earth, it finds its "prisoner" already inside—that is, the cold of the earth's center. They act upon each other, and as each seeks to destroy the other, the earth shakes. It is to this shaking that the term <term>earthquake</term> <note>original Latin: *terræ motus*</note> is applied.

As a result of that shock, the earth is cut and broken, and a great wind accompanied by thunder and noise erupts from that breach. This wind carries sods of earth and stones with it; no person, animal, building, or any other solid thing struck by one of those stones could escape it passing right through them.

It often happens during an **earthquake** <note>original Latin: *terræ motus*</note> that the sun is darkened. The cause of that darkness is the powerful wind that comes from that rupture of the

Following this, the variety of flavors found in water is due to the diversity of the places where they are located. Although all water has the same basic substance <note>original: *tustaint*</note>, it acquires various accidental qualities <note>original: *aicidec*</note> according to the flavor of the earth in which it resides. Therefore, water in stony or sandy places has a sweet taste; water in salty earth has a salty taste; water in muddy <note>original: *labain*</note> ground has a harsh <note>original: *tragain*</note> taste; and water in bitter earth containing sulfur or alum <note>original: *ailimi*</note>, or mines of brass or copper <note>original: *umha*</note> or other bitter things, has a bitter taste. Thus, according to the accidental quality of the flavor of the earth where the waters are, the water's own flavor changes.

Furthermore, when streams traveling across the surface of the earth encounter soft, open ground, they penetrate it and create hidden paths for themselves underground until they hit impenetrable earth that blocks their passage. When they are obstructed in this way below, they burst through the earth above them, forming springs <note>original: *toibreaca*</note> proportional to the size of the underground streams from which they originate or the amount of rainfall.

...from which the rivers emerge, for it is in this way that springs rise and fall <note>original: *linaid ⁊ traigid* — literally "fill and ebb."</note>.

The saltiness of the sea originates from several factors: from its own antiquity, from the constant battering of the waves against the rocks, from the sun’s passage directly above it, and from the expulsion of the sweetest parts of the water by the sun’s heat. For the heat of the sun draws the finest and sweetest portion of the seawater up into the clouds; this is what creates dew, rain, snow, hail, and everything else that falls from the sky. 

It leaves behind the heaviest, thickest, most earthy, and bitter parts. It is for this same reason that human urine is bitter; the action of the sun over the sea is similar to the way <term>red bile</term> <note>original: *lionn ruad* — in medieval medicine, this refers to choler, one of the four humors thought to regulate the body. Like the sun, it was considered "hot and dry."</note> acts upon urine, filtering it and extracting the finer elements from it.

This same process explains why water becomes bitter when it is boiled for a long time: the heat of the fire extracts the finer, sweeter parts of the water. When the salty water of the sea is heavily boiled over a fire, or evaporated by the sun in hot climates, it calcifies <note>original: *calcaidh* — to turn to lime or harden.</note> and hardens, transforming into a substance like earth; this is the salt that we use. This process is driven by the intense heat of the fire or the sun, which draws away all the fine elements; once filtered and hardened <note>The original text uses *cruthugad* (creation/shaping), but scholars suggest *cruadugad* (hardening) was intended here.</note>, the substance takes on a physical, earthy nature.

At times, sweet waters—especially the water in rivers—are bound by the intensity of the cold, turning into ice. However, the natural heat within the sea, combined with its vast nature <note>original: *comnaige* — literally "dwelling" or "residing," referring to the sea's stable body of water.</note>, prevents the cold from seizing it in this way. As the <term>philosophers</term> <note>A reference to natural philosophers like Aristotle, whose theories on the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) and their qualities (hot, cold, wet, dry) form the basis of this text.</note> say, it is the nature of cold to bind everything it touches, while it is the nature of heat to dissolve everything that is bound.
