<margin>Chapter 9</margin>
The volume of rain determines the flow of the streams, and it is because of this rainfall that wells either fill up or run dry.

The saltiness of the sea is caused by its great age, the constant crashing of waves against the stones, and the fact that the sun's path is always directly above it. The sun's heat draws the most volatile <note>original: "volatile (?)" — likely translating the Irish *luaimnech*, meaning "shifting," "fickle," or "light." In this context, it refers to the water most easily evaporated.</note> and sweetest parts of the seawater up into the clouds. From that moisture, dew, rain, snow, hail, and every other atmospheric phenomenon are created. This process leaves behind the heaviest, most solid, most material, and most bitter <note>original: "sour" — the author uses "sour" or "bitter" to describe the salty residue left after evaporation.</note> portions. By a similar natural process, human urine is bitter; the same action performed by the sun upon the sea is performed by <term>bile</term> <note>original: *lionn ruadh* — "red bile" or choler, one of the four bodily humors. Medieval medicine taught that this "hot and dry" humor filtered the blood and produced urine.</note> upon the urine, as it strains the liquid and extracts the lighter elements from it.

For this same reason, water that is boiled for a long time becomes bitter, as the heat of the fire evaporates <note>original: "vapourises (?)" — translating the process of turning liquid to steam.</note> its sweetest parts. When salty seawater is boiled extensively over a fire, or by the sun in warm countries, it becomes condensed and solidified, taking on the qualities of earth; this is the salt we use. This effect is produced by excessive boiling—caused either by fire or the sun—which evaporates the water. The remaining elements are thus filtered and become solid, converting into an earth-like substance because of their density. 

Sometimes fresh water, particularly in rivers, is "bound" by intense cold and converted into ice. However, the natural heat within the sea, and the fact that it is a vast, deep body <note>The original text says "the fact that it is still," which may refer to its massive, stable volume or internal heat, as opposed to the shallow, easily cooled water of rivers.</note>, does not allow it to be frozen by the cold. As the philosophers <note>referring to classical authorities like Aristotle, whose *Meteorologica* influenced medieval views on the elements.</note> say, it is the nature of cold to bind everything that flows, and the nature of heat to dissolve every bound thing.

<summary>This page explains the hydrological cycle and the origins of sea salt through the lens of medieval science, comparing the sun's evaporation of the sea to the biological functions of the human body.</summary>

<margin>Chapter 10</margin>
10. **Concerning the two burning mountains** <note>original: *Don da cnoc ar lasadh*</note>—namely, Etna in Sicily and Vesuvius <note>original: *Ueranes*; likely referring to Vesuvius or the island of Vulcano</note> in Italy <note>original: *Apaile*; likely Apulia or Italy generally</note>, which is a place rich in sulfur.

**I say that the cause of this fire is, and so on.** <note>original Latin: *Dico quod occacio huius ignis est etcetera*</note> I say that the abundance of sulfur is the material for the fire that burns continually. It works in this way: when the fire begins to act within the veins of sulfur beneath the earth, it does not stop constantly burning the sulfur and the ground before it until they are consumed.

In this way, the fire creates holes and hollows within the earth. When the sulfur that exists there naturally is exhausted, it grows back again. When it has grown back, the fire turns and burns it once more. This cycle—the growth of the sulfur and the burning of the fire—continues perpetually. As the flame rises out of the mountain, it casts out many masses and chunks of fire <note>original: *caeraib 7 da caebaib teined* — literally "balls and clods of fire," describing volcanic bombs or lava fragments</note> which come from the substance of the sulfur. These gather together in one place and form mountains. 

A great and terrifying sound is often heard as the wind enters those conduits <note>original: *fedanaib* — pipes or vents</note> and blows along with the flame as it exits. Furthermore, the waters produced from these fiery places are hot; for, as we have said, waters take on the qualities of the place from which they originate.

### *Concerning the two burning mountains, Etna in Sicily and Vesuvius in Apulia, in the sulfurous region.*

10. **I say that the cause of this fire is, and so on.** <note>original Latin: *Dico quod occasio huius ignis est, etc.*</note> I declare that the abundance of sulfur is the cause of the fire that burns constantly. It works like this: when the fire begins to act within the veins of sulfur beneath the earth, it continues to burn the sulfur and the ground ahead of it so that it cannot be extinguished.

Consequently, it creates holes and crevices within the earth as it moves. When the sulfur that is naturally there is used up, it grows back again. When it regrows, the fire returns and burns it once more. This cycle—the growth of the sulfur and the burning of the fire—is always increasing. As the flame rises, it throws up many balls and masses of fire <note>original: *caeraib 7 da caebaib teined* — these are volcanic bombs or fragments of molten rock</note> which come from the substance of the sulfur; these collect in one area and form mountains.

A great and terrifying sound is often heard from the wind entering those hollows and blowing along with the flame as it emerges. The waters produced from these fiery places are hot because, as I have mentioned, waters take on the characteristics <note>original: "accident"; in medieval philosophy, an "accident" is a property or quality of a thing</note> of the place from which they originate.

...so that the moon is at the central point of its orbit <note>original: *cercailli* — circle or orbit</note> beneath the earth. From that point again, it does not stop constantly ebbing <note>original: *tragadh*</note> until it reaches the eastern point of its orbit; then it begins flowing <note>original: *linad* — the rising tide</note> once more, just as it did before.

And so, according to the rising and setting of the moon, the sea never stops flowing and ebbing. When the moon is in conjunction <note>original: *enceime* — likely from the Latin *en-ceima*, meaning in the same degree or sign</note> with the sun, that is when its light is greatest and most intense—that is, at the beginning of every month—and that is when the ebbing and flowing of the sea is at its highest. The second time the ebbing and flowing of the sea is at its greatest is in the middle of every month when the moon has full light near us <note>original: *laca rinne* / *laimh rinde*</note>; for it is then that the light of the sun is reflected <note>original: *frithbuailter* — struck back</note> down from the moon toward the sea, and that action causes the ebbing and flowing of the sea.

And it is in this way that the moon demonstrates these same effects in the marrow, the brain, and the blood of humans <gloss>of the person</gloss>; for those three things increase and decrease at the beginning and the middle of every month according to the course of the moon. Therefore, diseases of the bad blood, such as boils and many other things, do not occur except at the beginning and the middle of every month.

Furthermore, the actions of the moon are also evident by the increase and decrease in cucumbers and gourds <note>original: *cucurbitaibh*</note>, and in every single thing where moisture dominates according to the course of the moon. This is the natural cause of that: the moon has dominion over moist things—and specifically over the water of the sea—just as a magnet <note>original: *adhamas* — historically "adamant," used here to mean a lodestone</note> has over iron. For just as a magnet draws the iron to it, so the moon draws the water of the sea, and that is called the flowing of the sea. When it ceases that drawing, the sea returns to its own place from the west, and that is called ebbing.

This ebbing and flowing is most visible in the east of the world and in the west, in the Red Sea or in the Sea of...

Thus, according to the rising and setting of the moon, the sea never ceases flowing and ebbing. When the moon is in the same degree as the sun <note>This refers to the New Moon, or conjunction</note>, then its influence is greatest and strongest—that is, at the beginning of each month—and it is then that the ebb and flow of the tide are at their greatest. In the second course of the moon's cycle, the ebb and flow of the tide are greatest in the middle of every month when the moon is at its full light and facing us; for it is then that the light of the sun is reflected <note>original: frithbuailter — "struck back"</note> down from the moon toward the sea, which brings about the ebb and flow of the tide.

In the same way, the moon reveals these same effects in the marrow, brain, and blood of men, because those three things increase and decrease at the beginning and middle of every month according to the course of the moon. Accordingly, diseases caused by "bad blood," such as boils and many other conditions, tend to occur only at the beginning and middle of every month.

The actions of the moon are evident again through the increase and decrease in cucumbers, gourds, and in every thing where moisture is the dominant element, all according to the course of the moon. The natural cause of this is that the moon controls all moist things—and particularly the water of the sea—just as a <term>lodestone</term> <note>A naturally magnetized mineral; the author uses it as a metaphor for gravity/attraction</note> attracts iron. For just as a lodestone attracts iron to itself, in the same way, the moon attracts the water of the sea, and that is called the "flow" of the tide. When that attraction ceases, the tide turns back to its original position, and that is called the "ebb."

This ebbing and flowing are more visible in the eastern and western parts of the world than in the Red Sea or in the African [coast]...

...Africa or in the other seas that come from the great ocean; for some of them move in a direct path <note>original: *sibal direach*</note> westward, and others flow directly eastward through the power of the moon's influence <note>original: *tre brig oibrigthi an re* — "through the force of the moon's operation"</note> above them. And to demonstrate the moon's influence on the ebbing and flowing of the sea, I will provide a geometric figure <term>geometric figure</term> <note>original: *fidair iomitre* — a diagram used for scientific explanation</note> here below. First, I will draw a circular figure of the earth divided into four equal parts, and I will write these four letters in their correct positions around those four parts: **A**, **B**, **C**, and **D**. Around the earth, I will draw a circle representing the moon's orbit. I will place **E** in the east, **F** at the highest point <note>original: *medon mullaid* — the zenith or "middle-top"</note>, **G** in the west, and **H** at the lowest point <note>original: *medon ictar* — the nadir or "middle-bottom"</note>. I will darken half of the sea around the earth and leave the other half dry and bright.

Therefore, when the moon is in the east of its own orbit at point **E**, directly opposite **A**, the sea begins to flood. It does not cease its constant flooding until the moon reaches point **F**, which is opposite **B**. At that point, the sea begins to ebb, and it does not stop ebbing until the moon reaches point **G**, which is opposite **C**. It then begins to flood again until the moon reaches point **H**, opposite **D**, and it continues to ebb once more until the moon returns to point **A**.

## 12. "CERTAIN UNLEARNED PEOPLE ARE ACCUSTOMED TO ASSERT," ETC. <note>original Latin: *Solent quidam imperiti asserere etcetera*</note>

That is to say: Some unlearned people <note>The Irish text uses *eolcaib* (learned), but the manuscript note suggests it should be *haineolchaib* (unlearned/ignorant)</note> say that the flooding of the River Nile comes from great rains that fall in distant lands. They claim that as the river fills, it overflows <note>original: *sceighidh* — "vomits" or "spews"</note> across the land of Egypt. They believe that what rain does for other nations, the water of the River Nile does for the Egyptians. However, we say that they have neither proof <note>original: *ardhamaint* — argument or fundamental evidence</note> nor reason to prove these things, but only their own opinion.

<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>

Chapter 12.

The sea, or other waters that originate from the Great Ocean <note>original: "Great Ocean," likely referring to the Atlantic</note>, behave differently; some of these follow a straight course directly westward, while in others the tide flows directly eastward through the power and influence of the moon above them. Consequently, the flow or ebb of the tide is not always visible on the shores in those specific locations.

To explain the moon’s influence on the ebb and flow of the tide, I will provide a geometrical diagram below. First, I will draw a circular figure of the Earth and divide it into four equal parts, labeling these four divisions with the letters A, B, C, and D. Around the Earth, I will draw a circle representing the sphere of the moon, placing E in the east, F at the center of the top, G in the west, and H at the center of the lower part. I will shade half of the globe to represent the sea and leave the other half white to represent dry land. Thus, when the moon is in the east of its own circle at point E, exactly opposite A, the tide begins to rise. It continues to rise steadily until the moon reaches point F, which is opposite D. At that point, the tide begins to ebb and continues to fall steadily until the moon reaches point G, which is opposite C. The tide then rises again until the moon reaches point H, opposite D, and it ebbs once more until the moon arrives back opposite A.

### 12. CERTAIN IGNORANT PEOPLE ARE ACCUSTOMED TO ASSERT, ETC.
<note>original: "SOLENT QUIDAM IMPERITI ASSERERE, ETCETERA"</note>

That is to say, some unlearned people claim that the flooding of the River Nile is caused by heavy rains falling in distant lands. They argue that as the river fills, it overflows throughout the land of Egypt, and that the water of the Nile provides for the Egyptians what rain provides for other nations. I maintain, however, that they have no logical argument or evidence to prove this assertion, aside from a single theory:

<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>

...alone, for just as they see other rivers of the world filled by rainfall, they believe that the River Nile fills in the same way.

We shall now prove that this opinion is false. For if rainfall were the cause of the Nile’s flooding, as they have said, the river would fill at no specific time <note>original: "aimsir airithe"</note>, but rather whenever it rained heavily throughout the year, just as other rivers are filled. And it is evident to everyone who sees it that this river does not fill except at a specific time of the year, namely in the month of August. 

When it rains excessively in a certain region near Egypt, the river receives a small increase from that rain. However, rain that falls in lands far from Egypt does not increase the Nile's flow because of the great distance of the river's source from Egypt and the extreme dryness of the earth. Therefore, no matter when it rains—whether in spring, summer, or any other time—the very dry, sandy land and the scorching heat of the sun soak up the rainwater and do not allow it to reach the river. Furthermore, even when the river's channel might be filled by excessive rainfall creating large pools, the heat of the earth around the river is so great that the ground soaks them up as quickly as they form. It is rare for "accidental water" <note>original: "tuisci aicideach," a term used here to describe external water like rain rather than the river's natural source</note> to remain in that river, and it is usually filled from bank to bank by its own natural waters.

To prove this, if a trench were made two hundred or three hundred miles long through dry land, even if a great deal of water were poured into one end of it, the earth would soak it all up before a single drop reached the other end. In the same way, the scorched, hot land surrounding the Nile soaks up the waters that fall around it before they reach Egypt; therefore, rainfall is not the cause of the Nile’s flooding.

Another thing that proves the same point is what I have heard from the ancient authorities <note>original: "arsantaib," referring to elders or historical sources</note> themselves: that the Egyptians once believed the Nile would not fill unless a maiden...

> “In the name of the merciful Lord, from Omar, King of Egypt. Long life and health to the River Nile. ...others blowing around it, thicken and solidify that air, so that it is only with a very great storm that clouds or rain pass through the boundary <note>original: "coicrich"; a border or territory, here used for atmospheric layers</note> of that air. And on the rare occasions they do pass through, there is terrifying thunder, a very great wind, and massive lightning which kills the livestock of Egypt. 

It is the nature of common air that when it heats up, it tends to expand and spread; and when it cools, it gathers together, thickens, and draws everything similar to itself toward it. Sea air is colder at night than during the day; therefore, when the sun rises to its midday position, the heat of the sun causes the air to expand and spread. From that hour until midnight, the wind blows from the west into the mouths of the rivers that flow into the sea in the west. It strikes against the rivers and drives them back against their will, preventing them from flowing into the sea until the cold of the night takes away the power of the sun’s heat. Thus, the resistance to the rivers comes from the heat of the air, and the flowing of the water comes from the cold of the air.

Furthermore, since sea air is hotter by day than by night, and because this change in the air is caused by the sun being near to or far from us, it follows that during the time of year when it is closest to us—that is, in summer—the sea air is at its hottest. For this reason, from the first day of May until the time the sun reaches the September equinox <note>original: "neccinocsium"; a loanword from the Latin 'aequinoctium'</note>, the sea wind blows eastward toward Egypt over the River Nile, causing the air of Egypt to move and be pushed from its place.

Because that air is dry, heavy, and difficult to move, it resists the wind and does not leave its own place. The wind, which blows continuously and finds no other path, forces the River Nile back against its will and does not let a single drop of it reach the sea. [The wind pushes] the sand of the sea into the mouth of the River Nile despite its current. At that time, the wind's power begins to fade and the sun leaves its position directly above the sea, gradually lowering toward the southern part of the world. Since the river water is no longer held back by the wind as it was before, it breaks through the sandbar <note>original: "sliab gainimh" (mountain of sand)</note> and flows onward into the sea, leaving the land of Egypt. It is then that the Egyptians perform their plowing and sowing <note>original: "treabad ⁊ cur"</note>, as they are certain the river will not hinder them again until the same time the following year. Thus, it is clear that they would often be hindered much earlier than that time if the flooding of the Nile were caused by rains <note>The author argues that if rain caused the flood, it would be unpredictable, whereas the wind-driven flood follows a reliable solar schedule</note>.

The same filling occurs in the rivers of other regions that flow westward into the sea, though not to the same extent as the River Nile. This is because the air there is weak and easily moved; it does not resist the wind but moves in whatever direction it is carried in those other regions. For this reason, the great scale of the Nile's flood is not found in any other river in those lands. May He who created the River Nile be blessed forever and ever <note>original: "in secla cecloram" (in saecula saeculorum)</note>.

## 13. I SAY TRULY AS BEFORE, ETCETERA. 
<note>original: "Dico sicut superius veraciter etcetera"</note>

These two direct movements belong to the four elements <note>original: "duilib"</note> and to everything composed of them. Therefore, since the firmament <note>The "firmamint" refers to the celestial spheres or the heavens</note> moves neither away from the center nor toward the center, it is understood to be neither heavy nor...

For that reason, no other river in other countries is as wide as the River Nile during its flood; and may He who created the River Nile be blessed forever, world without end <note>original Latin: "in saecula saeculorum"</note>.

13. I TRULY DECLARE AS I DID ABOVE, AND SO FORTH <note>original Latin: "DICO SICUT SUPERIUS UERACITER, ETCETERA"</note>. I declare truly, as I stated at the beginning, that "light bodies" are those that move from the center toward the surrounding circle, and that "heavy bodies" are those that move from that circle toward the center. The four elements <note>Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note> and everything that is composed of them possess these two direct motions. Consequently, since the firmament <term>firmament: the sphere of the stars or the heavens</term> does not move from the center or toward the center...

<meta>This page continues Chapter 13, which discusses the properties of the firmament (the heavens) in contrast to the four elements. The previous page established the concept of natural motion toward or away from the center of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>

...that it is not light. For if it were heavy, it would move toward the center, and if it were light, it would move away from the center. Since it does neither of these, it follows⁵ that it is neither hot nor cold. This is because lightness is found in hot bodies, such as fire; and since the firmament is not light (as we have proved), it must be that there is no heat in it. And since coldness is found in heavy bodies, such as the earth, and the firmament is not heavy, it must be that there is no cold in it.⁶

It is also understood from this that the firmament is neither wet nor dry.⁷ For wetness⁸ causes a lack of speed in the body where it exists, as it does in the air (which moves away from the center) and in the water (which moves toward the center). In the same way, dryness⁹ produces speed in the bodies in which it exists, as it does in fire (which moves away from the center) and in the earth (which moves toward the center).

And since there is neither speed nor slowness in the firmament, there has never been a time when it was faster or slower than at any other time since the moment God created it.¹⁰ Instead, it has one identical, steady, and eternal¹¹ motion, which it has had, currently has, and will have until the end of the world. It is for this reason that there is no wetness or dryness in it from which speed or slowness could arise. Consequently, the sphere of the high stars <note>original: "nairdrinnach" — referring to the fixed stars or constellations</note> and the seven spheres of the seven planets possess this same motion, which will continue until the end of the world.

There are some among the unlearned¹² who are ignorant¹³ of God’s works, saying that the firmament was mixed¹⁴ from the four elements <note>original: "ceithre duilib"</note>. This is clearly against the truth, for reason does not allow the firmament to be mixed¹⁵ from them, because the nature of the four elements is different in every respect from the nature of the firmament. For the nature of the four elements, as we have said, is that some of them move toward the center and others move away from the center. Furthermore, that motion is temporary <note>original: "haimserda" — finite or belonging to time rather than eternity</note>, being faster at the end than at the beginning. They also stay permanently in their own places and never leave those places¹⁶ except by force.

### Chapter 13 (continued)

Let it be understood that the firmament is neither heavy nor light. This is because if it were heavy, it would move toward the center of the universe, and if it were light, it would move away from the center. Since we understand it to be neither one nor the other, we must also assume it to be neither hot nor cold. Lightness is found in hot bodies like fire; since the firmament is not light, as we have proved, there cannot be heat in it. Similarly, cold is found in heavy bodies like the earth; since the firmament is not heavy, there cannot be cold in it.

Let it also be understood from this that the firmament is neither wet nor dry. Wetness <note>In medieval science, "wetness" or moisture was associated with flexibility and resistance to motion.</note> causes slowness <note>original: "tardiness"</note> in the body in which it exists, as it does in air (which moves away from the center) and in water (which moves toward the center). In contrast, dryness causes speed <note>original: "velocity"</note> in the bodies in which it exists, as it does in fire (which moves away from the center) and in earth (which moves toward the center). 

Since there is neither unnatural speed nor slowness in the firmament, it is never faster or slower at one time than at another. Since God created it, it had, has, and will have one identical, steady, and constant motion until the end of the world. Consequently, there is no wetness or dryness in it that could cause variations in speed. This same principle applies to the motion of the sphere of the constellations <note>original: "sphere of the nairdrinnach" — the "high stars" or fixed stars.</note> and the seven spheres of the seven planets; they have had, and will have, this same constant motion until the end of the world.

There are some unlearned men, uninformed regarding the works of God, who claim that the firmament was composed of the <term>four elements</term> <note>The four elements are Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>. This is clearly contrary to the truth. Because the four elements possess a nature entirely different from the nature of the firmament, reason cannot accept that the firmament could be made of them. As I said before, it is the nature of the four elements that some move toward the center and others move away from the center. Furthermore, their motion occurs at intervals and is slower at the end than at the beginning. They are also stationary in their own natural places and never leave them...

<summary>This page argues that the firmament is a "fifth element" (quintessence) that is neither hot, cold, wet, nor dry, characterized by its perfectly constant and eternal motion. It refutes the idea that the heavens are made of the same four elements found on Earth.</summary>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter 13 that the firmament (the heavens) is fundamentally different from the four earthly elements. The previous page established that the firmament lacks weight, heat, or moisture because its motion is perfectly circular and unchanging.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>

For every element within the four elements <note>original: *cetharduil*</note> is equal to the others in age and in its natural resting place.

Furthermore, there are many qualities within the four elements, namely: heat and cold, moisture and dryness, lightness and heaviness, and speed and slowness. The nature of the firmament is the exact opposite of those natures. For, in contrast to the straight [linear] motion of the four elements, there is the circular motion of the firmament. Against the temporary nature of earthly movement, there is the eternity of the firmament’s movement. And against the variable speed or slowness of earthly movement, there is an eternal constancy <note>original: *siraidacht maille*, literally "eternal slowness" or "steadiness"</note> in the movement of the firmament. 

In contrast to the four elements, which remain forever in their own natural places, the firmament has a perpetual, natural movement within its own place. Just as the four elements and their stability are linked, so too are the firmament and its motion linked. The firmament is devoid of every quality belonging to the four elements; for there is no heat or cold, no moisture or dryness, no lightness or heaviness, and no speed or slowness within it.

Unlike the components of the four elements, the firmament is never corrupted <note>original: *truaillidter*, meaning decayed or broken down</note> or changed. Instead, it has remained as it was from the beginning of the world: firm, enclosed, incorruptible, and indissoluble. It will remain so until the end ordained for it by the Creator <note>original: *tusmiteoir*, literally "progenitor" or "begetter"</note>. Therefore, since the nature of the four elements and the nature of the firmament are directly opposed to one another, it is contrary to reason and nature to claim that the firmament was composed <note>original: *comasced*, mixed or compounded</note> from the four elements.

There is another reason to oppose that same opinion: anything composed of contrary elements that work against one another and corrupt each other will eventually be completely destroyed. This is seen in humans, animals, and everything else composed of the four elements. In these things, the contrary qualities of the elements struggle to destroy each other; when one quality gains dominance <note>original: *tigernas*, literally "lordship"</note> over the others, the composite thing is entirely destroyed.

### 14. When God Created the Firmament, etc. <note>original: *QUANDO DEUS FIRMAMENTUM CREAUIT ETCETERA*</note> 

That is to say, when God created the firmament, He ordained for it a perfect and complete motion that neither increases nor diminishes with age. For the firmament completes its circuit every twenty-four hours, unerringly and without fail; and those twenty-four hours constitute a night and a day.

God also created the <term>sphere of the sun</term> <note>In medieval cosmology, the sun was believed to be carried around the Earth within its own transparent, rotating sphere.</note> to reveal the day and the night, their specific qualities, and their lengthening and shortening. He did this to demonstrate celestial balance and the variation in the growth and ebbing <note>original: *traighti* — literally "receding," like a tide</note> of heat and cold in various places, so that living creatures might propagate and the orderly arrangement of the world might be fulfilled. 

For if the firmament and the sun moved any faster than they do, the days and the nights would be shortened. There would not be enough time for the sun to complete its natural operations, and earthly creatures would be hindered from growing. Conversely, if they moved more slowly than they do, the day and night would be lengthened. In that case, the sun would remain above the earth for too long; it would scorch and dry up the face of the ground, preventing anything from growing through the soil. Furthermore, people would not be able to inhabit the southern regions of the world as they do now, for a span of six degrees...

Thus, since no sign of such dissolution is, has been, or ever will be observed, it must be understood that the firmament is not composed of the four elements. Therefore, may He who ordered it in such a manner be blessed by everyone who beholds it.

### 14. When God Created the Firmament, etc. <note>original: *QUANDO DEUS FIRMAMENTUM CREAUIT, ETCETERA*</note> 

When God created the <term>firmament</term> <note>The sphere of the stars that was believed to revolve around the Earth.</note>, He ordained for it a perfectly complete motion that neither increases nor decreases. Within twenty-four hours, the firmament completes its course without error or deviation; and those twenty-four hours constitute a day and a night.

God also created the sphere of the sun to reveal the day and the night—their nature, their length, and their shortness—and to show the regularity of the heavens and the variations of heat and cold in different places. This was done to enable living creatures to reproduce and to fulfill the orderly arrangement of the world. If the firmament and the sun moved more quickly than they do, the days and nights would be shortened, the sun would not have time to fulfill its functions, and earthly creatures would be unable to grow. If they moved more slowly than they do, night and day would be lengthened; consequently, because the sun would remain over the earth for too long, it would scorch and dry out the surface of the earth, preventing anything from growing in the soil. Thus, people could not live in the southern part of the world, as they do now at a distance of sixteen degrees

...[seven] degrees $^{18}$ <note>original: *secht ceim*; the main text says "deg" but variant 18 clarifies it as seven degrees.</note> inside the <term>central line of the world</term> <note>The Equator.</note>, because the constant presence of the sun would make that place uninhabitable $^{19}$. Likewise, around the end of sixty-six degrees $^{20}$ of the <term>firmament</term> northward from that same line, the place beneath them would be $^{21}$ uninhabitable $^{22}$ due to the cold of the very long nights.

And the region from the end of those sixty-six degrees is uninhabitable because of extreme $^{23}$ cold as far as the place that is directly within the path $^{24}$ of the sun. For when the sun inclines to the southern $^{25}$ side of the world, the cold increases so much in the northern side that animals cannot live $^{27}$ there and trees do not grow.

Likewise, on the outer side $^{28}$ of the line we mentioned to the south, people and animals do not live there because of excessive heat. It is in the region $^{30}$ closest to that line on the inner side that the <term>black men</term> <note>original: *fir gorma* (literally "blue men"). This was the standard Early Modern Irish term for Sub-Saharan Africans, as "gorm" described dark or dusk-colored skin.</note> live, who are darkened by the extreme heat of the sun.

We said above $^{31}$ that there is no quality among the opposing qualities—from which all corruption and dissolution comes—within the firmament. Thus, they have no opposition or [irregular] movement, for the movement of every body in the world must be circular, straight, or a mixture of the two, such as the movement of a cart.

The circular and straight movements are unmixed <note>In Aristotelian physics, "simple" or unmixed motion was considered more perfect than "composite" or mixed motion.</note>. Therefore, every body, whether composite or non-composite, moves in either a circular or a straight way. <term>Circular movement</term> is the movement that travels like a circle $^{33}$ around a center. <term>Straight movement</term> is the movement that goes from the center $^{34}$ above to the center below. These three movements are simple movements, but circular movement is simpler than the other two movements, because the body that moves in that way is simpler than any other body.

And those $^{35}$ two movements that go toward the center and away from the center $^{36}$ are composite compared to the movement...

### Chapter 14

...away from the equator, because if the sun stayed there too long, it would make that place uninhabitable. Similarly, at approximately the end of sixty-six degrees of the <term>firmament</term> <note>The heavens or the sphere of the stars.</note> northward from that same line, the region beneath them would be uninhabitable because of the cold of the very long nights. The region extending from the end of those sixty-six degrees is uninhabitable due to excessive cold, reaching even as far as the region that is directly under the sun’s path. <note>original: * (Sic.) The author likely means the region furthest from the sun's path, as the area "under the sun's course" (the tropics) is hot, not cold.</note> For when the sun moves toward the southern side of the world, the cold increases so much on the northern side that animals cannot live there and trees do not grow. Likewise, men and animals cannot live south of the previously mentioned line because of excessive heat; in the land closest to that line on the inner side are the <term>black people</term> <note>original: "negroes." This refers to the "fir gorma" (blue men/black men) mentioned in the Irish source text.</note>, who are darkened by the extreme heat of the sun.

I mentioned above that there are no "opposite qualities" <note>In Aristotelian physics, the four elements have opposing qualities (hot/cold, wet/dry) which lead to change and decay; the heavens were thought to be made of a fifth element, the ether, which had no opposites and was therefore eternal.</note> in the firmament from which all <term>corruption and dissolution</term> <note>Decay and the breaking down of physical matter.</note> result. Consequently, the celestial bodies have no opposing motion, since the movement of every body in the universe must be circular, vertical, or a compound of both—much like the motion of a wagon wheel.

Circular and vertical motions are "simple" motions; consequently, every body, whether it is a simple element or a compound substance, moves either in a circle or vertically. Circular motion is defined as movement that travels in a circle around a center point. Vertical motion <note>Modern: rectilinear or straight-line motion.</note> is movement that travels either upward from a center or downward toward a center. These three motions <note>Circular, upward, and downward.</note> are simple motions, and circular motion is simpler than the other two because the body that moves in this way <note>The celestial firmament.</note> is simpler than any other body.

Those two motions that move toward and away from the center are considered "compound" when compared to circular motion, and they...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the nature of the elements and celestial bodies, moving from the habitability of the Earth to the nature of motion itself.</meta>

<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>
<page-num>66</page-num>

Circular motion is mixed within the <term>four elements</term> <note>original: *cetharduil*; the four physical elements of earth, water, air, and fire.</note>, for in truth, there is no mixed movement there except for that specific motion. Although each of the four elemental bodies is composed of two qualities, they are considered unmixed when viewed in relation to the bodies created from them. Circular motion is unique because it is natural for it to be a <term>perfect motion</term> <note>In Aristotelian physics, circular motion was considered "perfect" because it is continuous and has no beginning or end, unlike straight-line motion.</note>, having neither beginning nor end; its travel does not aim for a state of rest, nor does it turn back upon itself. 

It is not so for <term>direct motion</term> <note>original: *gluasacht direc*; rectilinear or straight-line motion.</note>, which moves toward its destination or is constrained against the nature of the bodies. For when a certain element is outside of its own natural place, it moves suddenly back toward its place again and stays there naturally; because of this, such motion has both a beginning and an end. Anything that has a beginning and an end is an imperfect thing, and therefore, direct motion is an <term>imperfect motion</term>. Just as everything perfect is prior, greater, more enduring, and more noble than anything imperfect, circular motion surpasses direct motion in these same qualities.

**Further proof of this same point.** The <term>philosophers</term> <note>Refers to classical authorities, primarily Aristotle, whose "Physics" and "On the Heavens" shaped medieval science.</note> say that a motion which is <term>forced</term> <note>original: *ainneonac*; also translated as "violent motion," meaning motion against an object's natural tendency.</note> for one thing is natural for another. For example, the upward movement of fire and the downward movement of earth are natural, but to reverse them—sending earth up or fire down—is forced. 

Since everything forced is <term>accidental</term> <note>original: *aicideach*; a property that is not essential to a thing’s nature.</note> and everything natural is <term>substantial</term> <note>original: *sustainit*; relating to the core essence or "substance" of a thing.</note>, and since accident and substance are contrary to each other, so too are forced motion and natural motion. Not only that, but positions such as below and above, right and left, and before and behind are all contrary to one another. Since any thing that lacks contrariety is nobler than a thing that possesses it, circular motion—which has no contrary—is nobler than every other motion mentioned.

**Another proof of the same thing:** everything that moves naturally in a direct motion can be...

### Chapter 14

The motions of the heavens are simple compared to the motion of things composed of the **four elements** <note>Earth, water, air, and fire.</note>, because, in reality, there is no **complex motion** <note>original: "compound motion"; motion that combines different directions or qualities.</note> but that one. Though each of the four elemental bodies is composed of two qualities, they are simple compared to the physical bodies that are made from them. Thus, circular motion—because it is by nature a **perfect motion** <note>In Aristotelian physics, circular motion is "perfect" because it is continuous, uniform, and has no beginning or end.</note> without beginning or end—does not allow for being stationary or for turning back. 

It is not so with **vertical motion** <note>original: "vertical motion"; this refers to rectilinear or straight-line motion, such as an object falling down or smoke rising up.</note>, which sometimes moves independently of the bodies; for when an element is outside of its own natural position, it moves suddenly back towards its own place and remains there naturally. Thus, that motion has a beginning and an end, and anything that has a beginning and an end is imperfect; consequently, vertical motion is an **imperfect motion**. Just as every perfect thing is superior to, greater, more permanent, and more noble than every imperfect thing, in the same way, circular motion surpasses vertical motion to the same degree.

**Further proof of the same point:** the **philosophers** <note>Refers to classical authorities like Aristotle.</note> declare that the motion that is **forced** <note>original: "foreign"; motion caused by an external power rather than an object's internal nature.</note> upon one thing is natural to another—for instance, the upward motion of fire and of earth, or the downward motion of both. As every non-essential thing is an **accident** <note>original: "accidental"; in medieval philosophy, an "accident" is a characteristic that does not affect the essential nature of a substance.</note>, and every essential natural thing is a **substance**, and as the accident and the substance are opposite to each other, in the same way, forced motion and natural motion are opposite to each other. Not only that, but things that are below and above, right and left, before and behind, are contrary to one another. And as everything which lacks **opposition** <note>original: "contrariety"; the state of having an opposite direction or quality.</note> is nobler than that which has it, in the same way, circular motion—which has no opposition—is superior to every other motion I have mentioned.

**Additional proof of the same thing:** everything that moves naturally with a vertical motion can be moved...

...motion is forced and against nature, but it is not so with circular motion. For the circular course that was ordained for it at the beginning of the world was, is, and shall be unchanging, without shifting from one side to the other, maintaining that state forever. Therefore, it is clear that circular motion is more ancient and enduring than any other motion.

And although there is a distinct motion in each of the spheres <note>original: "rothaibh" (wheels); a common medieval metaphor for the celestial spheres.</note> of the firmament, they all move together without conflict. If there were conflict in the firmament, as there is in the elemental bodies <note>The four elements: earth, water, air, and fire, which are in constant flux on Earth.</note>, everything that operates within them would produce its own likeness in the firmament. Thus, a stronger body in the firmament would change a weaker body into its own nature; consequently, we would see the planets, the high stars, and the other stars appearing larger or smaller than one another at different times. This is not seen now, nor has it ever been seen, nor will it ever be seen. Therefore, it is clear from this that those changes—that is, conflict—do not exist in the firmament.

The great, subtle, and solid body <note>Refers to the "crystalline" nature of the heavens in medieval cosmology, believed to be made of a fifth element (quintessence) that is incorruptible.</note> in the firmament is a round circle, like a ball rotating around its own center. Its midpoint remains in one single place forever, while it moves perpetually. It is certain that this motion is uniform, since it has never stopped for even a moment, and it never moves faster or slower than itself. This is also how the seven circles of the seven planets are arranged: namely, **Luna** (the Moon), **Mercury**, **Venus**, **Sol** (the Sun), **Mars**, **Jupiter**, and **Saturn**; as well as the circle of the fixed stars and the circle of the twelve signs <note>The twelve signs of the Zodiac.</note>, which is the circle we previously called the firmament. These ten circles have existed in this way from the hour God created them, and they shall remain so forever, for as long as He wishes them to be so.

...is not by force or contrary to nature. Although each of the spheres of the heavens <note>original: "firmament"</note> has a separate motion, they all move together without opposition. If there were conflict in the heavens as there is among the elemental bodies <note>The four elements—earth, water, air, and fire—which in medieval science were believed to be in a constant state of flux and "war" with one another.</note>, everything that conflict produces in those elements would cause a similar effect in the heavens. Thus, the strongest body in the heavens would change the weakest body into its own nature; similarly, we would see the planets, the constellations, and the other stars appearing larger at one time and smaller at another. This is not seen now, was never seen, and never will be seen. Thus, it is clear how those changes—that is, the conflict—in the heavens can be understood to be absent.

Thus, the grand, smooth, solid body in the heavens is a round sphere like a ball, rotating around its own center, with its center at a midpoint which remains forever in one place while the sphere is always moving. It is certain that this motion is uniform, since it has never made the slightest halt, and does not move swifter or slower at one time than another. Such are the seven spheres of the seven planets: the Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note>, Mercury, Venus, the Sun <note>original: "Sol"</note>, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, as well as the sphere of the fixed stars and the sphere of the twelve signs <note>The Zodiac</note>—that is, the sphere which we call the firmament. These ten spheres have existed in this way since the time God created them, and so they will remain forever, as long as He wishes them to be so.

And the unlearned <note>original: "haineolaidh"</note> say that the primary qualities of the elements and the qualities of compound things are derived from the twelve signs <note>the Zodiac signs</note> and the planets. They say again that unless the sun <note>original: "grian"</note> were hot in its own substance, it would not perform the operations of heat that we see in earthly things; for the effects it produces in earthly things are exactly the same as those produced by fire, which is hot in its own substance—namely, burning, drying, and blackening.

And they say again that unless the moon <note>original: "re"</note> were moist in itself, it would not produce moisture in earthly things. But we argue against this, saying that such an opinion is a scientific error and a falsehood. I say that the signs of the firmament are neither moist, nor hot, nor cold, nor dry, nor good, nor bad. Instead, it is from their conjunction with one another and from their motion that they produce those qualities in earthly things, even though those qualities do not exist within the heavenly bodies themselves; for they were all made from one single substance and one single matter <note>This refers to the "quintessence" or "aether," the fifth element believed to make up the heavens, which was distinct from the four earthly elements</note>. We shall demonstrate this more clearly in the following sections.

The unlearned <note>original: "uninformed"</note> say that the primary qualities of the elements, and the qualities of the things composed of them, are contained within the twelve signs of the Zodiac and in the planets. They declare that *Aries*, *Leo*, and *Sagittarius* are hot and dry, following the nature of fire; *Taurus*, *Virgo*, and *Capricorn* are cold and dry, following the nature of earth; *Gemini*, *Libra*, and *Aquarius* are hot and moist, following the nature of air; and *Cancer*, *Scorpio*, and *Pisces* are cold and moist, according to the nature of water.

They even claim that some of the planets are good and some are bad, and that some are favorable and lucky for good people, while others are unfavorable, hostile, and unlucky for bad people. They argue that if the *sun* were not hot in its own substance, it would not perform the functions of heat that we see in earthly <note>original: "terrestrial"</note> things; for the sun performs the very same functions on earth as fire does (which is hot in its own substance)—namely, burning, drying, and charring.

They say again that if the *moon* were not moist in itself, it would not produce moisture in earthly things. Against this, I declare that such a theory is scientifically incorrect and false. I maintain that the signs of the heavens <note>original: "firmament"</note> are neither moist, nor hot, nor cold, nor good, nor bad in themselves. Although it is from their combination and their motion that they produce those qualities, those properties are not actually present in the celestial bodies themselves because they were all made from one single substance and one material. I will explain this in more detail later.

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.

## 15. IT IS TO BE KNOWN, ETC.
<note>original Latin: "Sciendum est etcetera"</note>

That is to say, it is clear that the nature of physical bodies is not recognized by their tastes or their appearances <note>original: "blathaib," literally "flowers," but here referring to outward appearance or bloom</note>. For if they were recognized by their colors, all white bodies would have the same nature, and everything that shared a color or taste would be of the same nature. However, we see that snow, white lead <note>original: "puisdian," a pigment also known as ceruse</note>, and fresh cheese all have the same color, yet each of them has a distinct and separate nature. We also see that although parsley <note>original: "apium"</note> and aloes agree in their bitterness, they do not have the same nature; for one of them is hot and the other is cold. We say the same regarding smell. Therefore, the nature of physical bodies is recognized not by their colors, nor by their tastes, nor by their smells, nor by their texture, but by their positions and their motions.

The unlearned <note>Those without scientific training</note> say that the firmament <note>The sphere of the stars</note> changes, either in its parts or entirely within itself over a long period of time. They claim that even though this change is not obvious to us, it is like how we see a tower, or iron, or a body, or hyacinth <note>A precious stone</note>, and many other noble stones. They argue that as these things age and endure over time, their size, colors, taste, and smell change due to the length of time, even if that change is not clear to us as it is happening because of the vast amount of time it takes to occur.

We say to them in response that everything beneath the moon <note>The sublunary sphere, where earthly laws of physics apply</note> in the sphere of the four elements <term>four elements</term><note>The basic building blocks of the physical world: earth, air, fire, and water</note> undergoes change. There are two types of this change: total change and partial change. These two types of change concern growth and decay <note>original: "fasti ⁊ truailligti," meaning generation and corruption</note>. Total change is more obvious than partial change. And every body

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.

### Chapter 15. IT IS TO BE KNOWN, ETC.
<note>original Latin: "SCIENDUM EST, ETCETERA"</note>

It is evident that the natures of physical bodies are not known by their taste or by their odor. If they were recognized by their colors, all white bodies would have the same nature, and everything of the same color or taste would be of the same nature. We see that snow, white lead <note>original: "dough (?)"; the previous page identifies this as white lead or ceruse</note>, and fresh cheese all have the same color, although each of them has a different nature. We see again that, although parsley <note>original: "apium"</note> and aloes agree in their bitterness <note>original: "pungency"</note>, they are not of the same nature since one is sharp <note>interpreted as "hot" in the previous page</note> and the other is mild <note>interpreted as "cold" in the previous page</note>; and we say the same regarding smell. Consequently, the nature of physical bodies is known not by their taste, smell, or touch, but by their positions and motions.

The unlearned <note>Those lacking scientific or philosophical training</note> declare that the firmament <note>The sphere of the stars and planets</note> changes, either in its specific parts or in its entirety, over a long period of time. They claim this change is not apparent to us, just as we see gold, iron, the human body, hyacinth <note>A precious stone</note>, and many other noble stones becoming discolored with age and the passage of time. They argue these things change in size, color, taste, and smell over long durations, even though we are not aware of the change while it is in progress because of the great extent of time over which it occurs.

I say to them in answer that everything beneath the moon, within the sphere of the four elements <note>The terrestrial realm composed of Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>, undergoes change. That change is of two kinds—complete and partial—and these two changes involve growth and decay. Furthermore, complete change is more evident than partial change. Every body...

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding how physical changes are perceived. Having previously argued that celestial bodies are known by their motion rather than sensory qualities like taste or smell, the author now demonstrates that because the stars do not appear to change in size or position, the firmament itself must be stable and unchanging.</meta>

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.

...changes occur therein. This change <term>claechlodh</term> happens either in quantity <term>cainndigecht</term> or in quality <term>cailidecht</term>, and it is the actions of the body that reveal this change to us. For when a sick body is made healthy, or a healthy body becomes sick, it is the visible functions <note>original: "gnimharta" - deeds or actions</note> of that body that demonstrate the change to us. And so, when a heavy body is made light, the actions of that same body reveal the change to us. Likewise, when a slow body is made fast, or a fast body becomes slow, it is the speed or lack of speed of that body that demonstrates that movement <note>original: "gluasacht"</note> to us.

It is the same way with the firmament: if it were to increase or decrease in size, its actions would reveal that change to us. For if it were to expand and grow wider, and the planets <term>hairdrinnaidh</term> <note>literally "high stars," usually referring to the seven planets of the Ptolemaic system</note> were placed further away from us than they currently are, the size we would perceive in them would be less than what we see now. Moreover, there are many stars we see now that we would no longer be able to see at all at that time. Conversely, if the firmament were to contract <note>original: "cumhgochaidhi" - to narrow or hem in</note> around the earth, the size of the planets would appear much greater <note>original: "aibsidhi" - more vast or impressive</note> to us than they do now, and we would then see many stars <term>retlannaib</term> that we had never seen before. Therefore, since no one has ever observed such changes in the firmament—for if anyone had, it would surely have been recorded in writing—it is certain and beyond doubt that the firmament does not increase, decrease, contract, or expand.

Likewise, if the firmament were to tilt to the right side or the left side, or forward or backward, or if it were to move up or down from the place where it is, it would be necessary for its center <term>seantrom</term> <note>original: "seantrom" - from the Latin "centrum"</note> to move with it—namely, the earth <term>talam</term>. However, the earth cannot leave its own place, for it has no other place where it could go.

<summary>The author argues that the firmament is immutable and fixed in size; if it expanded or contracted, the stars would change in appearance or number, which has never been recorded. Furthermore, any movement of the firmament would require the earth (the center) to move, which is impossible as the earth has no other place to occupy.</summary>

<keywords>firmament, astronomy, earth as center, immutability of heavens, planets, stars, medieval physics</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the unchanging nature of the heavens. Having established that physical change is revealed through a body's actions—such as a body becoming healthy or changing speed—the author applies this logic to the firmament. He concludes that since the stars do not appear to change in size or position, the firmament itself must be stable, fixed in size, and perfectly centered around the earth.</meta>

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT. 75

Chapter 15.]
Anything that is changed undergoes that change in either quantity <term>cainndigecht</term> or quality <term>cailidecht</term>, and the actions of the body make that change evident to us. For when an unhealthy <note>original: "unsound"</note> body becomes healthy, or a healthy one becomes unhealthy, it is the actions <note>original: "gnimharta" - the deeds or visible functions</note> of that body that make that change evident to us. So, when a heavy body becomes light, the actions of the same body make that change evident to us. Thus, when a slow body becomes fast or a fast one becomes slow, it is the swiftness or slowness of that body that makes that motion <term>gluasacht</term> evident to us.

In the same way, if the heavens <term>firmament</term> were increased or decreased in size, its actions would reveal that change to us. For if it were expanded and extended, and the constellations placed further from us than they are now, we would then see them as smaller than we currently observe, and there are many stars we see now that we would not be able to see then. Consequently, if it were to close in around the earth, the size of the stars would seem much more enormous <note>original: "prodigious"</note> to us then than they do now, and we would then see many stars that we did not see before. Now, since no one has ever seen these changes in the firmament (for if they had, it would have been recorded in writing), it is sure and certain that the firmament undergoes neither increase nor decrease, and is neither narrowed nor widened.

Similarly, if the firmament tilted to its right or to its left, or leaned forwards or backwards, or if it moved up or down from the position in which it currently exists, the center—*that is*, the earth—must necessarily change along with it. But the earth cannot leave its own position, since it has no other place to go; for

For every space around it is<gloss>42</gloss> full of other bodies, and since two bodies cannot occupy the same space in the same way, the earth cannot leave its own place to move toward another body. Thus, it is necessary for the firmament to remain in its constant state, immovable and unchanging, around the earth throughout all eternity. And if it were said<gloss>43</gloss> that the changing of the firmament occurred through weakness or the decay<gloss>44</gloss> of its<gloss>45</gloss> body—as the human body is weakened by illness—then the movement of the firmament would be weakened in the same way that the gait<gloss>47</gloss> of a sick person is affected. In that case, the hours and the seasons would be altered<gloss>48</gloss>, a thing which has never happened; for they have always had one consistent course in the past, today<gloss>49</gloss>, and forever. Therefore, since neither the substance <term>sustaint</term>, quantity <term>cainndigecht</term>, quality <term>cailidheacht</term>, place, movement, time of the firmament, nor the rising, setting<gloss>50</gloss>, concealment<gloss>51</gloss>, or appearing of the stars <term>airdrinnac</term> is changed—and since this has never occurred and never shall<gloss>52</gloss>—it must be that the firmament possesses a firm and indissoluble nature. This nature has been, is, and shall remain as long as it pleases Him who created it<gloss>53</gloss>, and it is of a different nature<gloss>54</gloss> from the nature of the four elements <term>dula</term>.

It should be understood in this way: by contemplating its image and form<gloss>3</gloss> as<gloss>4</gloss> it exists within itself, and by shaping it clearly within your reason and mind. <margin>Chapter 16</margin>
Every space around it is full of other bodies; and since two bodies cannot, under any circumstances, occupy the same space, the earth cannot leave its own position to move toward other bodies. Consequently, the firmament must remain in a permanent, immovable, and unchanging position around the earth forever. If it were argued that a change in the firmament resulted from the failure or decay of its substance—just as the human body is weakened by illness—then the motion of the firmament would be weakened in the same way that a sick man's gait is slowed. In that case, the hours and seasons would be altered—a thing that has never happened, for they have always followed the same course, they do so today, and they will continue to do so forever.

Thus, since neither the substance, quality, quantity, position, motion, nor timing of the firmament—nor the rising, setting, concealment, or appearance of the stars—has changed, and since such a change has never occurred and never will, the system must necessarily be the same firm and immovable one that it has always been. It will remain so for as long as He who created it desires, and it must possess a nature different from the nature of the four elements <term>earth, air, fire, and water</term>.

16. **HE WHO PERFECTLY UNDERSTANDS THE CIRCLES AND LINES, ETC.** <note>original: "QUI PERFECTE CIRCULOS LINEASQUE ETCETERA"</note> Whoever could perfectly understand the orbits, lines, and points of the firmament would undoubtedly understand the nature of the entire heavens. The proper way to understand this is to consider its form and shape as it exists in itself, pondering it carefully with your reason and mind. The position of the first orbit of the firmament is as follows: it moves from the eastern point to the central upper point above the earth <note>the zenith</note>, from there to the western point, from there to the central lower point beneath the earth <note>the nadir</note>, and from there back again to the eastern point where it first began. That circle is called the "oriental" <note>eastern; original: "orientalis"</note> and "occidental" <note>western; original: "occidentalis"</note> circle.

Imagine after that a perfectly round circle representing the construction of the earth, and a larger circle outside of that—like the path <note>original: *cercall*; circle or orbit</note> of the sun. I will place the letter **A** at the location of Babylon, **B** at the location of Egypt, and **C** at the location of Africa <note>In one manuscript (B), this is listed as France.</note>. And in the same way again, the sunrise of Africa is **F**, its midday is **G**, and its sunset is **D**.

And so I say that when the sun rises at point **D**, it is revealed to the people of Babylon, but it remains hidden from the people of Egypt until it reaches point **E**, which is midday in Babylon, sunrise in Egypt, and midnight <note>original: *medon aithi*; middle of the night</note> in Africa. For it is then that the day begins for the Africans, while it is the end of the day for the Egyptians and midnight for the Babylonians. The sun remains with the Africans until it reaches point **D**, which is the end of the day for them, midnight in Egypt, and the beginning of the day in Babylon. 

And so it is that at one single point, there is the beginning of the day for Babylon, midnight for Egypt, and the end of the day for Africa. At another point, there is the beginning of the day for Egypt, midday for Babylon, and midnight for Africa. Likewise, at one point, there is the beginning of the day for Africa, midday for Egypt, and the end of the day for Babylon. And so again, at one point, there is midday for Africa, the end of the day for Egypt, and midnight for Babylon.

And so, according to God's ordinance, when the sun rises for one certain region in the world, it sets for another region, and it is...

### 1. On the Size of the Sun

18. GEOMETRIC REASONS, ETC. <note>original: "Raciones Geometrice Etcetera"</note> That is, the geometric reasoning of Ptolemy the astronomer <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Roman astronomer</note> proves the size of the sun. He says that it is necessary for the size <note>original: "quantity"</note> of the sun to be either equal to, less than, or greater than the size of the earth. 

If the sun and the earth were equal in size, the shadow of the earth—which is the night—would extend as wide as the earth itself out to the sphere of the fixed stars <note>original: "firmament stars"</note> and would extinguish them. Furthermore, an eclipse—that is, a lack of light—would happen to the moon every single month throughout the year. This is because the mass of the earth, being as large as the body of the sun, would block the sun's light from the moon and the stars. Thus, there would never be a [full] moon, but only perpetual darkness from the beginning of the night until its end. Therefore, since the moon does appear and the stars are seen shining in the night, it is understood from this that the sun and the earth are not equal in size.

To illustrate this clearly, I will create a diagram of three concentric circles. The outermost circle represents the sphere of the fixed stars, the middle circle represents the orbit of the sun, and the smallest circle represents the orbit of the moon, with the earth at the center. If the sun, located below the earth in its own sphere, were the same width as the earth, then the earth's shadow on the side opposite the sun would also be as wide as the earth, extending directly out to the sphere of the stars.

<summary>This page uses Ptolemaic geometry to argue that the sun and earth cannot be the same size. If they were, the earth's shadow would be a constant cylinder that would cause monthly lunar eclipses and block the light of the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, astronomy, sun size, earth size, lunar eclipse, fixed stars, geometric proof, shadow of the earth</keywords>

->*Concerning the size of the sun.*<-

18. Geometrical calculations, etc. <note>original: "RATIONES GEOMETRICAE, ETCETERA"</note>

The geometrical calculations of Ptolemy, the astronomer <term>astrologer: in the medieval and early modern periods, "astrologer" and "astronomer" were often used interchangeably to describe those who studied the heavens</term>, prove the size of the sun. He says: the size of the sun must be equal to, smaller than, or larger than the size of the earth. 

If the sun and the earth were equal in size, the shadow of the earth—that is, the darkness that occupies the same amount of space as the earth itself—would travel out as far as the sphere of the fixed stars <term>fixed stars: the stars that appear to stay in the same position relative to each other, as opposed to the "wandering stars" or planets</term> and obscure them. Furthermore, an eclipse—meaning a deficiency of light in the moon—would occur every month throughout the year. This would happen because the earth's mass, being as large as the body of the sun, would block the sun's light from reaching the moon and the stars. Consequently, there would never be a visible moon, but only constant darkness from the beginning of the night until the end. Therefore, since the moon is actually present and the stars are seen illuminated at night, it should be understood from this that the sun and the earth are not equal in size.

To make it clearly understood, I will draw a diagram of three concentric circles: the outer circle for the orbit of the fixed stars, the middle circle for the orbit of the sun, and the smallest circle for the orbit of the moon. The earth is in the middle, and the sun is positioned beneath the earth in its own sphere, equal in size to the earth; the shadow of the earth is positioned opposite the sun on the other side of the earth, extending straight out to the sphere of the stars with the same width as the earth.

<summary>This page uses Ptolemaic geometry to argue that the Sun and Earth cannot be the same size, as a cylinder-shaped shadow would cause permanent monthly eclipses and obscure the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, solar size, Earth's shadow, lunar eclipse, fixed stars, celestial spheres, geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical proof regarding the relative sizes of the Sun and the Earth, moving from the hypothesis that they are equal to the hypothesis that the Sun is smaller, before concluding that the Sun must be significantly larger.</meta>

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

19. **If, however, the sun were of a smaller size, etc.** <note>original Latin: "SI AUTEM SOL MINORIS ESSET QUANTITATIS ETCETERA"</note> 
That is to say, if the size of the sun were smaller than the size of the earth, every impossible thing we previously mentioned would occur, and even more besides. For if that were the case, the shadow of the earth would continually grow in size and width as it moved away from the earth toward the sphere of the fixed stars <term>fixed stars: stars that appear to maintain a permanent position relative to each other</term>. It would darken the greatest part of them, and an eclipse would happen to the planets every single month. Furthermore, the eclipse of the moon, as we have said, would last from nightfall until morning. Therefore, since we have never seen this happen, nor heard of it, nor found it written in records, it is certain that the size of the sun is not smaller than the size of the earth. The figure below illustrates the point we are making.

20. **Therefore, it must necessarily be admitted.** <note>original Latin: "NECESSARIO IGITUR FATENDUM EST"</note>
It must be admitted that the size of the sun is greater than that of the earth, and that the shadow of the earth does not reach past the sphere of Mercury. The shadow of the earth is shaped like a cone <note>original: "ar cuma speiri," literally "in the shape of a sphere," but describing a tapering form</note>; it is wide at the earth and narrows gradually until it ends slightly above the sphere of the moon. It is this same shadow that darkens the moon according to how it encounters it; for when the moon is in the southern or northern part of the shadow, the shadow darkens only the part of the moon it touches, but when the shadow falls directly on the middle of the moon, it darkens it entirely. 

Nevertheless, we know and have found it written that this shadow of the earth does not reach the fixed stars or any of the planets except for the moon, which is the earth's neighbor. Because of this, all the planets (excluding the moon) and the fixed stars are perpetually illuminated by the sun. From this, it is proven that the sun is much larger than the earth, as the figure below shows.

But if the sun were of a smaller size, and so forth.** <note>original Latin: "SI AUTEM SOL MINORIS ESSET QUANTITATIS, ETCETERA"</note> If the size of the Sun were less than that of the Earth, every impossible and unbearable thing I have mentioned, and more besides, would occur. This is because the shadow of the Earth would be constantly increasing in size and width as it stretched away from the Earth toward the sphere of the constellations <note>The "fixed stars" or the celestial sphere</note>. It would darken the greater part of them, a planetary eclipse would occur every month, and a lunar eclipse, as I mentioned, would be in progress throughout the night until morning. Since we have never seen this, never heard of it, and never found it recorded in writing, the size of the Sun cannot be less than that of the Earth. The diagram below explains this statement I am making.

**20. It must therefore necessarily be admitted.** <note>original Latin: "NECESSARIO IGITUR FATENDUM EST"</note> It must be admitted that the size of the Sun is greater than that of the Earth, and that the shadow of the Earth never extends beyond the sphere of Mercury. The shadow of the Earth is conical in shape, with the wide side toward the Earth, while it becomes narrower by degrees until it comes to an end a little above the sphere of the Moon. This same shadow obscures the Moon as it passes over it; for when the Moon is in the north or south of the shadow, the shadow obscures the portion of the Moon it touches, and when the shadow spreads over the Moon, it obscures it completely. However, we know—and we have found it written—that the shadow of the Earth does not reach the stars or any of the planets, but only the Moon, which is the Earth's neighbor. Therefore, all the planets (except the Moon) and the stars always borrow their light from the Sun. Thus, he <note>Ptolemy, the 2nd-century mathematician and astronomer whose geocentric model dominated medieval science</note> proves that the Sun is much larger than the Earth, as this diagram below shows.

## 21. THE MOON HAS NO LIGHT EXCEPT [FROM THE SUN]<gloss>1</gloss> AND SO FORTH.

<note>original Latin: "LUNA NIHIL LUMINIS HABET NISI [A SOLE] ETCETERA"</note>

There is no light in the moon itself except for what it receives from the sun. Both of them are equally round, like a sphere or ball. If they were flat plates<gloss>2</gloss>,<gloss>3</gloss> as the unlearned have said, when they were in the east or the west of the firmament, you would see nothing of them but their edges; yet you would see them in full at the zenith <note>original: "mullach," literally "top" or "summit"</note> of the firmament. Therefore, since it is obvious<gloss>4</gloss> that they appear round at the zenith as they do in every other position, it is understood that they are spherical and not flat. And the round part that we see<gloss>5</gloss> in each of them is nothing but<gloss>6</gloss> a hemisphere—that is, half a sphere—while the other half is unseen. Thus it is clear that the sun, the moon, the planets, and all the high stars have a spherical body, for from whichever side they are viewed, they appear round.

And as we have said, <gloss>7</gloss>the moon has no light from its own nature.<gloss>7</gloss> Instead, it is<gloss>8</gloss> dark and polished like iron that has been cleaned,<gloss>9</gloss> and all the light it has it receives from the sun. Its sphere is the sphere closest to the Earth among the heavenly bodies,<gloss>10</gloss> and the sun's sphere is the fourth one above it. And though they are far from each other, when the moon is directly under the sun in the same degree as it,<gloss>11</gloss> it is then that the sun illuminates the upper half of the moon, while the side facing us remains dark; it is then that we have a completely dark moon. Because of this, we do not see<gloss>12</gloss> any part of the moon at that time.

And when the moon leaves that direct point where it is beneath the sun and glides<gloss>13</gloss> gradually away from it toward the east, the light flows<gloss>14</gloss>,<gloss>15</gloss> gradually westward over it.<gloss>16</gloss>

The light that the upper part receives from the sun illuminates<gloss>17</gloss> its lower edge, and it is then that the moon is at its prime—that is, its first light is visible to us. For that<gloss>18</gloss> is the first time we see it on its circuit; and the further it moves eastward<gloss>19</gloss> from the...
