Chapter 21.

## 21. THE MOON HAS NO LIGHT EXCEPT [FROM THE SUN], AND SO FORTH.
<note>original Latin: "LUNA NIHIL LUMINIS HABET NISI, ETCETERA"</note>

The moon has no light of its own except for what it borrows from the sun. Both are spherical, like the shape of a round ball. If they were flat planes, as the unlearned <note>original: "ignorant"</note> have claimed, then whenever they were in the east or the west of the firmament <term>the heavens, often imagined as a solid dome</term>, only their edges would be visible; they would only appear completely round when they were at the zenith <note>original: "top of the firmament"</note>. Since they appear no more clearly spherical at the highest point of the sky than they do in any other position, it must be understood that they are spherical and not flat. That round portion which we see in each of them is only a hemisphere—that is, half a sphere—while the other half remains invisible to us. Thus, it is clear that the sun, moon, planets, and stars all have a spherical form, because they appear round from whatever side they are viewed.

As I have said, the moon has no light of its own nature; it is dark and polished <note>The original suggests a reflective surface like a mirror.</note> like iron that has been cleaned. Whatever light it possesses, it borrows from the sun. The moon’s sphere is the one nearest the Earth among the heavenly bodies, while the sun's sphere is the fourth one above it <note>This reflects the ancient Ptolemaic system, where the Earth is at the center, followed by the spheres of the Moon, Mercury, Venus, and then the Sun.</note>. Although they are far apart, when the moon is directly beneath the sun at the exact same degree, the sun illuminates the upper part of the moon while the side facing us remains dark. For us, this is a completely dark moon. Therefore, we see nothing of the moon at that time.

When the moon moves away from that exact degree where it sat beneath the sun and begins to move eastward by degrees, the light gradually moves westward across its surface.

The light that the upper part borrows from the sun then illuminates the moon's lower edge. At that point, the moon is in its "prime" <note>The first appearance of the new crescent moon.</note>—meaning its first light is turned toward us—for that is the first stage in which we can see it. As it moves further eastward away from the sun...

[The nearer the moon is to the] sun, the more the light from above surrounds its upper part; and the further east it travels from the sun, the more its light increases on its western side. Its darkness increases on its eastern side until the fourteenth day <note>This refers to the Full Moon, which occurs roughly 14 days into the lunar cycle.</note>, for it is then at its furthest point from the sun and is directly opposite it on the other side of the Earth. At that moment, the <term>hemisphere</term> <note>original: "temisperium"</note> nearest to the Earth is entirely light, and the upper <term>hemisphere</term> is entirely dark. Thus, the moon is never without one half being dark and the other half light, regardless of the path it takes.

And when the moon deviates from the <term>diameter</term> of the sun toward the west—that is, from the straight line where it was on the other side of the Earth opposite the sun—the light of the moon gradually turns upward toward us. The amount that the upper edge <note>original: "ichtarach" (lower), though other versions use "uachtarach" (upper)</note> is illuminated at its prime <note>The "prime" refers to the New Moon or the very beginning of the lunar cycle.</note> is equal to the amount that its eastern part is darkened. And so, the amount it is illuminated each day until the fourteenth day is the same amount it is darkened each day until the end of the month, or until it is directly beneath the sun in the same degree as it—positioned between the sun and the Earth. At that time, the half of the moon facing us is dark and the upper half is light. To make this clearly understood, I will provide a <term>diagram</term> <note>original: "fidair"</note> here below, in which I will place the sun beneath one side of the Earth on the western side, and the moon twelve degrees slightly above the Earth to the east of it. I will draw the moon entirely dark except for the western edge nearest the sun, showing it at its prime.

I will also provide another <term>diagram</term> in which I will place the moon at the highest point of its own <term>sphere</term> <note>original: "speiri"; here meaning its orbit</note> at the end of the seventh day of the month, with half of it light and half dark; I will place the sun beneath one side of the Earth on the western side.

Finally, I will provide a third <term>diagram</term> in which I will place the moon directly in the east, with its upper half dark and its lower half—which faces the sun and the Earth—light. I will place the sun at that time, after it has set, beneath one side of the Earth, and so forth. And here follows the <term>diagram</term> itself.

->**The cause of a lunar eclipse**<-

22. After these arguments, etc. <note>original: "POSTQUAM ARGUMENTIS ETCETERA"</note> That is: after it has been proven to us by necessary arguments and by geometric diagrams that the moon and all the high stars are illuminated by the sun, we will now reveal what causes the natural darkness on the moon which is called an eclipse. Regarding this matter, we say that since the moon takes its light from the sun, and since there is nothing else that could take that light away from it except the earth, it must be the shadow of the earth—which is positioned directly between the sun and the moon—that surrounds the moon and takes the sun's light from it. This darkening of the moon by the shadow of the earth is what an eclipse is.

This happens unerringly during those months when the moon reaches the Head or the Tail of the Dragon <note>original: "ceann no derball na Dreaguine." In medieval astronomy, these terms refer to the lunar nodes—the two points where the moon's orbit crosses the path of the sun (the ecliptic). An eclipse can only occur when the moon is near these points.</note>. At those times, the moon is placed in a direct line exactly opposite the sun and the earth, and it passes precisely through the shadow of the earth and is entirely darkened. When the moon moves to the south or the north of that shadow, it avoids the eclipse entirely. However, if any part of it touches that shadow, as much of the moon as is covered by the shadow is the amount of the eclipse that appears upon it.

And that is why there are two kinds of eclipse: a universal eclipse <note>original: "eclipsis uneuersalis"</note>, which is a total eclipse, and a particular eclipse <note>original: "eclipsis particularis"</note>, which is a partial eclipse. And that is why it is not the same time...

### Concerning the cause of a lunar eclipse.

22. After [proving these] arguments, and so forth. <note>original: "POSTQUAM ARGUMENTIS, ETCETERA"</note>
Having demonstrated through strong arguments and geometric diagrams that the moon and all the stars receive their light from the sun, we will now show the cause of the natural darkness on the moon which is called an eclipse. As I begin this subject, I declare that since the moon receives its light from the sun, and there is nothing else to block that light except the earth, it is the shadow of the earth—positioned exactly between the sun and the moon—that covers the moon and blocks the sun’s light. This darkening of the moon by the shadow of the earth is an eclipse. 

It happens without fail whenever the moon reaches the <term>head or tail of the Dragon</term> <note>These are the lunar nodes: the two points where the moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic (the sun's apparent path). Eclipses can only occur when the moon is near these points.</note>. At those times, it lies in a straight line exactly opposite the sun and the earth; it passes directly into the shadow of the earth and is totally darkened. When the moon moves to the south or north of that shadow, it avoids a total eclipse. However, whichever part of the moon touches that shadow will be eclipsed.

Therefore, there are two types of eclipses: a "universal eclipse" <note>original: "eclipsis universalis"</note>, which is a general or total eclipse, and a...

### Eclipse of the other planets.

The planets themselves are the cause of the eclipse of other planets, for it is the lower planet that darkens the higher planet. In the same way, it is the moon <note>original: "re"</note> that darkens them all in their turn. To explain the nature of eclipses further <note>original: "a ndobart a cell"</note>, I will provide a geometric diagram below so that the eclipse may be clearly understood.

## 23. IT IS MANIFEST, ETC. <note>original: "MANIFESTUM EST ETCETERA"</note>

That is, it is clear, as we have said, that the light of all the planets <note>original: "airdrinnach"</note> comes from the sun, and that they are perfectly round like a ball, just as the sun and moon <note>original: "esca"</note> are. The reason why their roundness is not as obvious as the roundness of the other two [the sun and moon] is because they are all positioned above the sun and far away from us, except for Venus and Mercury.

Half of every star <note>original: "retlainne"</note> that is positioned above the sun is illuminated by the sun itself, regardless of where those stars are in their own orbits <note>original: "cercaill," literally "circles"</note>—whether they are in the same degree as the sun, or far from it, or near it, or ahead of it, or behind it—it is from the sun that they all receive light. Whenever any of the three planets located above the sun <note>In the Ptolemaic system, these are the "superior" planets: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> are directly opposite it, their light appears brightest <note>original: "aibsidhi"</note> toward the earth for two reasons. The first reason is that a person standing in darkness perceives the light they see outside that darkness to be brighter than a person who is standing in the light itself. Therefore, a person who is within the thick, dark shadow of the earth at night, looking out at the planets...

The eclipse of the other planets: One of the planets itself is the cause of the eclipse of the others, for a planet in a lower orbit <term>lower planet</term> <note>In the geocentric model, "lower" means closer to the Earth, while "higher" means further away.</note> obscures one in a higher orbit. 23. IT IS EVIDENT, ETCETERA <note>original: *MANIFESTUM EST, ETCETERA*</note>. It is evident, as I have stated, that the light of all the stars comes from the sun and that they are spherical <note>original: "round like a ball"</note> like the sun and moon. The reason why their roundness is not as obvious as the roundness of those other two is because they are all located above the sun, far from us, except for Venus and Mercury. Half of every star located above the sun is illuminated by it at whatever point in its own orbit that star may be; that is, whether they are in the same degree as the sun, or whether they are far from it, near to it, in front of it, or behind it, they all receive light from the sun. 

Every time any of these planets situated above the sun are directly opposite it, their light appears most brilliant <note>original: "prodigious"</note> from the perspective of the earth for two reasons. The first reason: a person who is in darkness perceives the light they see outside that darkness as more brilliant than a person who is standing in the light itself. Accordingly, a person who is in the thick, dark shadow of the earth at night, as he beholds the planets that are directly in

...is directly opposite the sun, its light appears very prominent because of the great darkness surrounding it. Another reason is that when any of these planets is placed directly opposite the sun, it must move backward in its own orbit <term>retrograde motion</term>. Its light is more visible during this turn because it is closer to the earth. This never happens with the planets located above the sun <note>In the geocentric system, the "superior" planets (Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn) were considered "above" the sun.</note>.

As for the planets located below the sun <note>The "inferior" planets: Mercury and Venus.</note>, namely **Mercury** and **Venus**, they never reach a position directly opposite the sun, nor do they reach any of the four "corners" <term>quadratures</term> <note>Aspects 90 degrees from the sun.</note>. Instead, they are always close to it, either ahead of it or behind it. The closer they are to the sun, the less their light appears; the further they are from it, the greater their light.

Truly, the light <note>original: *Solus uero*</note> of the planets that move toward opposition with the sun increases bit by bit until they reach a point they cannot pass, at which point they must turn back toward the sun again. During this turn, their light decreases <note>original: *ar dombisec*</note> until they are at the same degree as the sun, positioned beneath it. At that point, the half closest to the sun is illuminated and the other half is dark—just as we said of the moon when it is in the same degree as the sun. The moon is never visible except when it is at least twelve degrees away from the sun, either at its first appearance <term>new moon</term> or its very end <term>waning crescent</term>.

These planets follow the same pattern. When they are twelve degrees to the east, ahead of the sun and turning back toward it, or when they are twelve degrees to the west of the sun while moving, they appear "horned" <term>crescent-shaped</term> like the new moon. When they move away from the sun as far as they can go, they have their full light. However, this change <term>phases</term> is not as obvious to us as the changing light of the moon, because these planets are much further away from us than the moon is. Therefore, even when their light is great, their apparent size is small due to their distance. When they are at their closest point to the earth, the brilliance <note>original: *lasair* (literally: flame/blaze)</note> is seen...

<summary>This page explains the varying brightness and phases of planets, noting that Mercury and Venus (the inferior planets) never reach opposition and exhibit phases like the moon, though they are less visible due to distance.</summary>

...front of the sun, one considers the light extraordinary <note>original: "prodigious"</note> on account of the intense darkness which surrounds it. Another reason: when any one of the planets is placed directly in front of the sun, it must revolve in its own orbit, and its light is more brilliant because of that revolution, for it is then nearest to the earth; and this never happens in the case of the planets above the sun <note>The "superior" planets: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note>.

The planets that are beneath the sun, that is, **Mercury** and **Venus**, never arrive opposite the sun, and are never in the quadrate aspect <term>quadrature</term> <note>A position 90 degrees from the sun.</note>. They are not like the others, but are always near it, either before or behind it; and the nearer they are to the sun, the less is their light, and the further they are from it, the greater the light.

The light, however, of the planets that pass opposite the sun is always increasing by degrees until they reach the place beyond which they cannot go, and where they must turn towards the sun again. During that backward motion <term>retrograde motion</term>, their light is on the decrease until they are in the same degree as the sun, positioned beneath it. Then, the half of them nearest the sun is lit and the other half is dark, just as I said in reference to the moon when it is in the same degree as the sun; for the moon is never visible except when it is at least fourteen degrees to one side of the sun, that is, at its prime <term>new moon</term>, or at its extreme wane <term>the final crescent before disappearing</term>.

Those planets [Mercury and Venus] also behave in the same manner; for when they are to the east, twelve degrees in front of the sun turning towards the sun, or when they are in motion twelve degrees to the west of the sun, they appear horned <term>crescent-shaped</term> after the manner of the new moon. When they come away from the sun, as they cannot advance further, they have their full light, although that change is not as evident to us as is the change of the light of the moon, because they are much further from us than the moon. Therefore, when their light is great, their size <note>original: "bulk"</note> appears small on account of their distance from us. When they are nearest the earth, the beam that comes from them appears—

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.

24. **I SAY THAT THE MOON IS THE CAUSE OF THE SOLAR ECLIPSE, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "Dico quod luna soli quasam eclipsis etcetera"</note> That is, I say that the moon is the cause of the sun’s eclipse, because its sphere <note>In the geocentric model, each celestial body was thought to move within its own transparent "sphere" nested around the Earth. The moon's sphere was the closest to Earth.</note> is the lowest of the heavenly spheres, and the sun’s sphere is the fourth sphere above it. Therefore, every movement the moon makes is beneath the sun. 

When the moon happens to be at the "head" or "tail" of the Dragon <note>The "head and tail of the dragon" are medieval astronomical terms for the lunar nodes—the two points where the moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic (the sun's apparent path).</note> in the same degree directly with the sun, without deviating to the south or the north, it takes the light of the sun away from us; that darkness is what an eclipse is. However, when the moon deviates to the right or left <note>The author uses "right or left" to mean south or north of the ecliptic line.</note> and does not travel directly through the head or tail of the Dragon beneath the sun in the same degree, then an eclipse is avoided.

It is the body of the moon that causes the darkness seen in the sun when an eclipse occurs. Therefore, it is clear that an eclipse never happens except when the moon is directly beneath the sun in the same degree. It always begins on the western side of the sun and finishes on the eastern side. When the moon travels slightly outside that direct alignment with the sun, it sometimes darkens only a part of the sun for us. Because of this, there are two types of solar eclipse, just as there are for the moon: namely, a total eclipse and a partial eclipse <term>original: "rannaighi"</term>.

Furthermore, because of the diversity of different regions <term>original: "crich"</term>, the eclipse appears differently in them. When an eclipse occurs, it is not seen equally in every land; for there is a land where it is seen and a land where it is not, and a land where it is seen more and a land where it is seen less. 

The situation is like this: if the sun were in a direct line high above us, and the moon were in the same line beneath it, an eclipse would necessarily occur for us. Yet, if someone were at that same time in the East of the world looking at the sun, it would seem to them that the sun was in the West. And if another person were at the same time in the West of the world looking at the sun, it would seem to them...

It also introduces the concept of total versus partial eclipses and explains why eclipses appear differently depending on a viewer's geographic location.</summary>

24. I DECLARE THAT THE MOON IS THE CAUSE OF THE SUN’S ECLIPSE, AND SO FORTH. <note>original Latin: "Dico quod luna soli causam eclipsis, etcetera"</note> I declare that the moon is the cause of the eclipse of the sun, because its sphere <note>In the medieval geocentric model, each celestial body was thought to move within its own transparent "sphere" nested around the Earth. The moon's sphere was the closest to Earth.</note> is the lowest of the heavenly spheres, and the sphere of the sun is the fourth sphere above that. Accordingly, every course it makes is beneath the sun. When it arrives at the head or the tail of the Dragon <note>The "head" and "tail" of the Dragon are historical terms for the lunar nodes—the two points where the moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic (the sun's apparent path). An eclipse can only occur when the moon is near these points.</note>, in exactly the same degree as the sun, without inclining to the south or to the north, it deprives us of the light of the sun, and that darkness is an eclipse. When, however, the moon inclines to its right or left side <note>The author uses "right or left" to mean north or south of the sun's path.</note> and does not move exactly in the head or in the tail of the Dragon beneath the sun in the same degree, it avoids producing an eclipse. 

That darkness which is seen on the sun when there is an eclipse is the body of the moon; therefore, it is evident that an eclipse never occurs except when the moon is exactly beneath the sun in the same degree. It always begins on the west side of the sun and finishes on the east. When the moon moves outside that exact degree of the sun, it sometimes obscures a portion of the sun from us. Therefore, an eclipse of the sun is of two kinds, as is an eclipse of the moon: total and partial.

The eclipse varies in various lands, for when there is an eclipse it is not visible to the same extent in every land. There is one land in which it is visible and another in which it is not, and one land in which it is more visible and another in which it is less visible. It happens in this way: if the sun were in a straight line directly over our heads and the moon were in the same line beneath it, it would necessitate an eclipse for us. If a person were at the same time in the east of the world looking at the sun, they would imagine they saw it in the west of the world; and if there were another person at the same time in the west of the world beholding the sun, they would imagine they saw it in the east of the world, but neither of them see the eclipse of the sun...

...in the east of the world. However, neither of them sees a solar eclipse; for even if the line of sight for each person passed directly between the sun and the moon—even if the moon were in a direct line beneath the sun—due to its distance from that line, it [the moon] would not block the sun's light from them like a solid dark body would. And at the moment <note>original: "pinnsi," an adaptation of the Latin "punctum" meaning a point in time or moment</note> that the sun is darkened in this way, the high stars <note>meaning the planets or fixed stars</note> are visible during the day because of the intensity of the darkness.

A solar eclipse lasts a shorter time than a lunar eclipse because of the combined speed of both bodies as they move past one another. This is not the case with a lunar eclipse, which is caused by the Earth <note>specifically, the Earth's shadow</note>; nothing causes it to end except the movement of the moon alone while the Earth remains still.

And if anyone should object to us here and say that the moon is not the cause of a solar eclipse, arguing that if it were as we say, then Venus and Mercury would be just as likely to cause an eclipse when they are in a direct line beneath the sun. To that we answer: when Venus and Mercury are beneath the sun in the same degree, they are at a great distance from it, yet their size and width depart from it; <note>original note: The Irish translator misunderstood the original Latin text. In the source (Sacrobosco), it explains that while these planets may share a degree of longitude with the sun, they are usually too far away in latitude to cause an eclipse.</note> and it often happens the same way to the moon, for it is often beneath the sun in the same degree of longitude, yet it remains at a distance from it in latitude.

Just as a solar eclipse is not caused by the stars (which are smaller in body than the moon), because when a small body is placed beneath a large body and close to it, the closer it is to the large body, the less of it it hides. But when the small body is placed far from the large body and close to the eye looking at both—the farther it is from the large body and the closer it is to the observer’s sight—the more of the large body it hides. It is to such an extent that even a wild apple <note>original: "uball fiadain"</note> could hide the entire body of the sun from one's sight.

And to make this understood, I will provide a diagram here below...

A solar eclipse is of shorter duration than a lunar eclipse because of the speed with which the sun and moon pass one another. This is not the case for a lunar eclipse, which is caused by the Earth <note>specifically, the Earth's shadow</note>. Nothing ends the eclipse except the movement of the moon above while the Earth remains stationary <note>The text reflects the geocentric view that the Earth is fixed at the center of the universe</note>.

If anyone should challenge me by saying that the moon is not the cause of a solar eclipse, and that if it were as I described, it would be just as likely for Venus or Mercury to cause an eclipse when they are in a direct line beneath the sun, I answer them this way: When Venus and Mercury are beneath the sun in the same degree, they match its longitude; however, there is a decrease in their size and width. <note>The Irish translator misunderstood the original Latin source here. The source (Sacrobosco) explains that while these planets may share a degree of longitude with the sun, they are usually too far away in latitude to cause an eclipse.</note> The same thing often happens to the moon: it is often beneath the sun in the same degree of longitude, yet it remains far from the sun in latitude.

In the same way, a solar eclipse is not caused by stars that are smaller than the moon or by those closer to the sun. This is because when a small body is placed near a large body, the closer it is to the larger one, the less it conceals. Conversely, when a small body is far from the large body and near the eye of the observer looking at both, the further it moves from the large body and the closer it gets to the observer's sight, the more it hides the large body. In this manner, even a crab apple <note>original: "wild apple"</note> could hide the sun from one's sight.

Imagine a large circle, like the orbit <note>original: "cercall," circle</note> of the sun, and place the sun at its very top. However, the same groups at **A** and **B** see the sun clearly without any eclipse, because the moon appears very small in comparison to the sun. The moon does not hide the sun from them; because they are at a distance from the direct line to the sun, their line of sight passes easily above the moon to reach the sun.

### 25. THE MOON IS NOT SEEN UNTIL, ETC.
<note>original Latin: "LUNA NON UIDETUR PRIUSQUAM ETCETERA"</note>

That is to say: the moon is never seen until it is twelve degrees away from the sun, because the intensity of the sun’s light prevents us from seeing it. Therefore, the people in the west see the moon sooner than the people in the east on the same day. ### 26. I SAY THAT THE MOON AND THE STARS, ETC. <note>original: "DICO QUOD LUNA AC SIDERA ETCETERA."</note>

That is to say, I say that the moon and the stars are seen at the same hour and at the same time appearing bright in one region <note>original: "crích"</note> and dark in another region. For the people of the region who see them clearly, it is the night on their side of the world at that same time that allows them to see them. It is the day that prevents the people of the other region from seeing them; however, it is not the necessity of the daylight itself that causes this, but rather the weakness of human sight.

And thus, in the region where the sun is revealed, the moon and the stars are hidden for that duration; but when the sun is hidden, it is then that they are revealed. It is the same with fire <note>original: "tene"</note> and every other bright object: though they may be far from you in the night, their light is conspicuous, yet even if they were near to you during the day, their light would be inconspicuous.

Therefore, if you desire <note>original: "sailter let" — if it is thought by you</note> to see the stars during the day, go early in the morning or in the evening into a deep, dark pit and look up above your head; you will see them clearly at the peak of the firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>The "firmament" refers to the sphere of the sky or the heavens.</note> above you. You will also see them clearly at the time of a solar eclipse <note>original: "eclipsis na greine"</note>.

I will now make a circle representing the orbit of the fixed stars <note>original: "nairdrinnach ndaingean"</note>, in which I will arrange many of the great stars. I will make another sphere inside that one to represent the orbit of the sun, in which I will place the sun itself. Inside that, I will make another sphere for the orbit of the moon, in which I will place the moon itself as it would appear at the end of the thirtieth day from the start of the month. And I will make a fourth sphere inside those and I will mark it <note>the Earth</note> in the center <term>center</term> <note>original: "sentrom"</note>. I will label **A** as the east, **B** as the top, **C** as the west, and **D** as the bottom. The four small circles that are on the circle of the Earth...

### 26. I SAY THAT THE MOON AND THE STARS, ETC. <note>original: "DICO QUOD LUNA AC SIDERA, ETCETERA."</note>

I declare that the moon and the stars appear at the same hour and season, appearing bright in one country and dark in another. The reason why the people of one country see them shining is because it is night on their side of the world at that same time. Daylight prevents the people of the other country from seeing them; yet it is not the daylight itself that causes this, but rather the weakness of human sight. 

In this way, the moon and the stars are hidden from the country in which the sun is visible during that period, and when the sun is hidden, they are revealed. It is the same with fire and every other light-giving object: though they may be far from you at night, their light is immense <note>original: "prodigious"</note>, yet even if they were near to you during the day, their light would not seem great. Therefore, if you intend to see the stars during the day, go in the morning or evening into a deep, dark pit and look up; you will see them clearly above your head at the peak of the firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>The "firmament" refers to the sphere of the sky or the heavens.</note>. You will also see them clearly at the time of a solar eclipse.

Now I will describe a circle representing the orbit of the fixed stars <term>fixed stars</term> <note>In traditional astronomy, these are stars that appear to stay in a fixed position relative to each other, unlike the "wandering" planets.</note>, in which I will place a diagram of many of the great stars. Inside of it, I will create another sphere for the sun, in which I will place the sun itself. Inside of that, I will create another sphere for the moon, in which I will place the moon itself at the end of the thirtieth day from the beginning of the month. I will make a fourth sphere within these and write "I" in the center of it, "A" in the east of it, "B" at the top of it, "C" in the west of it, and "D" at the bottom of it; and let four cities be represented by

## 27. **IT IS ESTABLISHED THAT THERE ARE FOUR SPHERES OF THE MOON, ETC.** <note>original: "CONSTAT QUOD QUATUOR SPERE LUNE ETCETERA."</note>

That is to say, the scholars state that the moon has four circles <term>circles</term> <note>original: "cercailli"; in medieval astronomy, these "circles" refer to the nested spheres or orbits that explain the complex movement of planets.</note>. The first of these circles is called the Great Sphere <term>Great Sphere</term> <note>original: "speir mhor"; this refers to the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere that carries all celestial bodies from east to west every 24 hours.</note>. By its own great speed, it turns with<sup>2</sup> the moon once every day and night around the world, causing it to rise in the east and set in the west during that circuit.

And if this Great Sphere did not move the moon once from the east to the west of the world every<sup>3</sup> day and night as we have said, you would see it every single day and every single night<sup>4</sup> without hiding from the time<sup>5</sup> it was at its first appearance <note>original: "prim"; referring to the new moon</note> in the west, moving eastward little by little until it was finally in the middle of the<sup>6</sup> month in the east of the world. And having set there, it would be hidden by day and night<sup>7</sup> until it rose<sup>8</sup> in the west at its first appearance again at the start of the next month. Therefore, it is the movement of the Great Sphere...

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the moon's celestial motions, specifically contrasting the "forced" daily rotation from east to west with the moon's "natural" monthly progression from west to east through the zodiac.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
### AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

...which we said carries the moon with it during the day and night from the east to the west of the world. However, the direct movement of the moon itself is from the west to the east of the world. This is clearly the case because when the moon is at its prime <term>the new moon; original: "ar a primh"</term> in the west, we see it every single night moving eastward, bit by bit, until it reaches the east of the world. This is the most natural movement and the one most proper to it, for this is the movement of the sphere in which the moon itself is fixed. That other movement, which carries it from the east to the west of the world during the day and night, is nothing but a forced, unnatural movement <note>Medieval astronomers distinguished between the "forced" daily rotation of the heavens and the "natural" orbital motion of the planets.</note>.

The second sphere belonging to the moon is named the sphere similar to the sphere of the signs <term>the zodiac; original: "speir na comartad"</term>. When the moon is in this sphere, it can clearly be seen entering and leaving the zodiacal signs. For when it goes toward the southern signs, it declines from them <note>original: "claenadh uata doni". The Latin source used by the translator (noted as 'S.') suggests a more complex meaning: "When the signs are northern it declines toward them, and when the signs are southern it descends toward them." The Irish translator appears to have skipped part of this explanation.</note>. Nevertheless, it never truly leaves the course of the sphere of the signs.

The third sphere belonging to the moon is called the eccentric sphere <term>original: "speir sa sentrom", literally "sphere in the center"</term>, which has its center outside the center of the earth. Because of this, one part of it is close to the earth and the other part is very far away. For this reason, the moon in that sphere is sometimes near the earth and at other times far from it. The movement of that sphere is from the west to the east of the world. <margin>Note: One manuscript version (F) becomes mostly illegible from this point.</margin>

Within the body of that sphere is another small sphere named the epicycle <term>original: "speir bec eli", literally "another small sphere"</term>, which revolves downward toward the moon. The moon's own sphere is fixed and immovable within that small sphere, like a nail in a board <note>This "nail in a board" simile was a standard medieval way to explain how a planet remains fixed within its epicycle while the epicycle itself moves.</note>. This small sphere moves eastward within the larger sphere where the moon is fixed. When the moon reaches the top of that small sphere, its course quickens. When it is at the bottom, its course slows down. When it slows down, it performs a movement that turns back toward the west, just as the other planets do. However, this turning <term>retrograde motion; original: "an timpodh"</term> is not visible to the eye.

That other motion, which moves it from the east to the west of the world within a day and a night, is only a forced, unnatural one <note>Medieval astronomers distinguished between the "forced" daily rotation of the heavens and the "natural" slower orbital motion of the planets.</note>.

When the moon is in this sphere, it is visible moving into and out of the zodiacal signs. One portion of this is near the Earth, and the other portion is very far distant from it. On that account, the moon in that sphere is at one time near the Earth and at another far from it; the motion of that sphere is from the west to the east of the world.

Within the body of that sphere is another small sphere, called "the sphere which revolves downwards to the moon" <note>This refers to an epicycle, a smaller orbital circle used in Ptolemaic astronomy to explain planetary speed variations.</note>. This little sphere, which is within the sphere where the moon is fixed, moves eastwards. When the moon reaches the top of the aforementioned sphere, it accelerates its course; when it is at the bottom of it, it slows down that same course. 28. I SAY THAT THE SUN HAS TWO SPHERES, ETC. <note>original Latin: "DICO QUOD SOLI BINAE SPHAERAE ET CETERA"</note> That is, I say that there are two spheres belonging to the sun.

The first sphere of these follows the Great Sphere <note>The *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere that dictates the daily rotation of the heavens.</note>, which moves westward and carries the sun with it from the east to the west of the world. And if the movement of that [Great] sphere did not prevent<gloss>3</gloss> it, the sun would spend its own six<gloss>4</gloss> months<gloss>4</gloss> traveling from the west to the east of the world above the earth without being hidden. And it would spend that same length of time traveling from the east of the world to the west without being seen; and thus half of the year would be a single<gloss>5</gloss> day and the other half would be a single night.

The second sphere belonging to the sun is called "the sphere whose center is outside the center of the earth" <term>the eccentric sphere; original: "an speir sa seantrom ata leth amuith da sheantrom na talman"</term>, just like the moon has. The movement of that sphere is from the west to the east of the world. This sphere (and the sun within it) is close to the Earth on one<gloss>7</gloss> side<gloss>7</gloss> and far from the other side. And when the sun is close to the Earth in that sphere, it burns the southern part of the Earth so much that that region is uninhabitable. And when it is furthest<gloss>10</gloss> from the Earth in that same sphere<gloss>11</gloss>, its heat does not reach the Earth; therefore, that much of the northern part of the Earth is uninhabitable due to excessive cold.<gloss>14</gloss>

I will now provide a diagram to show how those two circles of the sun relate to<gloss>15</gloss> one another.

### *Concerning the spheres of the sun.*

28. I say that there are two spheres for the sun, etc. <note>original Latin: "DICO QUOD SOLI BINAE SPHAERAE, ETCETERA"</note> I declare that the sun has two spheres. The first corresponds to the "very great sphere" <note>In geocentric astronomy, this refers to the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere that drives the daily rotation of all heavenly bodies from east to west.</note>, which moves westward and travels from the east to the west of the world. If the movement of that sphere did not control the sun, it would take six full months to journey from the west to the east of the world above the earth without setting. It would spend an equal amount of time moving from the east to the west of the world while invisible <note>meaning, below the horizon</note>, and thus half of the year would be one long day and the other half one long night.

The second sphere of the sun, called the "eccentric sphere," is similar to that of the moon. The movement of this sphere is from the west to the east of the world. This sphere, which contains the sun within it, is closer to the earth on one side and farther from it on the other side. When the sun is near the earth in that sphere, it parches the southern portion of the earth so intensely that the land is uninhabitable. When it is farthest from the earth in the same sphere, its heat does not reach the earth, and a corresponding portion of the northern part of the earth is uninhabitable due to excessive cold.

### The Arrangement of the Ten Spheres.

29. **As Ptolemy says** <note>original Latin: "SICUD DICIT PARTOLOMEUS"</note>: that is, as that philosopher says, we see two types of movement in the firmament <note>The "firmament" refers to the heavens or the sky as a physical structure.</note>. One movement is from east to west, and the other movement is from the west to the east of the world. And the sun, the moon, and each of the other five planets <note>In traditional astronomy, the "five others" are Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> follow the extent of the width of their own spheres in this eastward movement. The westward movement, however, carries the planets along with it to the west; this is contrary to their own natural movement, which is toward the east.

And I say again that the sun, the moon, the other five planets, and all the fixed stars <note>original: "airdrinnacaibh daingne"—the stars that appear to stay in fixed positions relative to each other, unlike the "wandering" planets.</note> have one uniform movement. Among them all, side by side, there is not one star that moves faster or slower than another. And that is why there is no difference at all in the world between the movement of the sun or the moon and the movement of the other stars, for it is certain that they share the same nature and creation. And although it appears that Saturn is slower than the moon—for reasons we will explain again—their movement is actually uniform.

And as Ptolemy and the other philosophers say, there are ten great spheres. The largest of these, called the "very great sphere" <note>The *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere that dictates the daily motion of the heavens.</note>, and the "sphere of the signs" <note>The Zodiac.</note> have the same movement, for they both move westward.

However, the movement of the eight spheres—that is, the sphere of the fixed stars, the sun, the moon, and the other five—is from the west to the east of the world, as we have often said. These spheres are arranged one inside the other. The sphere of the moon is the closest to the earth. Then comes the sphere of Mercury, then Venus, and the sun is on the outside of those. Outside of those are the sphere of Mars, the sphere of Jupiter, and Saturn. Finally, the sphere of the fixed stars is on the outside of all of those. And it is not because they do not move that they are called "fixed stars," for they move from...

### *Concerning the Order of the Ten Spheres.*

29. **As Ptolemy says** <note>original Latin: "SICUT DICIT PTOLEMAEUS"</note>: As that philosopher says, we see two kinds of movement in the firmament <note>The "firmament" refers to the heavens or the sky as a physical structure.</note>—one movement from east to west and the other from the west to the east of the world. The sun, the moon, and each of the other five planets <note>In traditional astronomy, these are Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> move according to the width of their own spheres in this eastward movement. The westward movement, however, carries the planets along with it toward the west in a contrary direction, opposing their natural movement which is toward the east. 

I repeat that the sun, the moon, the other five planets, and all the fixed stars <note>Stars that maintain a permanent position relative to each other, unlike the "wandering" planets.</note> have the same uniform movement. Among them all individually, there is no star that moves more swiftly or more slowly than another. Therefore, there is no difference at all in the world between the movement of the sun and moon and the movement of the other stars, because it is certain that they share the same nature and form. Although Saturn appears to be slower than the moon for the reason I will now explain, their actual speed of movement is equal.

As Ptolemy and the other philosophers declare, there are ten large spheres. The largest of these, which is called the "very great sphere" <note>The *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere that drives the daily rotation of the heavens.</note>, possesses the same movement as the sphere of the signs <note>The Zodiac.</note>, since both move westward. Furthermore, the movement of the other eight spheres—that is, the sphere of the fixed stars and those of the sun, the moon, and the other five planets—is from the west to the east of the world, as I have frequently remarked. 

These spheres are situated within each other; the sphere of the moon is the nearest to the earth, followed by the spheres of Mercury and Venus respectively. The sun is outside of those, with the spheres of Mars and Jupiter outside of the sun, and the sphere of the fixed stars outside of all those. It is not because they do not move that they are called "fixed stars," for...

...from the east to the west of the world, just as the other planets do. This is not only because of their direction, but also because they do not deviate from the north to the south of the firmament as the others do. The Sphere of Signs <note>The Zodiac; the ninth sphere in this system</note> is the ninth sphere. And beyond those, on every side, is the tenth sphere, which is named the "Very Great Sphere" <note>The *Primum Mobile*, or first moved sphere, which drives the rotation of all others</note>, also known as the "Straight Sphere." Below is a diagram <note>original: "fidair"</note> from which all of these may be understood.

I stated above that the Moon <note>original: "an re"</note> appears swifter than Saturn. If the Moon were in Saturn's circle, it would take thirty years to travel it, just as Saturn does. Likewise, if Saturn traveled the circle of the Moon, it would complete it in twenty-eight days and a period of nearly seven weeks <note>The text specifies "seven weeks minus one day," likely a specific calculation regarding the lunar cycle</note>, just as the Moon itself does. Thus, it is the narrowness of the path for one and the vastness of the path for the other that causes these planets to appear fast or slow. In reality, they are not actually so; they are exactly the same in their course, nature, speed, and slowness. 

If you divided the distance from the center of the earth to the sphere of Saturn into three hundred and sixty equal parts <note>original: "tri fichit ⁊ tri ced"</note> and formed each part into a circle, each one would be equal to the sphere of the Moon. And if the Moon’s sphere were expanded, and three hundred and fifty-nine parts <note>original: "nai nurdail deg ⁊ a da fichet ⁊ a tri ced" — 19 + 40 + 300</note> were added to it, and all of that were formed into a sphere, it would be no larger than the sphere of Saturn. This proves that it is the narrowness and vastness of the planets' circles that makes some appear to move quickly and others slowly, though they do not actually differ in speed, as we have often said.

Ptolemy wrote a clear example to explain the two movements mentioned above—from east to west and from west to east across the world. Imagine a wheel spinning from the east to the west of the world throughout the day and night <note>original: "risin la co naiti"</note>. Imagine there was a small circle around the center <note>original: "seantrom"</note> of that wheel, and a circle twice as large as that on the outside of it, and the...

They move from the west of the world to the east, just as the other planets do, but because they do not tilt from the north of the firmament to the south as those others do, they are distinct. The Sphere of the Signs <note>The Zodiac</note> is the ninth sphere, and outside all of these is the tenth sphere called the "Very Great Sphere," or by another name, the "Straight Sphere" <note>original Latin: *orbis rectus*</note>. Displayed here is a figure that represents them all.

I said above that the Moon appears swifter than Saturn. If the Moon were in the orbit of Saturn, it would take thirty years to travel as Saturn travels. Similarly, Saturn would traverse the orbit of the Moon—if he were in it—in twenty-eight days <note>The original text includes "and seven weeks less one day," which is an interpolation (a later addition that likely contains a calculation error)</note> just as the Moon does itself. Thus, it is the narrowness of the orbit they have, or the wideness of the other orbit, which causes the planets within them to appear swift or slow <note>original Latin: *et illud quidem non est nisi per constrictionem orbis luno apud amplitudinem orbis Saturni* — "and indeed, that is only because of the narrowness of the Moon's sphere compared to the breadth of Saturn's sphere"</note>. They are not actually fast or slow, for they have exactly the same course, nature, speed, and slowness. 

If the sphere of Saturn were divided into three hundred and sixty equal parts from the center of the earth, and if each of those parts were given a circular form, each part would be equal to the sphere of the Moon. If the sphere of the Moon were opened out so that it was increased three hundred and sixty fold <note>original Latin: *tercentupla et sexagintupla*</note>, and the whole was made into the shape of a sphere, it would nonetheless be equal to the sphere of Saturn. Thus it is proved that it is the narrowness and the wideness of the orbits of the planets that makes some of them appear to have a swift movement and some a slow movement, although as I have repeatedly stated, such is not the case.

Ptolemy <note>A 2nd-century Greek astronomer whose geocentric model dominated medieval science</note> gave a clear example to explain the two motions I mentioned above: the movement from east to west and from west to east of the world. Imagine that a wheel revolved from the east of the world to the...

<summary>This page explains that the perceived speed of planets is an illusion caused by the size of their orbits; if the Moon were at Saturn's distance, it would move just as slowly. It also identifies the ninth sphere as the Zodiac and the tenth as the "Very Great Sphere" (Primum Mobile).</summary>

The third circle <note>original: *cercall*</note> outside of that is three times larger than the first circle. The fourth circle on the outside of that is four times larger than the first circle, and so on up to the eighth circle. Suppose there were a ball in each one of those circles moving separately from the west to the east of the world. This arrangement <note>original: *rot* (wheel)</note> is like the great sphere of the universe, and those small circles we mentioned are like the internal circles of that great sphere. 

Therefore, when the first circle turns around completely for the first time, the second circle has only completed its second part <note>i.e., half</note> of its revolution, the third circle is at its third part, the fourth circle at its fourth, the fifth at its fifth, the sixth at its sixth, the seventh at its seventh, and the eighth circle at its eighth part. And so, by the time the eighth circle had gone around entirely once, the first circle would have completed eight full revolutions. During the time these eight circles were completing their courses, the wheel <note>the entire system or the outermost sphere</note> would have frequently turned from the east to the west of the world, and the eight circles would have begun their own movement. We have provided this illustration below to sharpen the intellect of the reader.

### Chapter 30

**It should be known that the greatest sphere, etc.** <note>original Latin: *Sciendum est quia maxima spera etc.*</note> That is: you should know that the "Very Great Sphere" is what is called the "Straight Sphere" <note>original: *speir direach*; Latin: *sphaera recta*</note>. The ignorant have held many erroneous opinions about it; they said—because it is the highest, the most excellent, and the swiftest of the spheres—that it is the creator <note>original: *tuismidhteoir* (parent or begetter)</note> of the world. 

It completes its course in a day and a night <note>24 hours</note>, and there are three hundred and sixty degrees of the Zodiac <note>original: *Stodiacus*</note> within it. Moving against this <note>the daily rotation of the 10th sphere</note> is the sphere of the firm fixed stars <note>original: *aird rinnac ndaingin*</note>, which moves from the west to the east of the world, traveling only one degree every hundred years. Each one of the spheres of the planets completes its course according to its own narrowness or its breadth <note>the circumference of its orbit</note>.

**30. IT SHOULD BE KNOWN THAT THE GREATEST SPHERE, ETC.** <note>original Latin: *SCIENDUM EST QUIA MAXIMA SPHAERA, ETCETERA.*</note> Be it known to you that the "very great sphere" is the "straight sphere." <term>straight sphere: likely referring to the *Sphaera Recta*, the celestial sphere as it appears at the equator, or the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost moving shell of the universe.</term> Uninformed persons have offered many erroneous opinions concerning it, for they declared that since it is the highest, loftiest, and swiftest of the spheres, it is the origin of the universe. It completes its course in a day and a night and contains within itself the three hundred and sixty degrees of the Zodiac. The sphere of the fixed stars moves in a contrary direction to this—from the west of the world toward the east—and it takes a thousand years <note>The author writes 1,000 years here, but the footnote corrects this to the more common medieval calculation of 100 years.</note> to move just one degree. Each of the spheres of the planets completes its course according to the narrowness or wideness of its path.

The very great sphere <term>the very great sphere: the *Primum Mobile* or "First Mover," the outermost shell of the universe in medieval cosmology</term>, which surrounds every other sphere on all sides, governs them and causes them to rotate from the east to the west of the world. This is the cause of day and night, of light and darkness, and of the variety of the seasons: spring, summer, autumn, and winter.

Inside this sphere, everything is contained, governed, and moved. All things are moved so that their state, position, or order <note>original: *stait no a suidheochan no a nordochan*</note> must eventually change. This is what makes the planets so changeable in their movements while the earth remains difficult to move. For if the earth were easily moved, it would not maintain its own course through day and night as it does now, nor could the course of the planets and the rotation of the firmament <term>firmament: the sphere of the fixed stars</term> be proven as they are now. Furthermore, there are no stars in that sphere. The ignorant have claimed that there is a soul within it, and that everything receives its life <note>original: *anam*, literally "soul"</note> from it. However, I say that although its power over everything we have mentioned is great, it receives those powers from the Creator <term>Creator: the "Tuismigteoir," literally "Parent" or "Producer"</term> himself. To prove this: one does not know what work a person performs until after it is finished. Therefore, since the knowledge of every action that comes from our "very great sphere" exists before the actions are even performed, those actions are produced by someone else and not by the sphere itself.

### 31. THE SECOND SPHERE AFTER THE GREATEST SPHERE, ETC. <note>original Latin: *SECUNDA SPERA POST MACSIMAM SPERAM ETCETERA.*</note>

This is the second sphere after the very great sphere, and it is closer to the earth. It is called *Zodiace* <term>Zodiace: the Zodiacal sphere</term>, that is, the sphere of the signs. Like the very great sphere, it also contains no stars, and it moves from the east to the west of the world just as the first one does. The ancients believed that this sphere comprised the other nine spheres and that it was merely one of the circles of the very great sphere. However, Ptolemy <note>original: *Tolameus*</note> rejected this opinion in his own books. He says that he found a vast space between the very great sphere and the Zodiac in their zones and poles; for he found the north pole of the Zodiac at... <unclear>c</unclear>

Moreover, the very great sphere <term>the very great sphere: the *Primum Mobile* or "First Mover," the outermost shell of the universe in medieval cosmology</term>, which surrounds all the other spheres on every side, controls them and causes them to revolve from the east of the world to the west. This is the cause of night and day, light and darkness, and the changing of the seasons: spring, summer, autumn, and winter.

Inside this sphere, everything is protected, controlled, and set in motion to prevent things from ever changing <note>original: "lest at any time they might change"</note> their state, position, or order. This is what causes the planets to revolve so easily while the earth remains immovable. For if the earth were to move, day or night could not maintain their proper cycles as they do now, and the paths of the planets and the spheres of the firmament <term>firmament: the sphere of the fixed stars</term> could not be calculated as they are now. There are no stars in that sphere. The ill-informed have claimed that it possesses life and that everything receives life from it. However, I declare that as great as its powers are over everything I have mentioned, it receives those powers from its own Creator. To prove this: it is not known what work a person performs until it has taken effect. Therefore, since we know every action produced by the "very great sphere" before it has even happened, those actions are performed by some other Being and do not come from the sphere itself.

The Zodiac—that is, the sphere of the signs—is the second sphere after the very great sphere and is closer to the earth than the latter. This sphere is also without stars, just as I mentioned the very great sphere was, and it moves from the east of the world to the west just like the first one. The ancients imagined that there are nine spheres in...

[The northern pole of the Zodiac is] twenty-four degrees higher than the same pole of the very great sphere <term>the very great sphere: the *Primum Mobile*, or the outermost shell of the universe</term>. And the southern pole of the Zodiac <note>original: *Stodiace*</note> was found to be another twenty-four degrees below the same pole of the very great sphere. The circles of this sphere are twenty-four degrees apart at the summit of the firmament. Because of this, Ptolemy <note>original: *Tolameus*</note> established ten spheres <note>The Irish text mentions ten spheres here, though medieval systems varied between nine, ten, or eleven; the translator notes some confusion in the original manuscript's numbering</note>; and it is of the ninth sphere we speak when we say the sun, the moon, or another planet is in a certain degree of the signs of that sphere.

And the reason these names—namely Aries <note>original: *Airgeis*</note>, Taurus <note>original: *Taubhrus*</note>, Leo, and so on—were given to the signs of the Zodiac is because the constellations <note>original: *hairrdrinnaigh*</note> in the sphere of the stars correspond to those parts of the Zodiac named Aries or Taurus, matching the appearance and nature of those same animals we have here. Yet, there is no physical form at all in the Zodiac itself, for as we have said, there are no stars within it. <note>The author distinguishes between the "signs" (mathematical divisions of the 9th sphere) and the "constellations" (actual groups of stars in the 8th sphere).</note>

The philosophers <note>original: *feallsamain*</note> divided the Zodiac into twelve parts. They named each part a "sign" according to the name and image of the thing that lies directly beneath that sign in the sphere of the stars. In the same way, they divided the year into twelve parts according to those twelve signs of the sun, calling the sun's passage through each sign a "month." From this, the philosophers taught that the diversity of the seasons occurs according to the sun’s journey from sign to sign, and according to its rising and falling. For when the sun enters the first point of Aries, the equinox <note>original: *exinocsium*</note> occurs—that is, the equality of day and night—and that is when spring <note>original: *terrach*</note> begins. Spring continues until the sun is in Gemini <note>original: *nGemen*</note>. When the sun enters Cancer <note>original: *Cainnser*</note>, it is the beginning of summer <note>original: *samradh*</note>. Because the sun is then at the highest point of its sphere, it greatly heats the face of the earth; and when it reaches the final point of Virgo <note>original: *Uirgo*</note>, it brings summer to an end.

...all, and that the Zodiac is one of the orbits of the very great sphere <term>the very great sphere: the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost moving sphere in the medieval universe</term>. Ptolemy refutes this theory in his own book, stating that he found a great difference between the very great sphere and the Zodiac regarding their zones and poles. He found the north pole of the Zodiac to be twenty-four degrees higher than the same pole in the very great sphere, and he found the south pole of the Zodiac to be another twenty-four degrees below the same pole in the very great sphere <note>Ptolemy is describing the "obliquity of the ecliptic," the tilt of the Earth's axis (or in his view, the sun's path) relative to the celestial equator, which he measured as roughly 24 degrees (modern measurement is approx. 23.5°)</note>. Furthermore, the zones of this sphere are twenty-four degrees apart at the top of the firmament. Therefore, Ptolemy establishes ten spheres, and we are referring to the ninth sphere when we say that the sun, moon, or any other planet is in a certain degree of the signs of that sphere.

The reason that these names—*Aries* <note>original: the Ram</note>, *Taurus* <note>original: the Bull</note>, *Leo* <note>original: the Lion</note>, and so on—are applied to the signs of the Zodiac is because the constellations in the sphere of the stars <note>the 8th sphere of fixed stars</note> lying opposite those portions of the Zodiac correspond in shape and nature to the animals we have here on Earth. However, there is no physical figure or shape at all within the Zodiac itself because, as I have already stated, there are no stars located within it.

The philosophers divided the Zodiac into twelve parts and called each part a "sign," naming it after the constellation that lies directly behind that sign in the sphere of the stars. Similarly, they divided the year into twelve parts according to these twelve signs of the sun, and called the sun's passage through each sign a "month." These philosophers taught that the change of seasons occurs according to the sun’s course from sign to sign, and according to its elevation or depression in the sky. For instance, when the sun enters the first point of *Aries*, an equinox occurs—that is, an equality of day and night <note>original: *aequinoctium*</note>—and then spring begins, lasting until the sun is in *Gemini*. When the sun enters *Cancer*, that marks the beginning of summer. When the sun is there, at the highest point of its sphere above us, it heats the surface of the earth intensely; and when it arrives at the final point of *Virgo*, it brings the summer to an end...

And this is the reason why one winter is colder than another, one winter is wetter than another, and one winter is drier than another—and why one summer is hotter and drier than another. That is to say, it is the sun that creates spring, summer, autumn, and winter, and the other planets influence these same seasons. When the sun’s summer arrives while the other planets are in the signs of their own winter, there is a great deal of rain and cold during that summer. Conversely, when the sun’s winter arrives and the other planets are in the signs that manifest their own summer, there is specifically wind and a little rain and cold in that winter. It is the same for the other seasons; this is the cause of the heat, cold, dryness, and wetness of the four seasons of the year—namely, according to the movement of the planets in the signs of the Zodiac <note>original: *an Stodiace*</note>, just as the blessed Creator <note>original: *an tuismighteoir benaithi*</note> himself ordained them.

### 32. **I SAY THAT SATURN [MOVES] BY FOUR, ETC.** <note>original Latin: *Dico quod Saturnus per quatuor etc.*</note>
That is to say, I say that just as Saturn has four movements, he also has four spheres in which he moves. The first movement among them is the very great sphere <note>the *Primum Mobile*</note> that moves from the east to the west of the world. The second movement is its own natural movement from the west to the east of the world. The third movement is the movement of the sphere which is not fixed itself, in which the planet travels either directly forward or backward, quickly or slowly. The fourth movement is the movement of the sphere whose center is located outside the center of the earth <term>eccentric: an orbital circle whose center is not the Earth, used in medieval astronomy to explain why planets appear to change speed and distance</term>. It is by this sphere that every planet is raised to the greatest possible distance above the earth and lowered to the closest possible distance to the earth.

The reason why one winter is colder than another, one winter wetter than another, and one winter drier than another—and similarly, why one summer is hotter or drier than another—is because the sun is the primary cause of spring, summer, autumn, and winter, while the other planets also influence these same seasons <note>The text suggests that while the sun dictates the calendar season, the positions of other planets affect the actual weather experienced.</note>. **I SAY THAT SATURN [MOVES] BY FOUR [MOTIONS], ETC.** <note>original Latin: *Dico quod Saturnus per quattuor, etcetera.*</note> I declare, since Saturn has four motions, that it has four spheres in which it moves. The fourth motion is the movement of the **eccentric sphere** <term>eccentric sphere: in medieval astronomy, a circular orbit whose center is not the Earth, used to explain why planets vary in distance and speed</term>. It is by this motion that every planet is raised as high as possible from the earth or lowered as near as possible to...

...Earth. And these are the four motions that all the planets have, except for the sun, which has [only] two spheres and two motions.

I will write again about those four spheres along with their own motions. First, I will make a figure <note>original: fidair; a diagram or illustration</note> of the great sphere with the figure of the Earth in its center. I will place **A** in the east, **B** at the top, **C** in the west, and **D** at the bottom. The motion of the great sphere is from **A** to **B**, from **B** to **C**, from **C** to **D**, and from **D** back to **A**.

And I will make a figure of the second sphere, which moves from the west to the east of the world. This sphere is beneath the great sphere and is in a straight line under the Zodiac <note>original: Stodiaca</note>. The Zodiac is tilted, while the "very great sphere" is directly above us; this is because, as we have said, their poles and their axes <note>original: ludrach; refers to the hinges or pivots of the celestial spheres</note> are far apart from one another.

And I will make a figure of the third sphere, whose center <note>original: seantrom</note> is outside the center of the Earth, but located inside the two previous spheres. The location of the center of this sphere is to the south of the Earth's center by two and a half degrees, according to the measurement of the sphere's width, which is divided into one hundred and twenty parts. This sphere is close to the Earth on one side and far from it on the other side.

And I will make a figure of the fourth sphere, which firmly holds the body of the planet within it, inside the other three spheres. The center of that star <note>The text uses "star" (retlainne) to refer to the planet Saturn</note> is the same as the center of the sphere in which it resides. It moves from the west to the east of the world. At the top of this sphere—the one with a center that is outside the center of the Earth—the center of that planet is fixed like a firm, immovable nail <note>original: tairngi</note> in its sphere. The planets do not follow a straight course like an arrow; rather, they follow a natural circular course, like a wagon wheel traveling from the west to the east of the world. If there were a nail in the upper rim of the wagon [wheel], it would travel from the west to the east of the world as it goes down toward the Earth; it would not move [independently] west or east, and when it reached the Earth, it would continue its course...

...the earth; and these are the four motions that all the planets have, except the sun, which has two spheres and two motions.

I will again describe those four spheres together with their motions. First, I will create a diagram of the very great sphere <note>The "very great sphere" refers to the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost sphere in geocentric cosmology that provides the daily rotation of the heavens.</note>, with the figure of the earth in the middle of it. I will place **A** in the east, **B** at the top, **C** in the west, and **D** at the bottom. Thus, the motion of the very great sphere is from **A** to **B**, from **B** to **C**, from **C** to **D**, and from **D** back to **A**.

I will create a diagram of the second sphere, which moves from the west of the world to the east. This sphere is located under the very great sphere and in a straight line beneath the Zodiac. The Zodiac is oblique <note>meaning tilted or at an angle relative to the celestial equator</note>, while the very great sphere above it is straight because, as I mentioned, its poles and its pivot <note>original likely referring to the axis of rotation</note> are far apart.

I will create a diagram of the third sphere, the eccentric sphere, inside the two preceding spheres. The center of this sphere is south of the center of the earth <note>original Latin: *S. recedit a medio eiius*, meaning "it recedes from its center"</note> by two and a half degrees, according to the measurement of the diameter of the sphere, and is divided into one hundred and twenty parts; <note>This detail is a later addition to the original text.</note> and this sphere is near the earth on one side and distant from it on another.

I will create a diagram of the fourth sphere <note>In Ptolemaic astronomy, this is the epicycle.</note>, which firmly contains the body of the planet within itself, inside the other three spheres. The center of that star forms the center of the sphere in which it resides, and it moves from the west to the east of the world. At the top of the eccentric sphere, the center of these planets is fixed like a firm, immovable nail in a sphere. The planets do not follow a straight course like that of an arrow; instead, they have a circular, natural course like that of a cartwheel, moving from the west of the world to the east. If there were a nail in the upper rim of a cartwheel moving from the west of the world to the east, while the nail moved downwards towards the...

This is the reason that causes the planets to move in a direct path <note>original: *sibal direc*</note> at one time, and backwards at another, and to move rapidly at one time and slowly at another.

## 33. AFTER THE MOTION OF SATURN'S SPHERE, ETC.
<note>original Latin title: *Postquam Saturne sperus motus etcetera*</note>

That is to say, after we have described the sphere and the movement of Saturn and the other planets, we will now explain how they turn backward. This is called **retrograde** <note>original Latin: *retrogradcio*</note>, which means a "back-turning" <note>original Irish: *caissimpodh*</note>; this is the time when the planet appears to move backward from Aries toward Pisces.

To explain this, I will create a diagram <note>original: *fidair*</note> of Saturn's sphere. On the outside, I will place the sphere of the signs <note>the Zodiac</note> and divide it into twelve parts. After that, I will place Saturn's sphere inside it, the center <note>original: *seantrom*</note> of which is outside the center of the earth. At the very top, I will firmly place the sphere <note>the epicycle</note> that contains the body of the planet.

Next, I will place the earth in its own position with **E** at its center. I will place Saturn in four small circles <note>representing the epicycle at different stages</note> around its own main circle <note>the deferent</note>. I will place the first of those small circles at the summit of its own circle, with a line through it between Aries and Pisces. I will place the second circle at the position of the "first station" <note>original: *comhnaighe*</note> with **B** at its center. The third circle will be at the position of the "back-turn" <note>retrograde</note> with **C** at its center. The fourth circle will be at the position of the "second station" with **D** at its center.

I will then draw three lines from the center of the earth up through Saturn toward the likeness of the signs in the sphere of the fixed stars. These lines represent the line of sight from the earth toward Saturn. I will draw the straightest of these lines...

...earth, it would move neither west nor east. When it reaches the point closest to the earth, its course would incline from east to west; and when it rises up away from the earth, it would move neither west nor east. However, when it reaches the extreme top <note>the apogee</note>, it then moves eastward. This is what causes the planets to move in a direct path at one time and a backward path at another, and to move swiftly at one time and slowly at another.

## 33. AFTER THE MOTION OF SATURN'S SPHERE, ETC.
<note>original Latin title: *Postquam Saturni Sphaera Motus, Etcetera*</note>

Having spoken of the sphere and motion of <term>Saturn</term> and the other planets, I shall now explain how they turn backward. This is called **retrograde** <note>original Latin: *retrogressio*</note>, which means a "back-turning"—that is, when the planet appears to move backward from <term>Aries</term> to <term>Pisces</term> <note>original: *Piscis*</note>.

To explain this, I will create a diagram of the two spheres of Saturn. Outside of these, I will place the sphere of the signs <note>the Zodiac</note> and divide it into twelve parts. Inside of that, I will place the **eccentric sphere** <note>a circular path whose center is not the Earth</note> of Saturn. At the top of this, I will place the sphere <note>the epicycle</note> in which the body of the planet is fixed. Then, I will place the earth in its own position with **E** at its center, and Saturn in four small circles <note>representing the planet's position on its epicycle at different times</note> around its own circumference.

I will place the first of those small circles at the top of its own circle, with a line passing through it between <term>Aries</term> and <term>Pisces</term>. I will place the second circle at the first **stationary point** <note>original: "stopping place"</note>, with **B** at its center. The third circle will be at the place where it turns backward, with **C** at its center. The fourth circle will be at the second stationary point, with **D** at its center. I will then draw three lines from the center of the earth up through Saturn to the figure of the signs which are in the **sphere of the fixed stars**. These lines represent the line of sight from the earth toward Saturn. Finally, I will draw the vertical line upward...

<sig>I</sig>

<summary>This page explains the phenomenon of retrograde motion, where a planet appears to move backward through the zodiac. It describes a geometric model using eccentric spheres and epicycles to account for why planets like Saturn change speed and direction.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, retrograde motion, epicycle, eccentric sphere, Aries, Pisces, stationary point, medieval astronomy, planetary model</keywords>
