<meta>This page continues the technical description of planetary motion, using a lettered diagram to explain stationary points and retrograde movement, specifically focusing on Saturn as the primary example before applying these rules to other planets.</meta>

...from the earth up towards **A**. And so it is that when **Saturn** is at point **A** at the beginning of **Aries**, it is in the **Zodiac** <note>original: *Stodiaca*</note> and its movement is perfectly straight toward the east. As it continues its eastward journey through the specific degrees of Aries, it inclines toward **B**. When it arrives there, it moves neither east nor west; for this reason, this is called its **first stationary point** <note>original: *ced comhnaighi*; the point where a planet appears to stop before changing direction</note>. When it leaves that position, it moves westward toward **D**, and that is its place of **retrograde** <note>original: *caisimpoid*; "turning back"</note>. At that stage, its movement is directly from the east toward the west of the world. While moving westward, when it reaches point **C**, that is its **second stationary point** <note>original: *dara comnaighi*</note>, because it then moves neither east nor west. 

If anyone were to look up then from the center of the earth, they would find that Saturn—which was previously in Aries—is now in **Pisces**. After leaving **D** in the east, it gradually rises again until it reaches **A**. This is also why the planets appear larger at some times than others: when they turn back toward the earth, they appear more prominent <note>original: *aibsigi*; literally "more ghostly" or "more vast," referring here to apparent size</note> to the sight.

Everything we have said regarding the spheres, movements, and every other natural property of Saturn should also be understood to apply to **Jupiter** and **Mars**, since there is no difference <note>original: *deichfer*</note> between them in their travel, their motion, or their effects. The same things happen to the three planets that are located below the sun <note>the inner planets: Venus, Mercury, and the Moon</note>, although these things are not as visible in the **Moon** because of the extreme speed of its sphere and its **eccentric center** <note>original: *seantrom ata leth amuith do seantrom na talman*; a center located outside the center of the earth</note>. This is because the sphere that holds the Moon firmly moves toward the east, but when it performs its "back-turning" <note>retrograde</note>, it moves toward the west. For this reason, that turning is not obvious; nevertheless, its other movements and stationary points are clear, for there are days when it travels twelve degrees, and other days when it travels fourteen degrees.

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.

### Chapter 33.

If one looks from the Earth toward point A, when Saturn is at position A, it is at the beginning of Aries in the Zodiac, moving eastward in a full and direct path. When it has moved several degrees eastward into Aries, it tilts toward point B; when it arrives there, it appears to move neither east nor west. Therefore, that is its first stationary point <term>halt</term>. When it departs from that position, it moves back toward point D, which is the period of its retrogression <term>backward motion</term>. There, it moves directly from the east of the world toward the west. As it moves westward and reaches position C, it reaches its second stationary point <term>halt</term>, because there it again appears to move neither east nor west. Anyone looking up from the center of the Earth would then find that same Saturn in Pisces <note>original: "Piscis"</note>, even though it was previously in Aries. After leaving point D in the east, it will gradually ascend again toward point A. This explains why the planets appear larger at some times than at others; as they move in retrogression toward the Earth, they appear at their largest size <note>In medieval astronomy, it was believed that planets physically drew nearer to the Earth during their retrograde loops, making them appear larger and brighter.</note>.

Everything I have said concerning the spheres, motions, and every other quality which Saturn possesses should also be understood as applying to Jupiter and Mars, since there is no difference between them in their paths, their motions, or their actions. These three planets that are "above" the Sun <note>In the Ptolemaic system, the "superior planets" (Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn) were located in spheres further from the Earth than the Sun's sphere.</note> experience the same phenomena. However, these movements are not as evident in the Moon because of the extreme speed of its eccentric sphere <term>eccentric sphere</term>. This is because the sphere which holds the Moon firmly moves eastward, and when it turns on its backward course, it moves westward. Therefore, that specific revolution is not easily seen, even though its general course and stationary points are apparent, as it moves twelve degrees on one day and fourteen degrees on another.

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.

### Concerning the sphere of the fixed stars:

34. **THIS SPHERE IS SITUATED IN THE EIGHTH PLACE, ETC.** <note>original Latin: Hec spero octauo situatur in loco etcetera</note>. That is to say, this sphere is positioned in the eighth place in the firmament. It is formed in the likeness of Aries <note>original: Airgeis</note>, Taurus <note>original: Tabrus</note>, Gemini <note>original: Geimin</note>, and the other signs of the Zodiac <note>original: Stodiace</note>. The stars of this sphere move in a manner that is perfectly equal, constant, and uniform in relation to one another at all times, moving perpetually from the west of the world toward the east. They do not experience changes in speed or slowness, nor do they move in a straight line or turn back <note>referring to retrograde motion</note> as the wandering stars <term>planets</term> do. It takes one hundred years for these stars to travel just one degree of the great sphere.

35. **GEOMETRIC PROOFS ARE REQUIRED TO INVESTIGATE THESE MATTERS** <note>original Latin: Ad hec indiganda geomitrica sunt</note>. To investigate this subject, it is necessary to obtain geometric proofs <note>original: arrmainnte geomitric</note> so that we may believe it without doubt. Therefore, let us create a diagram of the Earth: place point **E** at the center and draw another circle from its north to its south. Draw a straight line from the North Pole <note>original: Pol Airtic</note> to the South Pole <note>original: Pol Antairtic</note> passing through the Earth and through the circle of the Earth. Place point **A** at the summit of the firmament, **B** at the northern pole of the circle, **C** at the bottom, and **D** at the southern pole.

If someone were at point **E** holding an astrolabe <note>a medieval instrument used to determine the altitude of celestial bodies</note> in their hand—since it is with that tool that a full and certain knowledge of this matter is gained—and if they placed their face along the center line of the astrolabe, holding it hanging from the thumb, and looked at the North Pole through the two holes of the sighting plates, they would find that the pole is level with the horizon. If they then traveled sixty-six and two-thirds miles from **E** toward **B** and placed the astrolabe toward the North Pole again, looking through it as they did before, they would find that the pole has risen by one degree in altitude.

### Chapter 34-35

*Concerning the sphere of the fixed stars.*

34. **THIS SPHERE IS SITUATED IN THE EIGHTH PLACE, AND SO ON.** <note>original Latin: Haec sphaera octavo situatur in loco, etcetera</note>. This sphere is positioned at the eighth level in the firmament and is patterned after the constellations of Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and the other signs of the Zodiac <term>Zodiac: the belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs</term>. The stars in this sphere move with perfect regularity, remaining at equal distances from one another at all times, moving perpetually from the west of the world toward the east. Unlike the "wandering stars" <term>planets</term>, they do not speed up or slow down, nor do they follow a direct course or move backward <note>referring to retrograde motion</note>. It takes one hundred years for these stars to travel just one degree of the great sphere <note>This refers to the "precession of the equinoxes," which the author estimates at 1 degree per century</note>.

35. **GEOMETRIC PROOFS ARE REQUIRED TO INVESTIGATE THESE MATTERS.** <note>original Latin: Ad haec indicanda geometrica sunt</note>. To study this subject, it is necessary to use geometric arguments which we can rely on without doubt. I will create a diagram of the Earth: I will place point **E** at its center, and I will draw another circle from north to south. Then, I will draw a straight line from the North Pole <note>original: Arctic Pole</note> to the South Pole <note>original: Antarctic Pole</note> passing through the Earth and through its circumference <note>original: orbit</note>. I will place point **A** at the summit of the firmament, **B** at the northern pole of the circle, **C** at the bottom, and **D** at the southern pole.

Therefore, whoever is at position **E** and takes an astrolabe <term>astrolabe: an ancient instrument used to measure the inclined position of celestial bodies</term> in their hand—for this tool provides certain knowledge of this matter—should hold it suspended by a thread from their thumb. By looking through the two holes of its sighting plates along the middle line and observing the North Pole, they would find that pole level with the horizon. If you then travel sixty-six and two-thirds miles <note>original: three score six and two-thirds</note> from point **E** to point **B** and then aim the astrolabe toward the North Pole as you did before, you would find the pole to be six degrees in height above the Earth.

...there above the earth; and the astrolabe shows that there is one single degree out of the three hundred and sixty degrees <note>original: three score and three hundred degrees</note>.

And if you move again from there toward point **B**, traveling another sixty-six and two-thirds miles, and place the astrolabe toward that same pole—observing as you did before—you would find that the pole has risen two degrees in height above you. And so it continues forever from **E** toward **B**: you would find at the end of every sixty-six and two-thirds miles, until you reach **B**, that the height of that same Pole increases by one degree. When all those miles are totaled along one path, they amount to twenty-four thousand miles, corresponding to the three hundred and sixty degrees that surround the sphere of the Earth. This is the measurement of the circumference of the circle of water and earth.

Furthermore, the *alcoterra* <note>original: alcoterra; from the Arabic *al-qutr*, meaning diameter</note>—that is, the thickness of the Earth’s circle—is eight thousand miles. Consequently, it is four thousand miles to the center of the Earth. It is calculated that three and one-seventh <note>original: tri mili ⁊ seachtadh; this refers to the Archimedean approximation of Pi (22/7) used to relate diameter to circumference</note> is the proper proportion for each of those miles.

### 36. THE ANCIENTS IMAGINED A LINE FROM EAST TO WEST, AND SO ON. <note>original Latin: Antiqui linea ab oriente ad occidentem etcetera</note>. 
The ancient scholars imagined a line passing directly through the center of the Earth from its eastern edge to its western edge, corresponding to the equinoctial line <term>equinoctial line: the celestial equator</term>. They left us the knowledge that the distance from this line to the North Pole <note>original: Pol Airtic</note> is the same as the distance from it to the South Pole <note>original: Pol Intartic</note>.

The inhabited part of the Earth is located between that line and the North Pole, although not all of that region is habitable. However, from that same line toward the South Pole, no living creature in the world can survive because of the excessive heat. For since the center of the sun's sphere is positioned outside the center of the Earth, the body of the sun is carried around...

### 36. THE LINE OF THE ANCIENTS FROM EAST TO WEST, ETC.
<note>original: ANTIQUI LINEA AB ORIENTE AD OCCIDENTEM, ETCETERA</note>

The ancients imagined a line passing through the middle of the Earth, running directly from east to west, coinciding with the equinoctial line <note>the Equator</note>. They passed down the knowledge to us that this line is at an equal distance from both the Arctic <note>North</note> and the Antarctic <note>South</note> Poles.

The habitable part of the Earth lies between that line and the Arctic Pole, although that entire region is not actually habitable. No living thing can exist on Earth from the equinoctial line toward the Antarctic Pole because of the excessive heat. This is because the body of the sun is carried around the Earth within the eccentric sphere <note>in the Ptolemaic system, an orbital path where the Earth is slightly off-center, explaining variations in the sun's apparent speed and distance</note>...

...of the earth. And because the tilt of that sphere is toward that side, it must be that this side is much hotter than any other side of the earth. However, the heat on that side is only slightly greater than the cold on the opposite side. Because of this, at the far end of the northern part of the earth, due to the sun's great distance from it, there is nothing but a fullness of dark clouds, wind, rain, frost, snow, and extreme cold. Consequently, that place is uninhabitable, whereas the place situated under the equinoctial line <note>the Equator</note> is temperate.

In that place, the days and nights of the year are exactly equal in length to each other. From that equinoctial line to the northern uninhabitable region lies the portion of the earth that can be inhabited. The ancients divided that habitable part into seven zones <note>original: "codcanna," referring to the "Climates" of ancient geography</note> extending in length from the east to the west of the earth, as this figure shows.

->**On the nature of those zones.**<-

The first climate <note>the first of the seven latitudinal zones</note> begins in width from the equinoctial line and extends in length, as we have said, from the east to the west of the world. In this zone, the longest day does not exceed exactly twelve hours and two-thirds of an hour twice a year, and the shortest day is never less than eleven hours and one-third of an hour. The sun is directly overhead for the people of that region twice a year—specifically, when it moves from the south to the north of the firmament, and from there back to the south again. Thus, there are two summers in a single year in that region. Furthermore, the shadows in that region never slant permanently toward the north or the south <note>because the sun passes directly overhead, shadows can fall in either direction depending on the time of year</note>.

The nature of the second climate: the excessive sand of that region makes it extremely hot, for the heat of the sun penetrates the sand and scorches and burns the surface of the earth. When a great wind arises there, it gathers the sand and...

And since that sphere <note>original: "sphere"; referring here to the celestial sphere or the sun's path</note> inclines toward that side, that side of the earth must necessarily be much hotter than any other, and the heat on that side is nearly as extreme as the cold on the opposite side. Consequently, in the extreme northern part of the earth, because the sun is so far away from it, there is nothing but many dark clouds, much wind and rain, frost, snow, and excessive cold. For that reason, that place is uninhabitable, while the part along the equinoctial line <term>the equator</term> is temperate.

In that place, the days and nights of the year are of exactly equal length. The habitable portion of the earth extends from that line along the equator as far as the uninhabitable district in the north. ->*Concerning the nature of those lands.*<-

The first zone begins at the equinoctial line in terms of latitude, and extends in longitude—as I mentioned—from the east of the world to the west. In this region, the longest day does not exceed twelve hours and two-thirds, which occurs exactly twice a year, and the shortest day is no less than eleven hours and one-third. Twice a year, the sun passes directly over the inhabitants of that region—that is, when it moves from the south of the firmament <term>the heavens</term> toward the north, and from there back toward the south again. Consequently, there are two summers in a single year in that region. In that area, from the north to the south of it, the shadow never inclines <note>meaning the sun is directly overhead at noon, so shadows do not lean toward the north or south</note>.

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

### The nature of the fifth zone.
The heat here is less and the cold is greater than in the region <note>original: "cric"</note> previously mentioned. Nevertheless, trees are more numerous and the yield of the fields is better. The inhabitants of this region have well-proportioned bodies and a mixed complexion, being closer to white than to swarthy <note>original: "riabac," meaning brindled, grayish, or tanned</note>. They possess less wisdom and have shorter lifespans, yet they are more prosperous <term>wealthy or affluent</term> than the people of the previous region. In this zone, the day grows to sixteen hours at its longest and decreases to eight hours at its shortest.

### The nature of the sixth zone.
The heat is less and the cold is greater, and the fruit of the trees and fields is diminished because of the intense cold compared to the previous regions. Snow, rain, clouds, wells, streams, hills, and mountains are abundant in this region. The inhabitants of this zone have weak bodies, a white complexion, and straight hair; they are brutish <term>animal-like in behavior</term> and difficult to teach. In this region, the longest day is seventeen hours and the shortest day is eight and a half hours.

### The nature of the seventh zone.
This region suffers from a lack of heat and a great capacity for cold. The inhabitants of this region are un-intellectual <note>The original Irish "indtleachtach" usually means intellectual, but the context here and in contemporary sources suggests the opposite or a scribal error</note>, difficult to teach, with weak understanding and animal-like memories. They have weak bodies and straight, yellowish-fair hair. If the inhabitants of this region were to go into the first or second zone, or if the people of those regions came here, they would all die from the change of air <term>the sudden shift in climate and atmosphere</term>.

### [Conclusion on the temperate zones]
The fourth region is the most temperate and the best of them all, because the temperance of the heavens <note>original: "mesaracht nimhe," referring to celestial balance</note> nurtures that region more than any other. In this region, the longest day is eighteen hours and the shortest day is six hours.

### The nature of the fifth zone.
The heat here is less and the cold is greater than in the preceding region. Despite this, trees are more plentiful and the crops of the fields are more excellent. The inhabitants of this country have medium-sized bodies; their complexion is neutral, being closer to white than to swarthy <note>original: "swarthy" refers to a dark or tanned complexion</note>. Their intelligence is lower and their lives are shorter, though they are wealthier than the people of the previous climate. ### The nature of the sixth zone.
The heat here is less and the cold is greater than in the preceding regions. The produce of the trees and fields is diminished because of the intense cold. Snow, rain, clouds, wells, rivers, hills, and mountains are very common in this region. The inhabitants have weak bodies, fair complexions, and smooth hair, though they are savage and uncouth <term>uncultured or lacking refinement</term>. The longest day in this region lasts seventeen hours, and the shortest day lasts eight and a half hours.

### The nature of the seventh zone: Lack of heat and excess cold.
The inhabitants of this region are cunning <note>original: "crafty (?)"</note> and uncouth, with weak minds, brutish <term>animal-like</term> memories, and weak bodies. They have smooth, fair, yellow hair. If the inhabitants of this region traveled to the first or second regions, or if the inhabitants of those regions came to this one, both would die because of the extreme change in climate.

Therefore, the fourth region is the most temperate and is the best of all when everything is considered, for the mildness of the heavens nourishes that region more than any other. The longest day in that region <insert>the seventh</insert> is eighteen hours, and the shortest is six hours.

### Concerning the places where the whole year consists of one day and one night.

37. **WE KNOW THAT THERE ARE TWO PLACES ON EARTH, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "DUO IN TERRA LOCA ESSE NOUIMUS ETCETERA"</note> That is, there are two places known to us on Earth: one of them lies in a direct line under the Arctic Pole, and the second place lies in a direct line under the Antarctic Pole, where the whole year consists of a single day and a single night. For there are six months of one continuous day and another six months of one continuous night. And if anyone were to be at the northern extremity of the Earth, in the place where the Arctic Pole is—that is, the northern axis <term>ludrac: a pivot or axis of rotation</term> of the heavens <note>original: "firmaminti"; the sky or celestial sphere</note> in a direct line above their head—they would see the circle of the direct line, which is similar to the circle of the signs <note>the zodiac signs</note> around them. And they would see, in that way, the movement of the heavens like the rotation of a millstone <term>bron: a quern or millstone used to grind grain</term>.

And so, when the sun enters the direct line in the first part of Aries, it rises in the east below the Earth for that aforementioned place and creates a day for it. It circles around like a millstone and turns from east to south, from south to west, from west to north, and from north again to the east. It continues this way, constantly traveling around at nearly the same level <note>original: "a nen ceime" (in one degree); the sun appears to circle the horizon rather than rising high into the sky</note> until it reaches Cancer. It is then at the highest point <note>the Summer Solstice</note> it can reach above that place, and it divides that long day into two equal parts. From then on, it begins to descend bit by bit until it reaches the end of the day and then takes its light away from the aforementioned place. Thus, there is daylight in that place from the middle of March until the middle of September.

And when the sun enters the first point of Libra <note>the Autumn Equinox</note>, the night begins to darken that same place. The sun is then traveling around in a circle like a millstone, gradually sinking lower below the horizon until it reaches the first point of...

### Chapter 37

## Concerning the places where the whole year is one day and one night.

37. **WE KNOW THAT THERE ARE TWO PLACES ON EARTH, ETC.** <note>original Latin: "DUO IN TERRA LOCA ESSE NOVIMUS, ETCETERA"</note> We know of two places on the Earth: one lies directly beneath the Arctic Pole, and the other lies directly beneath the Antarctic Pole. In these places, the entire year consists of only one day and one night, because six months make up a single day, and the other six months make up a single night. 

Anyone who stood at the extreme north of the Earth, at the spot where the Arctic Pole—that is, the axis <term>original: "pivot (?)"; likely referring to the celestial pole or axis of rotation</term> of the northern heavens <term>firmament: the sphere of the stars</term>—was directly above them, would see the circle of the horizon coinciding with the circle of the zodiac signs <note>the path of the sun through the stars</note> all around them. They would observe the movement of the heavens rotating like a millstone <term>quern: a hand-powered stone mill for grinding grain</term>. 

Consequently, when the sun enters the celestial equator at the first point of Aries <note>the Spring Equinox in March</note>, it rises on the horizon <note>original: "in the east under the earth"</note> relative to that place. It then begins the long day, revolving around the observer like a millstone, turning from east to south, from south to west, from west to north, and back to the east again. The sun continues this constant revolution at nearly the same height until it arrives at the sign of Cancer <note>the Summer Solstice in June</note>. At that point, being at the highest elevation it can reach above that location, it divides that long six-month day into two equal parts. From then on, it gradually sinks lower until it reaches the end of the day, at which point the light leaves that place. Thus, there is continuous daylight in that location from the middle of March until the middle of September.

When the sun enters the first point of Libra <note>the Autumn Equinox in September</note>, night begins to fall upon that same place. The sun then moves in a circuit, again like a millstone, but sinking gradually below the horizon <note>original: "underneath the earth"</note>, until he...

...Capricorn <note>the Winter Solstice in December</note>, and it cannot sink any lower than that; that point is the middle of that great night <note>original: "aithi"</note>. Then, the sun begins to rise gradually from that position until it reaches the first point of Aries <note>the Spring Equinox in March</note>, and day begins again in that location we mentioned. Thus, there is one long night in that place from the middle of September until the middle of March.

In the same way, if anyone were in the southern part of the earth at the point where the Antarctic Pole—that is, the axis <term>ludrach: literally a pivot or hinge</term> of the heavens—was directly above them, they would see the circle of the horizon <term>line dhirigh: literally "the straight line"</term> rotating like a millstone <term>quern: a stone mill</term> above them. When the sun enters the celestial equator at the first point of Libra <note>the Autumn Equinox in September</note>, it would rise in the east on the horizon for anyone in that place, and day would begin for them. The sun would revolve around them like a millstone, moving from the east to the north, from the north to the west, from the west to the south, and from the south back to the east of the heavens.

Thus, the sun continues to revolve without setting for that location until it reaches the first point of Capricorn <note>the Winter Solstice in December</note>. When it is at that point, it reaches its highest possible elevation for that place, marking the midpoint of that long day. It then gradually begins to sink lower until it reaches the final point of Pisces <note>late winter/early spring</note>, bringing the day to an end. This day lasts from the middle of September until the middle of March. 

Then, when the sun reaches the first point of Aries <note>the Spring Equinox in March</note>, the long night <note>original: "aithi"</note> begins to darken that place. The sun continues to revolve, sinking lower and lower below the horizon until it reaches the first point of Cancer <note>the Summer Solstice in June</note>, where it cannot descend any further relative to that location. This is the middle of that great night. The sun does not stop rising again, bit by bit, until it reaches the first point of Libra <note>September</note>, when the same long day begins again. The long night we mentioned lasts from the middle of March until the middle of September.

The sun then continues rising by degrees until it enters the first point of Libra, and the same long day begins again. The night I described lasts from the middle of March until the middle of September.

## Concerning the winds: what they are and from where they come.

38. **ANCIENT PHILOSOPHI ASSERT, ETC.** <note>original Latin: *ASSERUNT ANTEQUI PHILOSOPHI ETCETERA*</note> For the ancient philosophers say that **Eurus** <note>the East Wind</note> is hot and dry, **Zephyrus** <note>the West Wind</note> is hot and moist, **Boreas** <note>the North Wind</note> is cold and dry, and **Auster** <note>the South Wind</note> is cold and moist. However, some of the doctors <term>scholars or authorities</term> say that Zephyrus and Auster are not like that; rather, they say that Zephyrus is cold and moist, and Auster is hot and moist. I do not know if they said this regarding the general nature of the winds or the nature of the winds in specific regions, for we see the diversity of the winds changing according to the region. For Eurus and **Septentrio** <note>the North-East wind; original: *Stepterus*</note> are moist in some regions and dry in others; nevertheless, I will describe the certain general nature of all the winds.

The air, when it is heated by the sun, is enlarged and expanded; it swells, and a dark, murky mist rises from the sea up into the air, creating a cloud above. When that mist touches the cold air above, it is suddenly compressed <note>original: *cumgigter*</note> inward, and that causes it to drip and melt, and rain is made from it. However, when that mist from the sea happens upon the hot, dry air itself, the air [and mist] meet and are lifted up together toward the "place of the king" <note>likely referring to the upper atmosphere or the "sphere of fire"; original: *inadh an roigh*</note> or to the realm of cold. There they are constricted, and air remains there. 

It is the nature of hot air and the nature of a cold place to be in opposition to each other; they cannot endure being in the same place. It is for this reason that the place expels the air, and it continues expelling it, running from place to place. This movement of the air is the wind, and the greater the matter from which the movement comes, the greater the wind will be. There is another cause from which the wind comes: that is, when a battle or strife is occurring among the many...

<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT</header>

<margin>Chapter 38.</margin>

### *Concerning the winds: what they are and where they come from.*

38. **ANCIENT PHILOSOPHERS ASSERT, ETC.** <note>original Latin: *ASSERUNT ANTIQUI PHILOSOPHI, ETCETERA*</note> Although the ancient philosophers say that **Eurus** <note>the East Wind</note> is warm and dry, and **Zephyrus** <note>the West Wind</note> is warm and wet, and that **Boreas** <note>the North Wind</note> is cold and dry, and **Auster** <note>the South Wind</note> is cold and wet, some of the <term>doctors</term> <note>meaning learned authorities or scholars</note> declare that neither Zephyrus nor Auster are truly like that. Instead, they claim that Zephyrus is cold and wet, and Auster is warm and wet. I do not know whether they said this regarding the general nature of the winds or regarding how winds behave in specific countries. We certainly see a difference in the winds across various lands; for example, Eurus and Zephyrus are wet in some countries and dry in others. However, I shall describe the generally accepted nature of all the winds.

When the air is heated by the sun, it expands and spreads out. A dark, murky vapor rises from the sea into the air and is transformed into a cloud above. When that mist comes into contact with the cold air higher up, it suddenly contracts, which causes it to liquefy and dissolve, turning it into rain. 

Moreover, when that sea vapor and the air meet in the warm, dry atmosphere, and both are drawn up together to the "frost region" or the domain of cold, they contract and remain in the atmosphere. It is the nature of warm air and the cold region to be opposed to each other; they cannot endure staying in the same place. Consequently, the cold space drives the air out. As the air is continually expelled, it rushes from place to place, setting the atmosphere in motion. That motion of the air is the wind, and the greater the cause of the motion, the stronger the wind becomes. 

Another cause of wind: when a battle or conflict is being fought by large armies and vast troops—

<vocab>Eurus, Zephyrus, Boreas, Auster, philosophers, winds, atmosphere, frost region, rain, air</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the conflicting classical theories regarding the temperature and moisture of the four primary winds and provides a physical explanation for wind as the movement of air driven by the conflict between heat and cold.</summary>
<keywords>meteorology, Eurus, Zephyrus, Boreas, Auster, atmospheric motion, evaporation, condensation, classical elements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the physical explanation of atmospheric motion, transitioning from the behavior of large-scale winds to an analogy of human movement and a scientific experiment demonstrating thermal expansion.</meta>

<page-num>148</page-num>

# AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT

...a host and the many moving weapons, and the panting of people—some fleeing and others pursuing them—the thin air rushes before them and stirs up a wind.

And if you wish to clearly prove what we have said regarding the raising of wind in cold air after it has been heated, take a basin and put two or three inches of water in it. Take an empty glass vessel and leave them in a cold place overnight until morning. In the morning, you will find that vessel full of cold, <term>condensed</term> <note>original: *calcaithi*, meaning packed, hardened, or thickened</note> air. Turn the mouth of the vessel downward into the water in the basin, and place them together in the heat of the sun after sunrise. When that thick air in the glass heats up, it grows, swells, and expands, seeking a larger space. Because it has nowhere to go except through the mouth of the vessel down into the water, it pushes down against the water and raises the water level up to the rim of the basin. You will see it rising like a tide, growing until it eventually overflows the basin. This demonstrates that the air within the vessel grows and swells. Leave it in that state until the next night; as the heat of the day fades and the cold of the night arrives, that cold will gather the same air back into the vessel and condense it there again. Then the water will fall back into its original place.

Therefore, if that small amount of air can create such a great expansion, it is certain that the expansion of all the air—or even a part of the <term>sphere</term> <note>meaning the atmosphere or the celestial sphere surrounding the Earth</note> itself—is immense.

<summary>The author uses a practical experiment with a glass vessel and water to demonstrate the thermal expansion and contraction of air, arguing that if a small amount of air expands when heated, the entire atmosphere must undergo massive expansion.</summary>
<keywords>thermal expansion, atmospheric pressure, scientific experiment, air, water, wind formation, medieval science</keywords>

### Chapter 38 (xxxviii)
...with the movements and panting of the men—some of them fleeing and others in pursuit—the <term>rarefied</term> <note>original: "rarefied," meaning made thin or less dense</note> air rushes before them and stirs up a wind.

If you wish to clearly prove this point regarding how wind arises in the cold air after it has been heated, take a basin and fill it with two or three inches of water. Place an empty glass vessel in it and leave them in a cool place overnight until morning. In the morning, you will find that the vessel is full of cold, <term>condensed</term> <note>meaning thickened or packed together due to the cold</note> air. Turn the vessel upside down with its mouth in the water in the basin, and place them both in a spot exposed to the heat of the sun after it has risen. 

When the condensed air inside the glass heats up, it expands and <term>dilates</term> <note>original: "dilates," meaning to become wider or larger</note>. It spreads out, seeking more space. Since it has no way to escape except through the mouth of the vessel into the water, it pushes down against the water and raises the water level to the rim of the basin. It then looks like a rising tide, gradually increasing until it sometimes overflows the basin. This proves that the air inside the vessel expands and dilates. 

Leave it there until the following night; as the heat of the day fades and the cold of the night arrives, the cold will pull the air back into the vessel and condense it there again. Then, the water will fall back into its original place. Therefore, since even a small amount of air can expand so much, it is certain that the entire atmosphere—or at least a portion of it within its own <term>sphere</term> <note>the region of air surrounding the Earth</note>—expands greatly.

---

¹ How then does the air fill the world and that which fills the horizons? <note>original Latin: *Quomodo ergo aer implens mundum et qui implet orisantas.*</note>

### On clouds, thunder, rain, and lightning

39. THE SUN, SEAS, AND RIVERS OF THE EARTH UNTO THEIR PLACES <note>original Latin: "SOL MARIA ET FLUMINA TERRAE USQUE LOCA," likely a reference to a classical or medieval scientific authority</note> ETC. The sun draws up vapors and mists from the sea, from the streams, and from other wet places. These are often impossible to see because of their thinness, except in the morning or evening. When they are drawn up into the hot air, they are scattered, expanded, and mixed throughout that air, for they share the same nature as the air itself. However, when they are drawn up into the cold air, they are condensed and squeezed together, and a cloud is made from them. 

Just as it is the nature of similar things to seek one another—just as streams flow into the sea—the smaller clouds (being lighter and moving more easily) go toward the larger clouds. They form one great, dark mass <note>original: "motar," a thick clump or mass</note>. It remains there because of its internal heat, while the cold air surrounding it acts in opposition, the two "wrestling" with one another.

When the cold air is stronger and overcomes the cloud, it binds and hardens the outer edges of the cloud, turning it into snow. Furthermore, when heat is trapped inside the cloud while it is surrounded on the outside by cold—with the cloud frozen and hardened around it—the heat seeks, according to its nature, a place where it might grow, swell, and expand. 

Since the firm cloud does not allow that expansion to happen, the heat shakes the cloud powerfully and breaks it. From that rupture comes a great and terrible sound called thunder <note>original: "toirrnec"</note>. Because of the strength of the force by which that breaking is done, lightning bolts and flashes of fire <note>original: "soidhnein" and "teinntec"</note> come out of that rupture. Small fragments of that cloud fall from the breaking, clashing against one another. As they fall, they break each other into even smaller pieces again; and as the part closest to the earth reaches the air, the heat takes...

### *Concerning the clouds, thunder, rain, and lightning.*

39. THE SUN [RUNS OVER] THE SEAS, THE RIVERS, AND THE PLACES, ETC. <note>original Latin: SOL [CURRIT SUPER] MARIA ET FLUMINA ET LOCA, ETCETERA</note>. The sun draws vapors and mists from the sea, from rivers, and from other damp places. Because these vapors are so thin, they are invisible except in the morning and evening. When they are drawn up into the hot air, they scatter and spread out, mixing with the air because they share the same nature. On the other hand, when they are drawn up into the cold air, they become compressed and squeezed together, turning into clouds. Since it is the nature of similar things to seek one another—just as rivers flow into the sea—smaller clouds move toward larger ones because they are lighter and can move more easily. They form one large, dark mass. Since this mass is naturally warm but is surrounded by cold air, the two forces oppose and struggle with each other.

When the cold air is the stronger force and overcomes the cloud, it binds and thickens the outer edges, turning the moisture into snow. Consequently, when heat is trapped inside the cloud while it is surrounded by cold on the outside—causing the cloud to freeze and harden around the heat—that heat naturally tries to find a space where it can expand and spread out. Since the dense cloud does not allow this expansion, the heat shakes the cloud violently until it bursts. A great and terrifying sound, called thunder, results from that breaking. Due to the incredible force behind that rupture, thunderbolts and lightning are produced. Small fragments of that cloud then fall, striking and breaking against each other. As they descend, they break into even smaller pieces, and when they come into contact...

...in that place, it turns them into rounded lumps and makes them spherical; then the hail-stones fall. The small, fine drops of rain found mixed with the snow are the parts that have been separated from it through melting.

The greater the heat we mentioned and the cold are, the greater the <term>opposition</term> <note>original: *contraracht*; referring to the conflict between opposing physical qualities like hot and cold</note> between them becomes. As this opposition increases, so too do the thunder and the lightning that come from it. The portion of that cloud that does not descend to the earth spreads throughout the air and turns into lightning. The part of the lightning that does reach the ground splits hills and mountains, pierces the earth, and kills people and livestock.

To prove that thunder comes from this opposition, the <term>philosophers</term> <note>meaning natural scientists or scholars of antiquity like Aristotle</note> offer this example: when a green leaf is placed on a fire, before it is even burned, the heat causes it to snap with a loud sound. Likewise, when red-hot iron is plunged into water, the opposition between those two things produces a very great noise. Therefore, if the opposition of small objects creates such a sound, it follows that the collision of much larger bodies must produce a massive sound.

Thunder, lightning, and flashes are more frequent in the spring and autumn than at other times, because those two seasons fall between the heat of summer and the cold of winter. As for the clouds that the blowing wind lifts from the earth into the air—which is cold, wet, thin, and subtle, lacking heat or dryness except for what is within the clouds themselves—no opposition occurs within them.

The heavy portion within these clouds separates the frozen drops and turns them into rain. When such a cloud encounters hot air, the air thins it and turns it back into vapor again. Yet, through the discord and opposition of heat and cold, and the dryness and wetness of that air, great dark clouds are formed. These clouds produce heavy rain, and sometimes large raindrops and thick hailstones are created from the same substance. These phenomena occur most frequently in the spring and autumn; and the...

As proof that thunder results from the opposition I mentioned, the <term>philosophers</term> <note>meaning natural scientists or scholars of antiquity like Aristotle</note> have cited an example: when a green leaf is put into a fire, before it burns, it snaps with a loud sound as soon as the heat touches it. In the same way, when red-hot iron is put into water, the opposition between these two things draws a tremendous noise from them. Therefore, since the conflict of small bodies produces this noise, much larger bodies must produce a massive sound.

There is more thunder, lightning, and many thunderbolts in the spring and autumn than in the other seasons, because these two seasons occur between the warm summer and the cold winter. The clouds that the blowing wind draws up from the earth into the cold, wet, thin, and <term>attenuated air</term> <note>original: *attenuated*; air that is thin or less dense</note>—which lacks heat or dryness except for what is contained within the clouds themselves—possess no such opposition.

The heavy part contained in those clouds separates from them in drops and is converted into rain. When the cloud meets warm air, the air <term>rarefies</term> <note>original: *rarefies*; makes less dense or thinner, usually through heat</note> it and converts it back into air. Through the disagreement caused by the opposition of the heat, cold, dryness, and wetness of that air, it is changed into large black clouds. Those black clouds are then changed into heavy rain. Sometimes the same substance is converted into large drops of rain and great hailstones, which occur most frequently in spring and autumn; when they occur in the summer...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric phenomena, specifically how the "opposition" (conflict) between elemental qualities like heat and cold results in storms, thunder, and lightning, and moves into a medieval scientific explanation of sensory perception.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>AN IRISH ASTRONOMICAL TRACT.</header>

When this occurs in the summer, because the conflict <note>original: *contrarachta*; the opposition of physical qualities like hot and cold</note> we mentioned is greater during that season than in others, that is when the greatest storms occur. And when a great wind occurs during such a storm, it twists the clouds <gloss>33</gloss> together up in the sky, binding them and giving them various shapes. The ignorant <note>those without scientific education</note> then believe that they are dragons <gloss>34</gloss>. We see this same twisting motion when the wind blows the dust of the earth.

Even though thunder and lightning are created in the same place, the lightning is seen before the thunder is heard. The reason for this is that sight perceives things that are near and things that are far away simultaneously; for example, a person does not see the ground any sooner than <gloss>35</gloss> they see the stars furthest away from them in the <term>firmament</term> <note>the heavens or the sky</note>. 

But it is not so with hearing; a sound that is nearby is heard sooner than a sound that is far away. To illustrate this, the <term>doctors</term> <note>learned scholars or physicians</note> compared the sense of hearing to a <term>quern</term> <note>original: *broin*; a stone mill for grinding grain</note>. If an ear were inside the hole of the quern, it would hear everything near or far without any delay. The sense of hearing behaves like air, which is a slender, subtle, and easily moved body; its movement is greater, finer, and faster than the movement of water.

When a breaking, a striking, or any other sound occurs in the sky, the air closest to that sound moves the sound toward <gloss>36</gloss> the other parts of the air. In this way, the sound passes through the various parts of the air in succession until it finally <gloss>36</gloss> reaches the ear. From the ear, it travels to the brain, which distinguishes between loud and soft sounds, and between subtle and harsh ones.

In the same way, they compared the sense of the eyes <gloss>37</gloss> to a trumpet <gloss>38</gloss> which has <gloss>38</gloss> a narrow end; as it extends further <gloss>39</gloss> from that end <gloss>39</gloss>, it widens. Likewise, the sight of the eyes travels from the brain through a <term>sinewy tube</term> <note>the optic nerve</note> to the pupil <note>original: *mic imrisan*; literally "the son of the contention," an archaic Irish term for the pupil of the eye</note>.

That is not the case with hearing, for one hears a sound that is nearby sooner than a sound which is distant. In explaining this, the scholars <note>original: "doctors"</note> compared the sense of hearing to a <term>quern</term> <note>a stone hand-mill for grinding grain</note>; for if there were an ear in the opening of the quern, it would hear everything near to it and distant from it without distinction, because the nature of the ear is like air, which is a thin, <term>rarefied</term> <note>low-density or porous</note> movable body, the motion of which is greater, smoother, and swifter than that of water.

When some disruption, striking, or other noise occurs in the air, the air which is nearest that noise pushes the sound away from it toward other parts of the air, until finally it enters the ear and passes from the ear to the brain, which distinguishes between the smallest and the greatest, and between the softest and the loudest noise.

In the same way, they compared the sense of sight to a trumpet which has a narrow end, and the further from the end it is, the broader it becomes; thus, the sight of the eye passes through the <term>sinewy vein</term> <note>the optic nerve, which medieval scientists believed was a hollow channel for "visual spirits"</note> from the brain...

->**On stationary, non-sentient living things**<-
<note>original: *Do na hainminntib fastacha nemcedfadaca*; This refers to plants, which were classified in medieval science as "living" but lacking senses.</note>

40. **All non-sentient living things, etc.** <note>original Latin: *Universa animata insensibilia etcetera*</note>. That is to say: every non-sentient thing that has a "soul" <note>original: *anim*; in this context, the "vegetative soul" or life force that allows for growth but not thought or movement</note> is capable of growing by itself. For we see in the woods and on the hills many kinds of trees on which fruit grows naturally; however, the fruit of trees planted and tended by human hands is more perfect and better nourished.

No tree in the world can grow except in its own proper place and its own natural climate <note>original: *aer*; literally "air," but referring to environmental conditions</note>. The seed of these stationary, non-sentient living things is their "material form." For it was God’s will when He created them that they should possess a reproductive power <note>original: *brig silta*; literally "seeding power" or germinative force</note> within them, from which their own exact and similar species would grow, one after another, forever. Thus, when that seed falls into the earth, it swells from the moisture of the rain falling upon it.

It is the nature of water to enter into every body, except for a solid, impenetrable body. When the sun warms that seed, it draws out its moisture; for it is the sun's nature to draw all moisture upward. Then, out of the grain—once it has been warmed and moistened—grows the natural embryo <note>original: *geineamhain*; birth or offspring</note> that was hidden within its internal powers. This is the "plant-matter," identical to the plant from which it originally came. The earth continually provides moisture to the plant to replace the moisture that the sun takes away. From these two things—moisture and heat—grows the power called "stationary life" <note>original: *ainim fastac*; the vegetative soul</note>. Vessels then grow downward from it, which are called roots <note>original: *precmha*; an archaic term for roots</note>, through which the plant draws nourishment from the earth into itself. And when the sun draws up those same moistures, it draws the hidden power with them, and from this are formed branches, foliage, flowers, and fruit, and...

->**Concerning living, growing things that lack sensation**<-
<note>This section discusses plants, which in medieval science were considered "animate" (having a soul) because they grow and reproduce, but "insensible" because they lack the five senses.</note>

40. **All non-sentient living things, etc.** <note>original Latin: *Universa animata insensibilia, etcetera*</note>. Every living thing that lacks sensation is capable of growing on its own. For we see many types of trees where fruit grows naturally in the woods and on the hills, even though the fruit of trees planted and tended by human hands is more carefully cultivated and of better quality. 

No tree in the world can grow except in its own natural environment and climate <note>original: "place and climate"</note>. It is the seed of these things (which possess "vegetative life" but lack sensation) that provides their physical origin <note>original: "material creation (?)"</note>. This is because God, who created them, willed that they should possess the power of reproduction <note>original: "propagation"</note>, so that their own kind would grow in succession forever. Thus, when a seed falls upon the earth, it swells from the falling rain.

It is the nature of water to penetrate every body, unless that body is solid and impenetrable. When the sun heats that seed, it draws the moisture out of it—for it is the sun's nature to draw up all moisture. Then, after being heated and moistened, the natural growth contained within the seed's hidden potential begins to emerge: namely, the germ of a plant identical to the one from which it originally came. The earth constantly supplies the plant with moisture to replace what the sun draws away. From these two forces—heat and moisture—a power is generated called "vegetative life" <note>original: "vegetable life"; the *anima vegetabilis* was believed to be the life force responsible for growth in plants</note>. From this, "veins" (called roots) grow downward, through which the plant draws nutrients from the soil. As the sun draws up the aforementioned moisture, it also draws up this hidden life force, and from this process, boughs, foliage, and blossoms are created...

# GLOSSARY.

The following glossary contains the rarer words occurring in the text, along with those used in specialized or technical senses. The words are cited exactly as they appear in the manuscript, though the letter **h** has been added to indicate aspirated **c** and **t**. The numbers refer to the page numbers in the original document.

**Acmainn, acmuinn** 124, 138, abundance.
**acmainnech** 50, *heavily* (used of rain); 52, mightily; 138, abounding in.
**adhamas** 46, lodestone <note>original: "lodestone" is a naturally magnetized mineral</note>, ZCP II., 242.
**adbar, adbur** *everywhere* <note>original: "passim"</note>, cause, reason; 12, *and elsewhere* <note>original: "et pass."</note> subject; 36, 40, 152, material, substance; 156, 158, *germ of a plant* <note>original: "a. planda"</note>.
**adburtha** 156, material (used as an adjective); *material form* <note>original: "cruth a."</note>.
**adhuist** 50, 52, *genitive singular* <note>the form of a noun showing possession or description</note>, August; *also* **anust** 52.
**aentadach, aentaghach** 18, 20, agreeing, in agreement with.
**aer** *everywhere* <note>original: "passim"</note>, air, atmosphere; 156, climate.

**cuil** 96, **cuilib**, <term>dative plural</term>, an angle or a quadrate. This translates the term *quadratura* used by Sacrobosco <note>Johannes de Sacrobosco, author of the 13th-century astronomical text *De Sphaera*</note>. A quadrate is an astronomical aspect in which heavenly bodies are 90° apart from each other.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical index of Irish terms, completing the letter "G" and beginning the letter "I." The entries cover physical actions (touching, acting), descriptions of terrain (salty soil, gravel), and astronomical or navigational terms (the waning of the moon, the direction south, and the "bosom" of a sail).</meta>

**glactar** 12, <term>present indicative passive</term>: is taken; **glacmaid**, **glaccaid** 12, 14, <term>1st person plural and 3rd person plural present indicative</term> of *glacaim*: I touch; **glacadh** 14, **glaccadh** 72, <term>verbal noun</term>: touch, feel.

**goirt** 40, <term>adjective</term>: salt or bitter; as in *talam g.*: "salty land."

**glotain** 18, <term>dative singular</term> of *glota*: bosom; O’Reilly's Dictionary suggests (?) embrace. <note>original: "cf. IT. iii. p. 322, Rothimairged in cú ina glotain in macc bec"</note> Compare: "The dog gathered the little boy into his bosom"; *glotu*: belly (according to Stokes); <note>original: "TDG, Part II., § 31, roleigiodar an ghaoth a nglotain an tseolchrainn"</note> "They let the wind into the 'bosom' (hollow) of the mast"; <note>original: "3. C. 13, 866, ni meinic bhios ós baire leathain a lán gaoithe a nglotain siúil"</note> "It is not often that a full wind is in the 'bosom' of a sail over a wide sea-surface"; <note>original: "B. iv. I. fo. 50. l. 11. a ngloitnibh na nglainbhreideann siuil"</note> "In the 'bosoms' of the clean-canvassed sails."

**gnathochann** 52: use, vogue, fashion; (?) compare with *lethnochon* and *suidheochan* on page 120.

**gné** 72, 92, 100, 114, 156: kind, species; **gneithe** 158, <term>plural</term>.

**gnimach** 14: active; **gnimacha** 12, 20, <term>plural</term>; *cail g.*: "an active property."

**gnimaidhidh** 44, <term>3rd person singular present indicative</term>; **gnimaidhid** 38, <term>3rd person plural</term>: works, acts, operates.

**gnimartha** 70: actions, functions (of heat, etc.); **gnimarthad** 62, <term>genitive plural</term>.

**grinn** 32: careful, accurate, in detail; **grinne** 70, <term>comparative</term>.

**grinneall** 40: coarse gravel, sea bottom, or the bottom of a pit.

**grinnigh** 24: examine, consider carefully (?). Evidently the same word as occurs in the following passages: —<note>original: "Eriu II. i. p. 24, fecaidsom beous for a ngrinigud som"</note> "He still looks to their scrutiny."

<column-break/>

<note>original: "IT. IV. p. 185, grindiugud cacha dala 7 cacha caingne . . . . is co grind tucais in carpat dam"</note> "The scrutinizing of every case and every cause... it is accurately that you brought the chariot to me"; notice here the play on the words *grindiugud* ("scrutinizing") and *grind* ("accurate" or "fine"). The Franciscan Manuscript of *Agallamh na Senórach* has *glindiugad* in this passage; it is evidently the same word as the Modern Irish *glinneamhuin*: scrutinizing, with an interchange of the letters *r* and *l* <note>original: "cf. biorar: biolar"</note> (compare *biorar* and *biolar*, meaning "watercress").

**guin** 90: the wane (of the moon); literally "wounding," then "failure" or "decrease"; **firderedh** 96 (extreme end) evidently describes the same phase. <note>original: "Ruithnedh an eusga an uair ghontar. K.M. Miscellany, p. 172"</note> "The radiance of the moon when it is 'wounded' (waning)." <note>original: "le linn guin na rae. GJ. No. 148, pp. 207, 208"</note> "During the wounding of the moon" (the change of the moon). <note>original: "Gach aon . . . . do bhiodh tinn le goin an ræ. Math. iv. 24."</note> "Everyone... who was sick with the 'wounding' of the moon" (meaning lunacy or epilepsy; Matthew 4:24).

---

**iacingetis** 72: jacinth; a mineral, specifically a variety of zircon. The name was also applied to cinnamon, topaz, and sapphire; from the Latin *hyacinthus*, later *iacinctus* or *iacinthus*.

**ichtar** throughout <note>original Latin: *passim*</note>: bottom, lower part; often used for "south" in the text.

**imaidh** 76: image.

**imaraib** 36, <term>dative plural</term> of *imaire*: ridge.

**imarcraid**, **imarcridh**, **imarcaig**, **imarcaid**, throughout <note>original Latin: *passim*</note>: excess.

**imbualadh** 36: beating to and fro (?); usually "mutual beating" or "mighty beating."

**imchubaid** 26, suitably or appropriately; 70, favorable or auspicious (often used in reference to the planets).

**impoidid** (i) 42, <term>present indicative</term>: turns or changes into; *i. i naduir na talman*: "adopts the nature of the earth."
**impodh** <term>frequently occurring</term>, <term>verbal noun</term>: turning or evolution; in the phrase *re na impodh* (page 24), meaning <note>original: "vice-versa"</note> conversely or "with its turning."

**inchinn** 46, 154, the brain.

**Labain** 40, muddy; the <term>genitive singular</term> of *lábán* (mud); *láib* also means mud, and *lábach* means muddy. <note>original: "D. Labb, lap. .i. loth no cechair, O'Dav."</note> This entry references O'Davoren's Glossary, which defines the term as a slough or quagmire.
**la ca** 46, 104, the "cheek" (or face) of the moon; *laca rinne*: "facing us." Alternatively, some read this as *laimh rinne* (beside us), according to the journal *Zeitschrift für celtische Philologie*.
**ladh** 36, mud or mire; *a l. na mara*: "in the mud of the sea."

<column-break/>

<note>original: "cf. Songs of Summer and Winter (Meyer), p 25, lath, W. llaid, hence lathach ; also ZCP III. p. 46, ni meirb docengat latha, sloughs. (?)"</note> Compare the poem "Songs of Summer and Winter" edited by Kuno Meyer, page 25, where *lath* is related to the Welsh word *llaid* (mud), leading to *lathach*. Also see the *Zeitschrift für celtische Philologie*, volume 3, page 46: "not weakly do they traverse the sloughs."
**laidigtar** 46, **laidhditer** 74, <term>present indicative passive</term>; **laigdithi** 74, <term>past subjunctive passive</term> of *laighdighim*: I lessen or decrease.
**lan re** 88, the full moon.
**lanmor** <term>frequently occurring</term>, very great; *in speir l.*: <note>original: "sphaera maxima"</note> "the greatest sphere."
**lasad, lassad** 44, burning.
**lasair** 44, 96, 98, light, flame, or a beam of light.
**leagad** 146, 152, melting or dissolving.
**leanmhain** 98, *a l.*: continually or always.
**leithimillech** 84, outer or outmost; *cercall l.*: "the outermost circle."
**leithne** <term>frequently occurring</term>, wideness or extent; 114, amplitude; *cainndigeacht leitne a speiri fein*: <note>original: "quantitatem amplitudinis orbium suorum"</note> "the quantity of the amplitude of its own spheres."
**lethni** 54, the dilation or expansion (of air).
**lethan** 88, flat or level; the opposite of *cruinn* (round).
**lethead** 102, 136, latitude.
**lethimill** 126, the outer edge or rim.
**lethnaidh** 54, 148, spreads or dilates.
**lethnaigi** 152, **lethnaigid** 26, <term>3rd person singular present indicative</term>; **lethnaigter** 146, **lethnaidter** 150, <term>present indicative passive</term>; **lethnochad** 150, <term>2nd future passive</term>; **lethnugad** 28, <term>verbal noun</term>: widens out, spreads, dilates, amplifies.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical index of Irish terms, focusing on the letter "L" and the beginning of "M." The entries cover linguistic nuances of verbs, anatomical and medical terms like the "humors," and geographical concepts such as the Equator.</meta>

**linas** 48, <term>present indicative relative</term>; **lintar** 50, <term>present indicative passive</term>; **linfaide** 50, <term>second future passive</term>; **linad** 52, <term>past passive</term> of *linaim*: to fill or flood (used in reference to rivers); **linaid** 42, <term>third person plural present indicative</term> (used in reference to wells).

**line medonach an domain** 64, the Equator.

**lionn ruad** 42, bile; also known as <term>choler</term> <note>original: "choler O'R."</note>. In a fragment of a medical tract, *lionn ruad* (literally "red humor") is discussed as one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids; the others are *fuil derg* (red blood), *lionn fionn* (white humor or phlegm), and *lionn dub* (black humor or melancholy).

**locfadais** 62, <term>second future, third person plural</term> of *locaim*: I hinder or refuse.

**loisgnech** 10, burning.

**lorgaireacht** 132, <term>verbal noun</term>: following or tracing out; *da l. an adbuir so*: to pursue this subject.

**luas** <term>frequently occurring</term>, speed or swiftness; *l. cumscaithi* 16, speed of motion <note>original: "velocitas"</note>.

**luathaiges** 20, <term>present indicative relative</term>: hastens or accelerates.

**ludrach** 126, 142, 144, a pivot, foundation, or seat; a stay or a hold; a mooring. *Also spelled* **lundrach**. *Sludrach* means a foundation <note>original: "Sludrach .i. fundamentum O'C."</note>.

**Ma** 52, equivalent to *um a* (concerning or about).

**macasamla** 68, <term>plural</term> of *macsamail*: a copy; *daghenadh a m. isin firmamint*: "would produce similar effects in the <term>firmament</term> (the sky)."

**maille** <term>frequently occurring</term>, slowness; *m. cumscaithi* 16, slowness of motion <note>original: "tarditas"</note>.

**mallaiges** 20, <term>present indicative relative</term> of *malluigim*: I retard or slow down.

<column-break/>

**meallad** 6, *gan m.*: without fail; literally "deception" or "delusion."

**medaigther** 46, 74, 146, 152, <term>present indicative passive</term>; **medaithi** 74, <term>past subjunctive passive</term>; **medochaidh** 74, <term>second future passive</term> of *meduigim*: I increase or augment.

**mesarda** <term>frequently occurring</term>, temperate, moderate, or mild.

**mesardacht** 140, mildness; **mesardachta** 62, <term>genitive singular</term>: regularity.

**mianach** 40, a mine.

**mic imrisan** 154, <term>plural</term> of *mac imrisan*: the pupil of the eye.

**millsi** 42, <term>superlative</term> and <term>plural</term> of *milis*: fresh (used for water, in contrast to salt water).

**minaiter** 34, <term>present indicative passive</term> of *minighim*: I polish or make smooth.

**minbainneda** 152, <term>plural</term> of *minbainne*: a small drop.

**minbrisid** 150, <term>third person plural present indicative</term>: break into small pieces.

**miniasgaib** 36, <term>dative plural</term> of *miniasg*: small fish.

**minrainn** 150, <term>plural</term> of *minrann*: a fragment.

**mir** 136, a piece, portion, or fraction; *treis mir uaire*: one-third of an hour.

**mitaill** 138, <term>plural</term> of *mitall*: metal.

**mnaamail** 70, feminine.

**mocrach** <term>frequently occurring</term>, equivalent to *moctrath*: early, or in the morning. Note the spelling variation in other texts <note>original: "LL. 104a, 26"</note>; *mocrach (no tratnona)* 150 translates the Latin phrase for "at daybreak" <note>original: "in diliculis," S., diluculum, daybreak</note>.

**monad** 52, money or mintage, derived from the Latin *monetum*; subsequently used to mean a stamp, kind, or type. <note>original: "Teora fidhbhuidi . . . . . 7 is dia"</note> "Three forests . . . and it is of..."

**monad** [page 384] "from those skins money is made for the king for buying and trading in the territory" <note>original Irish: "croicnib sin donither monad don righ fria creic 7 cundrad na crichi"</note>; [page 384] "coral is their currency for buying and trading" <note>original: "curel is monad doibh fria creic 7 cunnrad"</note>; [page 372] "with wild animals, the head of every kind in the world" <note>original: "cu fiadhmhilaib ecennus da gach mhonad for bith"</note> (here meaning "kind" or "sort"); also in *Zeitschrift für celtische Philologie* <note>original: "ZCP," a journal of Celtic philology</note> volume II, page 272, note, and page 290, "a third part of the same kind" <note>original: "tristeil don monadh chetna"</note>; *Ériu* <note>a journal of Irish philology and literature</note> volume IV, part I, page 57, "a hundred of every cattle was given to him, and a hundred ounces of every kind of currency, and the king's own equipment" <note>original: "tugadh ced da gach crodh do 7 ced uinghe da gach monadh 7 errad an righ feisin"</note>.
**mothar** 150, cluster, collection, mass.
**muchfadh** 84, <term>second future</term> of *múchaim* (I extinguish); here it means "to obscure."
**mullach** occurring throughout, top, highest point; often used to denote the north.

**Naduir** occurring throughout, nature; from the Latin *natura*. It is written as "n" with a superscript "r" throughout the text in all grammatical cases.
**nadurtha** occurring throughout, natural; also written as "n" with a superscript "r."
**a neach as ludha** 96, (used instead of *do neach*), at least.

**neamhfoirfi** 66, imperfect.
**neamneachtarda** 70, neutral, not belonging to either side.

**neascoidi** 46, plural of *neascoid*: ulcer, boil; also appears as *neasc* in Zeuss <note>original: "Z.", referring to Johann Kaspar Zeuss, a founder of Celtic philology</note>.
**neimeasbadhach** 6, *co n.* (used adverbially): without fail.

**neimcedfadach** 156, destitute of sensation; translates the Latin *insensibilis*.
**nematmar** 28, not sloping, not convex.
**nemhuaigertha** 8, *co n.* (used adverbially): orderly, without confusion.
**nemcunntabartach** 30, 132, *co n.* (used adverbially): without doubting, unquestionably; **nemcunntabartacha** 36, plural adjective.
**nemmeascaithi** 8, without confusion.
**nemmellta** 68, 92, *co n.* (used adverbially): without fail.

**Oibriugad** occurring throughout, action, function; **oibrigthi**, plural.

**oisceol** 52, <term>first person singular future</term> of *osclaim*: I open or disclose.
**ordochan** 120, order, arrangement.

**Plained** occurring throughout, a planet.

**pol** occurring throughout, pole; **poil**, <term>nominative plural</term>; **pollaib** 120, <term>dative plural</term>.

**Uachtar** throughout <note>original Latin: passim</note>, upper part; often used to mean "north" in this text; also means "surface," as in the surface of the earth <note>original Irish: u. na talman</note>, 138.
**uisceamlacht** 156, wateriness, moisture.
**urdail** 116, a like amount, equivalent.

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page outlines the mission, subscription details, and contact information for the Irish Texts Society, following the listing of its officers and consultative committee.</meta>

**The Irish Texts Society** was established in 1898 for the purpose of publishing texts in the Irish language, accompanied by such introductions, English translations, glossaries, and notes as might be considered necessary.

The Annual Subscription has been fixed at 7 shillings and 6 pence <note>original: 7s. 6d.; this was the British pre-decimal currency before 1971</note> (two dollars for American subscribers), payable on January 1st of each year. Upon payment, Members will be entitled to receive the Annual Volume of the Society and any additional volumes which they may issue from time to time.

Volumes 1, 2, and 3* are now out of print, but Volumes 4 to 13 can still be obtained by new members joining the Society at the original Subscription rate of 7 shillings and 6 pence for each year.

---

The Committee makes a strong appeal to all those interested in the preservation and publication of Irish Manuscripts to join the Society and to contribute to its funds, and especially to the Editorial Fund, which has been established for the payment <note>original: remuneration</note> of Editors for their demanding <note>original: arduous</note> work.

---

All communications should be addressed to the Honorary Secretary, Miss **Eleanor Hull**, 20 Hanover Square, London, W.

---

*A new Edition of Volume 3 was published in 1911, as the annual volume for 1909.

# IRISH TEXTS SOCIETY.

---

The Fifteenth Annual General Meeting of this Society was held on April 24, 1913, at 20 Hanover Square, London, W., with Dr. E. C. Quiggin—a Lecturer in Celtic at Cambridge University—presiding. After the minutes of the last meeting were accepted as read, the Honorary Secretary presented the

## FIFTEENTH ANNUAL REPORT.

Two volumes have been published since the Society's last General Meeting: Mr. J. G. O’Keeffe’s edition of the Middle Irish romance titled *Buile Suibhne Geilt* <note>original: *Buile Suibhne Geilt*; translated as "The Frenzy of Sweeney," it is a famous tale of a king cursed to live as a bird-man</note>, and the second volume of Reverend John MacErlean’s edition of the *Poems of David O’Bruadair* <note>Dáibhí Ó Bruadair (c. 1625–1698) was a major Irish-language poet who chronicled the downfall of the old Gaelic order</note>. These constitute the Society’s publications for the years 1910 and 1911.

In addition to the book proposals mentioned in the last Report—all of which are progressing in the hands of their various editors—the Council is very pleased to announce that its President, Dr. Douglas Hyde <note>Douglas Hyde (1860–1949) was a leading figure in the Gaelic Revival and later became the first President of Ireland</note>, has offered to edit portions of the large collection of Irish folk tales that have come into his possession. These stories were collected through prize competitions held at the various *feiseanna* <note>original: *feiseanna*; traditional Irish festivals focused on music, dance, and storytelling</note> of the Gaelic League <note>An organization founded in 1893 to promote the Irish language and culture</note> throughout the country.

This unique and valuable collection of folk tales—which is likely large enough to fill three of the Society’s volumes—has been saved from being scattered or destroyed thanks to Dr. Hyde’s vigilance. He reports that he is making good progress on the first volume with the help of one of his students, who is assisting him in arranging and preparing the stories. At the Council’s suggestion, the stories are being grouped according to the districts where they were collected. Although this work differs somewhat from the Society's usual publication focus, the Council is confident that a volume of folk tales will be welcomed by Irish speakers and folklore experts throughout the world.

<summary>This page records the 1913 Annual General Meeting, noting the publication of Sweeney’s Frenzy and the poems of David O’Bruadair, and announcing a major project to publish Irish folk tales collected by Douglas Hyde.</summary>
<keywords>Irish Texts Society, 1913 Report, J. G. O'Keeffe, Buile Suibhne Geilt, David O’Bruadair, Douglas Hyde, folk tales, Gaelic League, feiseanna, Middle Irish literature</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the 1913 Annual Report of the Irish Texts Society, detailing ongoing projects including an Irish astronomical text and editions of works by Tadhg Dall O’Higgin and Geoffrey Keating.</meta>

Another very interesting proposal is an edition of an astronomical treatise prepared by Miss M. Power as a research project for her Master of Arts degree from the National University, for which she was awarded First Class Honors.

The main text is taken from a manuscript <term>MS.</term> <note>original: MS.; an abbreviation for "manuscript"</note> in the Royal Irish Academy (referenced as B. II. i.). It is a translation of Latin originals, the most important of which is preserved in the Bodleian Library at Oxford. These Latin originals were themselves based on an Arabic treatise by Messahalah (or Mascha Allah), a Jewish astronomer from Alexandria who lived shortly before 800 <term>AD</term>. His work was first translated into Latin in the 13th century.

The exact date of the Irish translation cannot be determined, but its language—which is nearly identical to that used by fluent Irish speakers today—suggests it was likely written in the 14th or early 15th century. The style is simple and straightforward, becoming almost conversational in parts. The text provides a fascinating example of the scientific education offered in Irish schools during that period.

Miss E. Knott, having discovered that several copies of the poems of Tadhg Dall O’Higgin <note>Tadhg Dall Ó hUiginn (1550–1591) was one of the most famous professional poets of his time; his work provides deep insight into Gaelic politics and society</note> not found in Dublin were held in the Advocates’ Library in Edinburgh, spent time there this summer transcribing and comparing these poems.

Mr. Tadhg O’Donoghue reports that the prose text for his volume is ready and that 30 out of 45 poems have been prepared for printing. The proposal to publish this book, which focuses on poems related to the O’Neills of Clandeboy <note>A significant branch of the O'Neill dynasty based in northeast Ulster, in what are now counties Antrim and Down</note>, has attracted the attention of His Excellency "The O’Neill of Lisbon," who has sent a special donation to help cover the costs of publication.

The Reverend P. S. Dinneen is working hard on his volume of Keating’s *Indexes* <note>original: Indices; lists of names, places, or topics for reference</note> and *Genealogies*. He reports that significant progress has been made on this, the fourth and final volume of the Society’s edition of Keating’s *History of Ireland* <note>Written by Geoffrey Keating in the 1630s, this work was a massive undertaking to document the history and legends of Ireland from the earliest times</note>. However, the nature of this work is necessarily slow and painstaking.

The Council is making active efforts to bring its publications up to date.

<summary>This page details the Society's progress on various scholarly editions, including a medieval Irish astronomical treatise based on Arabic sources and the final volume of Geoffrey Keating’s monumental "History of Ireland."</summary>
<keywords>Miss M. Power, astronomy, Messahalah, Tadhg Dall O’Higgin, O’Neills of Clandeboy, Reverend P. S. Dinneen, Geoffrey Keating, Irish manuscripts, 1913 Annual Report</keywords>
